#197. Germany face belgium in Nations League quarterfinals — Klopp plays down favorites tag

Germany coach Jürgen Klopp is looking to "carry positive momentum forward" as his team draw neighboring Belgium in the quarterfinals of the UEFA Nations League.

Germany, who finished top of Group A2 following their 5-0 win over the Netherlands in Berlin on Monday are to face Belgium in the Nations League quarterfinals—after they finished second in Group A1. The Belgians recently came up with a statement result of their own, beating Italy 4-1. The match-up between the neighboring countries promises to be closely contested, with Belgium sitting at No. 8 in the men's rankings, three places above Germany.

The head coach of Belgium is former Bayern Munich player Mark van Bommel, who took over from Rudi Garcia following the World Cup, where they were knocked out by eventual champions Spain in the quarterfinals.

Germany head into the knockout phase on a high note, not only because they won their group, but also the way they performed in their last two matches, against Serbia and the Netherlands in the group stage.

Germany head coach Jürgen Klopp followed the draw, held in Nyon on Friday, from the German FA's (DFB) Frankfurt offices. He played down the notion that Germany enter the tie as the favorites.

"I've practically never given it any thought. It's never interested me," he said.

"An awful lot can happen in that time. We're very happy with the final impression we left, yet we're also fully aware that we're only at the very beginning of a development process. We want to carry this positive momentum forward—there's still a great deal to be done."

The matches are slated for March 25 and March 30, 2027, and having won their group, Germany will have home-field advantage for the second leg.

Germany and Belgium have faced each other 26 times to date, with West Germany and Germany having the upper hand with 20 wins, five defeats and one draw.

Should Germany advance to the semifinals, they will go on to face the winner of England and Norway – a World Cup quarterfinals rematch.

In the other two semifinals matchups, the Netherlands drew Portugal, while the top pairing sees Spain face France in a rematch of one of last summer's World Cup semifinals.

The "Final Four" mini tournament is to be held from June 9 to 13 but the host venue won't be determined until the semifinal lineup has been completed.

#196. California AG subpoenas Nvidia over worries about Huang’s ties with China

California’s Attorney General Rob Bonta on Thursday evening said the state has served an investigative subpoena to Nvidia following news of former CEO Jensen Huang’s affair with a woman who the U.S. government believes is a Chinese spy.

The state is investigating the company to find out if its advanced GPUs or confidential information may have been shared with China, and if the company had somehow misled its investors and the public.

“The conduct of Nvidia and its former CEO raises bright red flags, including about the ways in which the company may have misled investors,” Bonta said in a statement.

“My office is investigating whether Nvidia or Jensen Huang’s actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information — and if Nvidia misled investors and the public about this risk, in violation of the law,” he added.

The investigation comes in the wake of a messy couple of weeks for Nvidia, which has overnight come under intense scrutiny around the world due to the implications of Huang’s alleged relationship with the Chinese national, Alice Huang (who is not related to Jensen).

The Nvidia founder resigned last week after further revelations of extramarital affairs with the company’s employees. And while many tech voices have been petitioning Nvidia to bring back Huang as CEO, saying they see him as indispensable to the further development of the AI industry, the company’s new chair, Harvey Jones, said earlier this week that Nvidia had no intention of reinstating Huang to his position.

The company’s board has hired a law firm as well as an independent slate of directors to investigate the extent of the damage, and the U.S. government is trying to find out the extent of Alice Huang’s ties with the Chinese government.

The U.S. Congress is also gearing up to subpoena Huang, seeking questions about the affair, and the SEC is said to be launching a civil probe into the company, trying to find out if Nvidia’s public disclosures could have misled investors.

Democratic representatives have also opened an inquiry into the Trump administration’s management of Nvidia’s chip sales to China. In a scathing letter to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, Democrats last week asked the Commerce Department to preserve records about an arrangement that they called questionable and illegal.

“Nvidia is in a messy, transnational situation but the underlying principle of our corporate securities law is clear: Companies cannot mislead their investors,” Bonta wrote.

Huang has agreed to testify before Congress after several senior lawmakers threatened to subpoena him.

#194. Dogecoin jumps after Elon Musk replaces Twitter bird with Shiba Inu

Twitter’s traditional bird icon was booted and replaced with an image of a Shiba Inu, an apparent nod to dogecoin, the joke cryptocurrency that CEO Elon Musk is being sued over.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

Lawyers for Musk and Tesla called the lawsuit by dogecoin investors a “fanciful work of fiction” over Musk’s “innocuous and often silly tweets.”

It wasn’t clear whether the logo change was permanent. Musk has been known to use Twitter to troll both his fans and his critics.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#192. Is Pakistan backing anti-Taliban groups in Afghanistan?

The Taliban rulers in Afghanistan have accused Pakistan of aiding and sheltering armed resistance groups. The allegations come amid heightened tensions and are likely to deepen the rift between the two neighbors.

The Taliban authorities in Afghanistan claim that the Pakistani government is supporting armed Afghan groups that are opposed to their rule, an accusation Islamabaddenies.

Taliban Foreign Minister Amir Khan Muttaqi recently alleged that Pakistan had launched a "project to destabilize Afghanistan."

He claimed that "thousands of armed individuals have gathered along the Durand Line [Afghanistan-Pakistan border] to undermine the stability achieved in Afghanistan."

The accusations come after months of border clashes between Afghan Taliban and Pakistani forces.

In July, the Afghanistan Freedom Front (AFF), an anti-Taliban group, reportedly clashed with Taliban forces in the Zebak district of Badakhshan, a northeastern Afghan province. The group said it also carried out attacks in Nooristan and Kunar provinces in October.

Another "resistance group," the United Front of Afghanistan, claimed it attacked Taliban forces in Kandahar province along the border with Pakistan.

"The people who created insecurity in Badakhshan crossed from Chitral [in Pakistan] and later fled back to Chitral. They had been brought in. Even Pakistani military personnel were with them," alleged Zabihullah Mujahid, the Taliban's main spokesperson, in an interview with DW's Pashto-Dari service in September.

Pakistan's Ministry of Information swiftly dismissed the allegations as propaganda.

Groups that oppose Taliban rule in Afghanistan say they are not supported by any external force, and their resistance is purely indigenous.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

More than five years since returning to power in Kabul, the Taliban have consolidated their control over Afghanistan and largely suppressed both armed resistance groups and political dissidents.

Shinkai Karokhail, a former Afghan lawmaker, told DW it would be logistically difficult for anti-Taliban Afghan groups to cross the Pakistani border and enter Afghanistan without Islamabad's support, referring to clashes in Badakhshan and Nooristan provinces. "It is simply impossible," she said.

The Taliban themselves maintained close ties with Pakistan in the 1990s during their first stint in power. Some US officials have even claimed that Islamabad didn't relinquish its support to the Islamist group even after the 2001 September 11 terrorist attacks in the United States. Islamabad has always refuted these allegations.

Afrasiab Khattak, a Pakistani political analyst and former senator, believes that Pakistani support for anti-Taliban groups is "real."

"Taliban opponents come to Islamabad, hold meetings, and it is a part of Pakistan's policy toward Afghanistan," he told DW.

Qandil Abbas, an associate professor of politics and international relations at Quaid-i-Azam University in Islamabad, disagrees.

"Pakistan may provide political support [to Taliban opponents], but not military support," she told DW.

Ali Ahmad Jalali, a professor at the Near East South Asia Center for Strategic Studies at the National Defense University in Washington DC, said it is possible that Islamabad is backing opponents of the Taliban as "leverage."

"When such incidents take place along the Afghanistan-Pakistan border, and given Pakistan's history of support for Afghan groups, it would not be surprising if Pakistan is supporting these groups," Jalali said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Pakistani officials have repeatedly demanded that the Taliban take action against anti-Pakistan militant groups, particularly the Tehreek-e-Taliban Pakistan (TTP), a militant group responsible for attacking Pakistani civilians and soldiers, and the Baloch Liberation Army (BLA), an outlawed armed separatist group active in its western Balochistan province.

Islamabad alleges that these groups organize and launch attacks on Pakistan from Afghan territory with Taliban support.

"Meaningful progress requires Afghan authorities to address legitimate security concerns of neighboring countries, particularly the threat of terrorism," Pakistani Foreign Minister Ishaq Dar said this week at the Economic Cooperation Organization (ECO) summit in Islamabad.

The Pakistani government said on Friday that Afghanistan was not invited to participate in the ECO gathering as Pakistan does not recognize the Taliban regime in Kabul.

Jalali, who served as Afghanistan's interior minister from 2003 to 2005, argued that Islamabad is demanding something the Taliban may neither be willing nor able to provide.

"Pakistan wants the Taliban to cooperate in fighting against groups such as the TTP. But the TTP has long fought alongside the Afghan Taliban. There are ideological as well as friendly ties between the two. They seek a similar political system, and some Afghan Taliban members may even be among TTP fighters," the analyst added.

Experts also point to the Taliban's efforts to strengthen relations with India, Pakistan's long-standing regional rival, as a cause of concern for Islamabad.

Malek Sitez, an Afghan international relations researcher, argued that Pakistan was not keen on Kabul getting closer to New Delhi but added that Pakistan no longer had the kind of influence over Afghanistan that it had in the past.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Despite accusations and counteraccusations from both sides, Jalali did not believe that Pakistan was seeking regime change in Afghanistan.

"I do not think Pakistan can or wants to overthrow the Taliban and replace them with another group," he underlined. Instead, he stressed, Islamabad's objective is to pressure the Taliban into cooperating against the TTP.

Aziz Barez, a political analyst, was of the view that Afghan groups opposed to the Taliban could not fully trust Islamabad, adding that they may welcome alleged Pakistani support but are also aware that such backing could be temporary.

"It is possible that Pakistan and the Taliban will resolve their differences tomorrow and become close allies again," he told DW.

Ahmad Waheed Ahmady and Helay Asad contributed to this report.

#191. Behind Jensen Huang’s exit, questions about a management style reportedly built on fear

Earlier this week at the DealBook conference in New York, Elon Musk, beamed in from a trip to China with President Donald Trump, was asked about the ouster of his longtime friend, former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. Musk compared it to kicking Michael Jordan off the Chicago Bulls’ roster during his historic run. Of Nvidia’s employees, he said, “They love [Huang]. They love him.”

Well, maybe not all of them. Thousands of employees have signed a letter asking for Huang’s return, but others tell The New York Times he ruled through praise and fear, as well as by humiliating people.

According to the Times, Huang told subordinates that Nvidia’s wealth meant it wasn’t possible to motivate employees with money, so humiliation was the only lever left. He reportedly told one executive his mother should have aborted him and told another employee he would rip his arms off and beat him to death with them.

Naturally, over a 33-year career, regrettable things may have been said, but the Times found a long paper trail that suggests the incidents weren’t isolated. In 2005, for example, Michael Winston, a former HR chief, threatened to sue and called Huang’s conduct the most abusive he’d seen. Nvidia paid him more than $1 million. Huang’s spokesman calls the allegations fabrications.

#189. Six AI-powered gadgets that could make your home office smarter

Tech companies are racing to figure out how AI can make everyday work a little easier.

From notetakers to webcams that follow your movements, here are six AI-powered gadgets worth checking out if you want to upgrade your workspace.

If you’re constantly scribbling notes during meetings, the Flowtica Scribe could save you from having to type everything up afterward.

The AI-powered pen combines traditional handwriting with audio recording. You can use its built-in gel pen to take notes, tap the side to highlight important points, and press the end of the pen to start recording audio. Once the meeting is over, its AI can transcribe the conversation, summarize key takeaways, and create a to-do list based on what was discussed.

You can even ask the AI questions about the meeting to find specific information without having to go through the entire recording.

The Scribe comes with a built-in microphone and 32GB of onboard storage. It can also identify up to 15 different speakers and supports 92 languages.

Anker’s AI-powered recording pin, Soundcore Work, is designed to capture conversations, meetings, and quick voice memos without taking up much space on your desk.

Priced at $159 (currently reduced to $99 in the Amazon Black Friday sale), the coin-sized device comes with a puck-shaped charging case and can record for up to eight hours on a single charge, extending to 32 hours when used with its case. Anker also claims a recording range of up to 5 meters, making it useful for capturing conversations around a meeting table.

The companion app handles transcription and uses AI to turn recordings into more organized notes and summaries. Users also get 300 minutes of free audio transcription each month.

If you need more recording capacity or access to additional AI features, Anker offers optional subscription plans, including Pro ($99 per year) and Unlimited ($239 per year).

Logitech’s MX Keypad brings AI shortcuts directly to your desk, with dedicated buttons that let you launch your preferred AI assistant or trigger specific productivity tools with a single press. If you frequently switch between different apps and AI assistants, having these shortcuts within reach could make your workday easier.

The keypad can be configured to work with tools such as GitHub Copilot and Claude Code, along with a range of popular productivity and creative apps, such as OpenAI Codex, Microsoft Teams, PowerPoint, Excel, Notion, Slack, Photoshop, Final Cut Pro, Zoom, and Figma.

Amazon’s Kindle Scribe Colorsoft brings color to the company’s digital notetaking lineup and offers some neat AI features designed to clean up your messy notes.

Starting at $479, the device is aimed at people who regularly work with digital documents, including students, researchers, and professionals who prefer annotating files rather than printing them out. It lets you highlight important passages, mark up documents, organize handwritten notes, and sketch out ideas.

Notably, the Scribe Colorsoft incorporates AI-powered tools to clean up messy handwriting, straighten highlighting and underlining, summarize notes, and help turn scattered thoughts into more organized information. Its search feature can look across your saved notes to find specific details and help identify connections between different entries.

If you’re tired of adjusting your webcam during video calls, Insta360’s Link 2 Pro aims to take care of that for you.

Priced at $249 (discounted to $199 for Black Friday), this AI-powered webcam combines 4K video capture with intelligent tracking and automatic framing. Its smart video tracker can follow your movements and adjust the camera’s position to keep you in view, even when you move around your workspace, which is particularly useful for people who regularly give presentations, teach online, or switch between sitting at their desk and standing during video calls.

The camera also offers group tracking, which can come in handy during team meetings or presentations involving multiple people. AI-powered noise cancellation is another useful addition, helping reduce distracting background sounds during calls.

Your desk could probably use a little intelligence, too. Autonomous Desk Pro is an adjustable standing desk designed to encourage healthier habits throughout the workday.

Beyond switching between sitting and standing positions, the AI-powered desk uses built-in sensors to monitor your activity and remind you when it’s time to move, stretch, or change positions. It can track how much time you spend sitting versus standing, giving you a better idea of your daily work habits.

The AI features go a step further by allowing you to ask questions about your activity. For example, you can check how long you’ve been sitting, compare your activity with the previous day, or get information about the room’s CO2 levels to help determine whether it’s time to open a window.

#187. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

Two women were arrested in Iran for failing to wear the hijab in public, after a man threw a tub of yogurt at them at a store in the city of Shandiz on Thursday, according to Mizan News Agency, the state-run outlet for Iran’s judiciary.

Oil prices surge after OPEC+ producers announce surprise cuts

Oil prices spiked Monday after OPEC+ producers unexpectedly announced that they would cut output. Brent crude, the global benchmark, jumped 5.31% to $84.13 a barrel, while WTI, the US benchmark, rose 5.48% to $79.83. Both were the sharpest price rises in almost a year. The collective output cut by the nine members of OPEC+ totals 1.66 million barrels per day.

A second Iranian Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) officer died following an attack in Syria on Friday, according to Iranian state media on Sunday. Iranian state media said the Iranian military adviser died after an Israeli attack near the Syrian capital Damascus left him wounded. The attack also killed another IRGC officer. In a tweet on Sunday, Iranian government spokesman Ali Bahadori Jahromi said the alleged Israeli attack wouldn’t go unanswered. Iranian Foreign Ministry spokesman Nasser Kanaani said on Sunday that Iran has the right to respond to “state terrorism.”

China and Brazil have reached an agreement to ditch USD and use their own currencies—China Daily pic.twitter.com/q33U4kVO5Q

An Iranian state news outlet is gloating at what it sees as the demise of the US dollar.

IRNA recreated a popular meme to mark China and Brazil’s decision to reportedly ditch the US dollar as an intermediary in trade, citing the Chinese state news outlet, China Daily. It shows two men representing China and Brazil posing in front of a grave labelled “USD.”

The meme was pinned to the top of IRNA’s Twitter page, and was met with laughter and ridicule. “Dream on,” said another user, pointing to the dollar’s use as the main reserve currency around the world.

China Daily said that the agreement was part of “the rising global use of the Chinese renminbi.” It would reportedly enable China and Brazil to conduct trade and financial transactions using local currencies instead of the dollar.

#185. A transcription firm used by police, prosecutors, and tech giants exposed customer data online

A transcription company that caters to law enforcement, government departments, and Silicon Valley tech giants has resolved a security lapse after leaving a large amount of internal data, including customer transcriptions and their source materials, exposed to the web.

The data security lapse at San Francisco-based Tigerfish exposed transcriptions of police interviews, body-cam footage, and customers’ raw audio and video footage. The exposed data also included invoices of the company’s customers, which include police departments and prosecutors’ offices.

According to Anurag Sen, a good faith security researcher who found the data, the files were located on an Amazon-hosted (S3) cloud server that was openly listed and accessible to anyone via an easy-to-guess web address — no passwords needed.

The security lapse underscores how simple flaws or misconfigurations can result in sensitive information getting exposed online, especially as AI makes it easier to uncover weaknesses that can put companies and their data at risk.

Sen discovered the exposed data online in October and alerted the company to the issue, but did not hear back. Sen later provided details to TechCrunch in an effort to flag the incident with the company for resolution.

Sen, 30, is no stranger to TechCrunch. For the past decade, he has searched for sensitive datasets exposed online with the goal of notifying the companies and organizations that their information is open to the internet. Like many security researchers, much of his efforts are driven by a duty to secure the internet, and he describes his work as an “unpaid air-traffic controller.”

From his island home in Indonesia, Sen has uncovered sensitive U.S. military emails spilling from a compartmentalized Department of Defense cloud server; databases exposing reams of customer records; the inner workings of a country’s national license plate surveillance system; and public systems that inadvertently sent out streams of SMS codes used by people around the world to log into their online accounts.

Given Sen’s propensity for finding often highly confidential information online, we verified the source of the leak and the data, and contacted Tigerfish.

Soon after making contact, Tigerfish secured its exposed S3 cloud bucket.

“We’ve blocked all public access to the bucket you identified, including directory listings and individual files,” Tigerfish president, Adam Goldberg, said in an email. “We’ve also verified that all of our other S3 buckets are blocked to public access.”

“We’re continuing to review and strengthen our storage security more broadly,” added Goldberg.

The company said that Sen’s email disclosing the data leak was caught in its spam filter.

It’s unclear if the company has notified its customers. Tigerfish did not respond to a follow-up email.

Discoveries of exposed datasets have consumed a large part of Sen’s early cybersecurity career so far. He scours the web daily using automated scanners and specialist search engines to identify databases, cloud storage, or other sensitive information that likely shouldn’t be left unprotected online.

The modern web runs on absolute trust, he said, and operates under the notion that the world’s data is kept secure. But he described how his findings reveal the reality of how “fragile” the internet is behind the scenes.

Despite the recent focus on how AI is accelerating people’s ability to discover vulnerabilities, Sen said that many of the data leaks and exposures that he finds come down to basic human errors.

“An engineer spinning up a test cloud database on a Friday, disabling authentication to test an API, and forgetting to shut the door before heading into the weekend,” was just one example, he recounted.

Sen’s findings over the years highlight the ongoing problems caused by such security mistakes, just as the new wave of vibe-coding and AI-generated development is creating new security problems due to insecure and unchecked code deployed to the web.

Similar to Amazon’s recent efforts to make it difficult to make S3 buckets public without going through clear multistep warnings, newer vibe-coding platforms like Lovable and Supabase have also made changes to their platforms to prevent user mistakes.

“Everyone often fears shadowy hackers,” Sen said. But he argues that the real crisis is leaving sensitive systems and databases “wide open to the public internet without a password.”

#183. Bending Spoons buys Mailchimp for $3.68B

Bending Spoons has bought up another distressed internet company. This time, it’s the email marketing firm Mailchimp, bought for $3.68 billion from the tax-preparation giant Intuit.

Intuit acquired the company in 2021 for roughly $12 billion, so it’s taking quite a loss — and, perhaps, Bending Spoons is getting quite a bargain. At the time of the sale to Intuit, that acquisition caused headlines because Mailchimp employees reportedly received no equity, so no share of the sale proceeds, because the founders had allegedly promised them they didn’t need it as they would never sell.

Flash forward to now, with Intuit hit hard by the Death Star to SaaS trade — aka AI — with the company’s shares worth less than half what they were a year ago. The company told Reuters that the sale will let it sharpen its focus on AI tools for small businesses, and the new cash will be used to repurchase shares.

Bending Spoons describes the transaction as an all-cash deal, although that’s not quite true: As part of the same package, Intuit is buying $368 million in newly minted shares of Bending Spoons, so the net effect is a split of 90% cash to 10% stock.

Still, the transaction is a major pickup for Bending Spoons, which has become a kind of clearinghouse for late-stage web properties, with its game plan to hold them long term. In just the last year, the company has snapped up AOL, Eventbrite, Airtable, and Miro. Previous acquisitions include older stalwarts like Evernote, Meetup, and Vimeo. In each case, Bending Spoons integrated the products into centralized engineering and revenue systems, resulting in significant cost savings while often improving the available support and product resources.

“We want to place ourselves as an operator that takes beloved brands and makes them much better,” Bending Spoons co-founder and chief product officer, Matteo Danieli, told TechCrunch in July.

#181. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#179. Chips can survive the space environment — can their financing?

The push to build data centers in space faces plenty of obstacles, from tricky engineering to a dearth of rockets that could take chips to orbit. But what about paying for the chips?

The data center ecosystem currently depends on debt financing, with cloud providers borrowing to build out data centers and then paying back the loans with a share of the revenue.

Those deals typically use the chips as collateral — if the data centers default on their loans, the lenders can take possession of the chips and resell them. That backstop makes it cheaper for companies to finance their chips. Even older chips retain value — according to Compute Credit Index Research, some H100s are selling for about half of their original cost basis from 2023, while some H200 configurations are selling for the same price they did when introduced in 2024.

But you’re not going to repossess chips installed in a satellite orbiting 300 miles above Earth at 17,500 miles per hour. The Financial Times is reporting that lenders behind SpaceX’s $40 billion chip financing deal asked that none of the chips purchased be used for the company’s orbital data center plans. It’s not clear if they succeeded in getting SpaceX to agree with the provision.

That may not matter to the company in the near term — SpaceX’s ability to deploy compute at terrestrial ground stations has proven lucrative, and on the company’s most recent earnings call, CEO Elon Musk detailed how he was dispatching engineers from the company’s rocket team to accelerate that build-out by taking on challenges like casting turbine blades for generators at faster than existing suppliers.

However, open questions remain about how easy it will be to find land and power to support the data center build-out. Many potential projects face delays and local opposition, while markets wait to see the state of compute demand and the ability of its buyers to turn it into revenue.

Musk, meanwhile, began saying at the beginning of 2026 that he believes space will be the “cheapest” place to operate data centers in two to three years. That’s unlikely until SpaceX can fly its Starship rocket in a fully reusable configuration. In its most recent flight test, the company failed to recover the booster stage but did succeed in landing the orbital stage of the rocket back at its launch facility in Texas. It’s not clear, though, when the second stage will fly again or how expensive it will be to prepare it for a second trip to space.

Even with Starship, however, SpaceX — as well as startups like StarCloud, Cowboy Space Corporation, Sophia Space, Orbital, and others — will need to buy chips. If the already daunting economics of space data centers weren’t challenging enough, it looks like they’ll be spending more to buy them than their terrestrial counterparts.

That might be an advantage for Google, which is focused on deploying its own TPU chips in space through Project Suncatcher. SpaceX and Tesla are working together to build their own chip foundry, Terafab, in the U.S., but that project has yet to break ground and will require years and billions of dollars before it starts producing semiconductors at scale.

#176. Super-hot rock geothermal could end data center energy woes

Geothermal startups are in a race to see who can tap the deepest, hottest rocks. The winners could power the next phase of the grid, data centers and all.

The broader markets got their first taste of enhanced geothermal earlier this year when Fervo Energy went public. But this week was all about super-hot rock geothermal.

Geothermal has become a darling among investors, tech companies, and even the Trump administration. The basic technology has been around for decades, but advances in drilling techniques borrowed from the oil and gas industry has supercharged it. The U.S. has plenty of potential, too, with up to 57 terawatts of generating capacity, more than enough to power the next wave of AI data centers.

An emerging class of geothermal energy, known as super-hot rock, could help the industry unlock much of that potential.

The secret to super-hot rock geothermal is what happens to water at extreme depths. As it gets closer to the superheated core of the Earth, both temperature and pressure rise significantly. When they get high enough, ordinary water turns into a supercritical fluid — a sort of gaseous-liquid state that can carry tremendous amounts of energy. A single super-hot rock well could generate 15 megawatts of electricity or more.

Super-hot rock geothermal is inching closer to commercial reality. Quaise Energy said on Tuesday that it had landed Google as a customer. The hyperscaler said it would pay more than $500 million to secure 50 megawatts of power from a Quaise project in central Oregon, which will tap into searing heat three miles deep.

Google is paying a premium for the amount of power Quaise will deliver, but that’s in line with the company’s strategy of getting in early with potential suppliers. For instance, it was an investor in and an early customer of Fervo.

Meanwhile, on Monday, Khosla-backed Mazama Energy announced it had figured out how to keep its drill heads cool as they tap into super-hot rocks. And last week, XGS Energy was given a grant by the Department of Energy to develop and refine materials, well designs, and models to improve reliability at depths where super-hot rock exists.

XGS is dipping its toes in the super-hot waters, while Mazama and Quaise have made it their core focus, though they’re taking very different approaches.

Quaise’s technology sounds like science fiction: Starting at about 2.5 miles deep, the company will swap out its conventional drill bit for one powered by a gyrotron, bombarding rock with millimeter-wave radiation and turning it into ash. The hope is that the technology can make drilling under super-hot conditions cost-effective, something that’s challenging with traditional metal drill bits, which wear out faster at those temperatures.

Mazama, on the other hand, thinks that traditional drill bits will work just fine if they can be kept cool.

The startup’s newly announced technology, Carbon Chill, uses carbon dioxide in place of water-based drilling “mud” to keep the bit cool. At those depths and temperatures, the carbon dioxide turns supercritical, allowing it to hold significant amounts of energy. Just above the drill bit, the CO2 flows through a valve that looks similar to a rocket nozzle. As the gas flows through the valve and expands on the other end, the expansion rapidly cools it, allowing it to absorb significant amounts of heat. The CO2 also removes drill cuttings on its trip to the surface, where it’s filtered and reused.

In both cases, Quaise and Mazama are betting that their technology could leapfrog today’s state-of-the-art commercial geothermal power plants. By going deeper and hotter, they’re hoping to get substantially more power out of each well. Given that drilling accounts for about a quarter to a third of a geothermal power plant’s total costs, drilling fewer wells could make a project more competitive.

Investors have been betting that the technologies will soon prove themselves. Quaise has raised more than $270 million from investors, including Prelude Ventures, Collaborative Fund, and Engine Ventures, with Vinod Khosla stepping in as an angel investor, according to PitchBook. Mazama has raised $171 million from investors, including Khosla Ventures, Doerr Capital, and Bill Gates.

As with any new technology, there’s some risk that the new techniques won’t pay off. But early results suggest they will — Mazama drilled its second well 80% faster than its first, and its CO2-cooling tech cut drilling time another 20%, co-founder and CEO Sriram Vasantharajan told TechCrunch.

That, plus Google’s vote of confidence in Quaise, suggests the race to super-hot rock geothermal is, well, heating up.

De Chant is also a lecturer in MIT’s Graduate Program in Science Writing, and he was awarded a Knight Science Journalism Fellowship at MIT in 2018, during which time he studied climate technologies and explored new business models for journalism. He received his PhD in environmental science, policy, and management from the University of California, Berkeley, and his BA degree in environmental studies, English, and biology from St. Olaf College.

#173. Measles in America: Here's a constructive approach to beating the disease, based on Brazil's experience

The US is now officially a measles contagion zone. By mid-2026, there were more infections than in all of the previous year. But the country could beat measles again, just like Brazil did two years ago.

Health experts saw it coming — the fact that after 26 years, the US would lose its status as measles-free. Back in July 2026, DW reported how one epidemiologist had suggested an 'amnesia' about the measles risk was pushing a growth in US infections.

Four months later, it was official. On November 17, 2026, the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) said the US had lost its measles elimination status, along with Mexico.

Look at Brazil, where in 2019, with more than 21,000 measles cases, the country also lost its measles-free status. But within five years — during the COVID-19 pandemic — it fought and won back its measles-free status. And since 2024, Brazil has stayed measles-free, even as the highly contagious disease spread across the rest of the Americas.

In 2026, with the data available up to the time of writing this article, Brazil had only had 70 measles cases. They were all linked to infections brought in from abroad, often from neighboring Peru.

Brazil has fewer people than the US, about 213 million compared to the US's nearly 350 million, but the two countries are similar in size. So the comparison is worth considering.

Brazil moves fast. The PAHO asks countries to respond within 72 hours of a suspected measles case. Here's Brazil's own rule:

It has also made hunting for cases a national habit. In a program called "Dia S," health workers knock on doors and visit schools, daycare centers and churches to find people who may be sick but have not seen a doctor.

Brazil also holds mass vaccination days called "Dia D." There was one in November, in the area where Brazil borders Colombia and Peru.

If someone is diagnosed with measles, health workers try to track down every person the patient may have met ("contact persons") and vaccinate them and other people living nearby.

The country has so far provided more than 16 million doses of the measles vaccine in 2026 and has donated 500,000 doses to Peru.

Brazil isn't perfect, though. According to official estimates from the World Health Organization, only 76% of children got both required doses of the vaccine in 2025 — well short of the 95% target. Experts warn this leaves the country at risk because measles is so highly contagious. A country needs to be as close to 100% immunized as possible to protect people and stop the disease from spreading.

"Elimination is not a trophy that we win once and put on a shelf. It must be protected every day," says PAHO Director Jarbas Barbosa.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The US lost its measles-free status because the PAHO found that the same strain of the virus had been spreading in the country for 12 months or more. Mexico and Bolivia lost their status on the same day as the US.

The US has had more than 5,400 cases in 2026 (at time of writing), more than twice as many as in all of 2025, and about 19 times as many as in 2024. Nearly everyone who got sick with measles was unvaccinated or had an unknown vaccination status.

US President Donald Trump signed an executive order on new vaccination recommendations for children in August 2026. It came at a time, when, as we reported, experts said people had stopped getting vaccinated against measles because infection rates were low. But like PAHO's Barbosa said, neglecting vaccination when infection rates are low is a mistake.

To be fair, Trump's executive order listed the measles vaccine in a top category of vaccinations recommended for every child. But the administration's stance on vaccination in general is unclear.

Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has often declared opposition to vaccination, including against measles, advising people to take vitamin A instead. In Texas, this led to children being hospitalized with toxic levels of the supplement, and left them with no protection against measles.

Before the COVID-19 pandemic, 95% of US kindergartners had their measles shots. Now, it's 92%, according to the US Centers for Disease Control and Protection (CDC). That leaves about 280,000 kindergartners in the US unprotected.

In the Texas counties where a 2025 measles outbreak began, coverage was as low as 77% at the time.

US children usually need vaccines to start school, but most states let parents opt out for religious or personal reasons, with no doctor involved.

But vaccination rates have fallen elsewhere in the world as well, including in Europe. In Germany, where measles infections have also risen, experts blame anti-vaccination rhetoric that emerged during the COVID-19 pandemic.

A person sick with measles can infect up to18 other, unprotected people. With vaccination, it is possible to increase the number of protected people and lower the number of people the sick person can infect.

To prevent a measles outbreak, a community needs about 95% of people to be vaccinated and/or immune. The vaccination consists of two doses of the MMR (measles, mumps, rubella) vaccine.

But even after the two doses, a person can expect to have only 97% protection against measles — 3% short of full protection — so, nearly everyone in the community has to be vaccinated to keep the average level of protect as close to 100% as possible.

"For some diseases like measles, even a small decrease in vaccination rates can lead to more outbreaks of disease [so] community immunity requires over 95% of susceptible individuals to be immunized," says Andrew Racine, president of the American Academy of Pediatrics.

And the 95% has to hold everywhere. A good national average can be affected by, for example, a single school where only 70% of the children are vaccinated. And that is where the outbreak is likely to start.

Around the world, measles deaths have dropped by 88% since 2000, thanks to vaccines.

But progress has stalled. Only about three out of four children worldwide get both doses of the vaccine. The biggest outbreaks right now are in Bangladesh, Indonesia and Guatemala.

Data from Europe shows that things can get better. In the European Union, cases fell from more than 35,000 in 2024 to about 2,300 the following year, with no measles-related deaths.

Romania went from more than 30,000 cases in 2024 to about 4,200 in 2025.

In Hungary, where 99% of children are vaccinated, there have been no measles cases in the past year.

But in January, six European countries lost their measles-free status, including the UK, Spain and Austria. Across Europe, eight out of ten people who got measles in 2025 were unvaccinated.

The Americas are seeing a major surge in measles infections, with more than 58,000 cases in 2026, over four times as many as by November in 2025. Guatemala has been hit hardest, with almost 35,000 cases.

The deadliest outbreaks are in Africa. African Union countries have so far reported nearly 1,800 measles deaths in 2026, about 1,650 of them in Democratic Republic of Congo. Yet, Sudan, despite the war, raised first-dose measles coverage by 22 percentage points in 2025, which was the world's largest gain.

Measles is extremely contagious. One person can infect up to 18 unprotected people, which makes it about 12 times more contagious than the flu.

The virus can linger in the air for up to two hours after a sick person has left the room. And people spread it before they know they are sick. Someone with measles is contagious for four days before the rash appears and four days after.

It's not just a rash. About one in five unvaccinated people who get measles in the US ends up in the hospital. Measles can weaken the immune system for years, making it harder to fight off other infections.

But it's preventable. Two doses of the MMR vaccine (which also protects against mumps and rubella) protect 97% of people for life.

#172. AI claimed a solution to the 167 year-old Riemann Hypothesis — and a human did, too

It’s a big week for Levent Alpöge: The mathematician saw his employer, Anthropic, become the first publicly-traded frontier AI lab. He also appears to have solved one of the most important problems in mathematics.

“The precise timing is horrible,” he wrote in an essay announcing his findings and describing how he messaged Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei last Sunday night to ask how to announce that he had found a Lean-compilable solution to the Riemann Hypothesis, which dates back to 1859 and is among the Millennium Prize math problems.

The Hypothesis conjectures that there is a hidden structure underlying the appearance of prime numbers. If the claimed result holds true, we’re entering sublime-mysteries-of-the-universe territory.

Maybe the best way to understand how exciting this is to the mathematics community is to watch the experts puzzling through the result in a chat on Zulip. Mathematicians like Chris Birbeck, a number theorist at the University of East Anglia, are posting thoughts like this …

The cohomology bound is proved by what looks like an automorphy-lifting argument. It genuinely uses the existence of Galois representations attached to regular algebraic, polarized cuspidal automorphic representations of GL_n over CM fields, along with base change, Jacquet–Shalika, Clozel’s purity, Bernstein–Zelevinsky, the main theorem of CM, Deligne–Ribet and more.

For my part, I tapped out of mathematics at college calculus, so I can’t help decode exactly what that means.

But that comment was followed by twenty readers reacting 🤯.

It will be a while before the mathematics community feels like it has a handle on the proof, but most appear confident that the formalized Lean result will hold. Lean is an an open-source language that essentially helps verify mathematical proofs.

Alpöge appeared in his essay to feel a bit guilty about releasing the proof without a truly human-intelligible explanation, but his obsession with the problem since childhood made him feel he “could not live” if he waited to release the finding.

Alpöge described a “stream-of-consciousness jaunt through the experience of finding all the pieces of the argument through incredible confusion with Claude” with more than a hint of AI euphoria, describing how the model “flew, and danced, and sparkled near that finish line.”

However, for all that action to mean anything, we’ll have to wait for humans to be able to understand the result and explain it to each other, something Alpöge has committed to pursue.

Alpöge and a collaborator, NYU mathematician Tristan Buckmaster, also released another important proof related to the Navier-Stokes equations, problems that helped kick off the race between frontier labs and mathematicians to solve outstanding problems this summer.

Meanwhile, Ravi Vakil, a Stanford professor and the president of the American Mathematics Association, posted a proof of the General Riemann Hypothesis written by Stanford postdoc Alessandro Masullo, noting that “it is all human, with no AI. The hope was to bring Alessandro to Stanford for a year to polish the writing and then announce it after running it by enough people. Now it seems best to put out the current draft.”

In a brief introduction his proof, which he said is the result of twelve years of work, Masullo wrote that “if any message at all this work is meant to send to the mathematician who feels discouraged by the pace and tone of the current climate, it is this: we are still here. We are very much alive. We are not going anywhere.”

#167. Latest botched execution prompts calls for US moratorium on lethal injections

Reinaldo Dennes screamed in pain and took over an hour to die – but Texas officials branded the execution a success

A botched execution in Texas on Thursday night that lasted more than an hour and caused the prisoner to scream out, begging the Lord to end his suffering, has led to renewed calls for a nationwide moratorium that would pause all lethal injections.

It took 63 minutes for Reinaldo Dennes, 70, to be proclaimed dead in the Huntsville penitentiary. The execution team had struggled to find a vein through which a lethal dose of the sedative pentobarbital could be administered.

Descriptions of the botched lethal injection bear chilling similarities to the recent failed execution in Tennessee of Christa Pike who in September became the only person ever to survive a full dose of execution drugs. Both Dennes and Pike were subjected to lethal injection protocols using pentobarbital, in both cases staff struggled to establish intravenous lines, and in both the prisoner expressed experiencing excruciating pain and burning sensations.

“The world saw what happened to Christa Pike, and now to Mr Dennes,” said Robin Maher, executive director of the authoritative Death Penalty Information Center. “The question now is, how many more of these botched executions will be tolerated by the American people?”

A harrowing eyewitness account of the night’s events was given by the prisoner’s spiritual adviser, the Rev Jeff Hood, who stood beside the gurney with his hand on Dennes’s shoulder. Hood describes how for up to five minutes Dennes fought against the pain.

“The pain!” Dennes shouted, according to Hood’s account.

“My leg, my leg, my leg,” he screamed, adding that his leg was “on fire”.

Hood writes in his account that Dennes “shifted on the gurney, back and forth, back and forth. He contorted. He grabbed hold of the leather straps and pulled against them until his body lifted. His shoulder drove up into my palm. Then it fell away. Then it drove up again. Twice he screamed, ‘It’s not working like it’s supposed to. Fuck. Fuck. Fuck. What is going on? Fuck. Fuck. Fuck.’”

Throughout the ordeal, Hood reports, the prisoner continued to pray, begging for an end to it.

“Take me home, Father. I don’t think this is normal. Take me, Father. Don’t let me suffer like this. C’mon.”

Dennes was convicted of capital murder in the 1996 death of Janos Szucs, a diamond dealer who was shot and killed in Houston.

Maya Foa, chief executive of the human rights group Reprieve, called for a moratorium on lethal injections. She said: “Lethal injection is torture – and Reinaldo Dennes was not sentenced to be tortured. Even the most ardent supporters of capital punishment must recognise that the dominant method of execution in the US is broken and cannot be allowed to continue.”

The Texas department of criminal justice insisted that the execution went fully according to plan. Its spokeswoman, Amanda Hernandez, told the New York Times that “as far as this agency is concerned, it is 100 percent not a botched execution. This was a successful execution, and we did follow all of the proper protocols to do it correctly.”

But such a blanket dismissal of any concerns failed to dampen a chorus of criticism. “Americans are now seeing the harsh reality of the death penalty, and I just don’t know how much longer state officials think they can double down on a story that is false,” Maher said.

Texas has been under the spotlight for many years for the way it obtains and stores its stocks of lethal execution drugs, a manoeuvre which it is careful to shroud in secrecy in an attempt to avoid public scrutiny. In the wake of an international boycott, the state’s prison service has taken to buying pentobarbital from compounding pharmacies that make up the chemicals to order.

Lawyers for condemned prisoners have long argued that the supplies can be of variable quality and are often held by the state beyond their expiration dates, running the risk of causing excruciating deaths.

The way that Texas conducts executions is likely to come under fresh review given the political upheaval that the state has experienced in the midterm elections. Not only did the governor’s mansion fall to the Democrat Gina Hinojosa, but Democrats flipped 20 state House seats as well as taking all seats up for election in the Texas supreme court and court of criminal appeals that have enormous power over capital cases.

Jonathan Groner, an expert on execution methods and emeritus professor of clinical surgery at Ohio State University, said that the Dennes execution illustrated why lethal injections were a “medical charade”. He said: “They imitate general anesthesia which is done in hospitals across the country thousands of times a day, by people with expertise, but when it is done in the death chamber without necessary equipment or skills, people suffer.”

Groner said that the location of the two IV lines placed in Dennes’s body – in a foot and in his shoulder – indicated that the execution team was struggling. “Placing IVs in the neck is very painful and difficult, and a last resort; and the foot is not a top choice because there is a long way to the heart for the medication to be circulated.”

He added: “It’s time for the medical profession to make a stand on executions. People are being tortured. We need to stop, and take a hard look at why we are still doing this.”

#165. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.
