#164. Can German auto manufacturing recover after Mercedes and VW cuts?

German automakers are pulling back on investments at home, sparking fresh anxiety about the auto sector's future. Some industry watchers think there is still a narrow window to stem the decline. But how?

Mercedes-Benz's announcement last week that it would halt further investments at its factories in Germany has added to the sense that the country's best automaking days are behind it.

In a written interview for staff, production chief Michael Schiebe noted how the running costs of German plants are far too high.

Schiebe added that the automaker is "now forced to carefully examine which German sites we would have to close if we cannot achieve a change in the cost structure."

The decision came one day after Volkswagen (VW), Europe's largest carmaker, said it was shelving plans to modernize its massive Wolfsburg plant.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Germany's auto sector is in a deep crisis, amid fierce rivalry from ambitious Chinese automakers. VW alone is looking to cut 100,000 jobs globally by 2030, while four of its German factories are earmarked for possible closure.

Yet, many auto analysts doubt this really marks the beginning of the end for car making in the country.

"We have to admit that a third of our capacity [in German plants] is overcapacity and that needs to be cut," Thomas Puls, senior economist for transport and infrastructure at the Cologne Institute for Economic Research (IW Köln), told DW. "This will hurt a lot."

A report last month by Munich-based consultancy Roland Bergeragreed that domestic automaking needs an urgent "reset," but predicted that Germany could still "preserve a sustainably profitable core of automotive value creation."

In other words, car making in Germany may shrink, but what remains could be leaner, more specialized and more profitable.

Many doomsayers overlook the fact that Mercedes' investment halt is tied to tough negotiations with unions over cutting labor costs at its German plants. This includes scrapping the 35-hour workweek. The investment freeze could be lifted if a deal is reached.

Even so, Achim Kampker, an electric mobility engineering professor at RWTH Aachen University, sees the investment cuts as more than a negotiating tactic.

Kampker compared China's lightning-fast industrial strategy with Germany's, which he said was "too slow," as excessive bureaucracy and complex business structures prevent value creation.

"We need to become faster, more innovative and significantly more productive," Kampker told DW.

China's EV-makers can bring a new model to market in around 20 months, versus 48 to 60 months for Germany, according to Roland Berger. Plus, the Chinese have a 30% cost advantage.

Production costs at German plants are also much higher — between €3,500 ($3,840) and €5,000 per vehicle — than in Eastern Europe, where they range from €2,000 to €2,500.

A separate report by consultancy PwC in September noted that despite German carmakers being well managed, well capitalized and knowing exactly what is coming, they may have a "false sense of security."

PwC said Germany needs to focus on four areas to ensure its auto industry is "future-fit" to compete with China.

These include splitting its internal combustion engine (ICE) business from the electric vehicle (EV) business, investing bumper ICE profits into those new technologies, redesigning workplace culture and training, and leveraging existing partnerships with Chinese automakers and US tech firms.

"The future viability of the German automotive industry depends significantly on decisions made in the next 12 to 18 months," wrote Harald Wimmer, global and German automotive leader at PwC Germany.

With the transition to EVs, some predict that automaking will become part of a much larger global electronics sector, which Germany must exploit.

Wimmer noted how top Chinese automakers were already benchmarking themselves against consumer electronics giants. These include smartphone maker Xiaomi, which now also produces around 400,000 EVs per year.

"Anyone who continues to base their competitive framework solely on their own industry is making a mistake that cannot be corrected once it becomes apparent," he warned.

Despite all the doom and gloom, car production within Germany is holding up pretty well.

Domestic manufacturing sits around 12% lower than pre-pandemic levels. This year, however, the auto sector is on course to meet a December estimate of 4.11 million vehicles, just 1% lower than last year, according to the German Association of the Automotive Industry (VDA).

IW Köln's Puls thinks German automakers must now avoid a trap Italy fell into in the early 2000s when its national auto champion, Fiat, struggled with profitability.

In Italy, "the politics and the unions blocked any attempt by Fiat to cut overcapacity," Puls told DW. "What's the result? They have a capacity of 1.1 to 1.2 million cars and utilization of about 250,000."

Last year, those same Italian plants built just 214,000 passenger cars, according to the FIM Cisl trade union, the fewest since 1954.

For Germany, the issue isn't a lack of expertise or ambition; it's that reforms to huge industries often get weighed down by caution and red tape.

As the country's auto giants try to push through painful cuts, RWTH Aachen University's Kampker warned that if they fail to address their shortcomings, "we will see more production sites closing and a substantial shift of industrial investment and employment away from Germany."

"There is still an opportunity to reverse this trend, but the window of opportunity is narrowing," Kampker concluded.

#162. Princeton doctoral student reportedly detained by ICE on way back from conference

Eva Yi Xie was taken into custody at Washington DC airport after presenting research at neuroscience conference

A Princeton University doctoral student has reportedly been taken into custody by US Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) after traveling to Washington DC, for a neuroscience conference.

Eva Yi Xie, a third-year PhD candidate at the Princeton Neuroscience Institute, was detained Thursday at a Washington-area airport, according to university officials and her colleagues.

The Daily Princetonian, the university’s student newspaper, reported that Xie is believed to be held at an immigration detention facility in Chantilly, Virginia.

The Brody Lab, a Princeton neuroscience research group, identified Ronald Reagan National airport as the location of her detention in a post on X. According to the lab, Xie had been returning from the Society for Neuroscience conference, where she presented a research poster, when immigration authorities detained her.

Princeton has since secured legal representation for Xie, university spokesperson Michael Hotchkiss confirmed to the Daily Princetonian.

The detention also prompted a late-night message to graduate students from Rodney Priestley, dean of Princeton’s graduate school. Priestley sent the email to students at the Princeton Neuroscience Institute late Thursday night, acknowledging the situation and outlining the university’s response.

“We hope she is able to soon resume her studies at Princeton,” the email, obtained by the Daily Princetonian, read. “Please be assured that we will stay in close contact with the student’s family, her legal team, and her PNI colleagues as this situation is resolved.”

Xie’s current immigration status has not been publicly disclosed.

Xie moved to the US from China in 2016. She earned degrees in computer science and mathematics from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology in 2024, alongside a concentration in economics, according to her portfolio.

Her professional experience includes internships at Meta, Microsoft AI and IBM Research, among other organizations. At Princeton, her doctoral work focuses on the “neural basis of decision-making,” according to the Daily Princetonian.

The Guardian has reached out to ICE and Princeton for comment.

Xie’s detention comes amid an aggressive nationwide immigration enforcement campaign under Donald Trump, whose administration has made mass deportations a defining priority.

Department of Homeland Security (DHS) figures show that ICE deported 483,200 people and conducted more than 491,000 immigration arrests during fiscal year 2026, setting new annual records for the agency.

The Trump administration maintains that its enforcement efforts are intended to uphold immigration laws and remove dangerous offenders from the country. However, federal figures and independent reviews indicate that substantial numbers of people kept in ICE detention have no criminal convictions.

The enforcement campaign has affected international students and immigrants who originally entered the US legally. Xie’s detention now joins a string of prominent immigration cases that have drawn worldwide scrutiny and raised serious questions about the administration’s tactics.

Among those cases is Rümeysa Öztürk, a Tufts University student detained by immigration agents in Massachusetts last year. Videos of Öztürk’s arrest spread rapidly online, drawing widespread attention to the administration’s treatment of international students.

She was among numerous foreign students targeted over pro-Palestinian expression and protest during demonstrations against Israel’s military campaign in Gaza. Those protests have been particularly prominent on American university campuses.

Another high-profile case involved Mahmoud Khalil, a 30-year-old Palestinian permanent resident and Columbia University graduate student who emerged as a leading figure in campus protests last year.

Khalil was arrested without due process in front of his pregnant wife and subsequently detained at an immigration facility. Authorities denied him permission to leave detention to attend the birth of his child.

The administration’s enforcement policies also came under intense scrutiny after the wrongful deportation of Kilmar Ábrego García, a Salvadoran immigrant who had been living in Maryland. Ábrego García was deported from the US to El Salvador in March 2025, despite the Trump administration’s admission that the removal was an error.

#159. Lauren Boebert says Trump ‘did some things to hurt’ her constituents

Controversial Colorado Republican narrowly lost House seat in midterms but says ‘I’m not mad at the president’

Lauren Boebert says Donald Trump “did some things that hurt” her constituents before they voted the pugnacious Colorado Republican out of her US House seat during midterm elections, which saw her and the president’s party lose control of Congress.

“This could be a referendum on President Trump,” Boebert said to the Associated Press in an interview published on Friday. “It could be the referendum on Congress or the Republican party. I think it’s all of it.

“I’m not mad at the president. [But] the president did some things that hurt my voters.”

Boebert expressed those thoughts during some of her first comments to the news media after Democratic challenger Eileen Laubacher defeated her by less than a 1% margin in a race called 10 days after the polls closed.

The conservative firebrand who first joined Congress in 2021 had widely been favored to win a fourth consecutive term. But low approval ratings for Trump after he started the war in Iran, pursuing an aggressive mass deportation campaign and other unpopular initiatives created an environment in which Democrats flipped nine seats in the US Senate and about four dozen in the House – including Boebert’s.

In Friday’s interview, Boebert pinned some of the blame for that outcome on the agenda that Trump set for the first half of his second presidency. But she also said, “it’s all of the Republican party,” which had majorities in both congressional chambers dating back to the latter part of Joe Biden’s lone Oval Office term.

“I think it’s more all of us,” Boebert told the AP, alluding to high fuel prices after the Iran war, Republican infighting and unfulfilled promises. “We had solutions, and we didn’t take action.”

Boebert navigated various controversies throughout her time in Congress, which began with her supporting Trump’s lies that the 2020 election that he lost to Biden was unduly rigged by fraudsters.

A 2023 scandal saw her and a man with whom she was on a date get kicked out of a performance of the stage production Beetlejuice in Denver for inappropriate behavior, including vaping and groping each other. She later apologized for the conduct, which was recorded on surveillance video, saying: “I simply fell short of my values.”

Just weeks before her contest against Laubacher, an ethics complaint filed against her accused her of having sexual relationships with three staffers, including one who was paid to stay silent. She denied the allegations cited in the complaint, which she dismissed as “politically motivated”.

Boebert’s interview with the AP on Friday was not the first time she has spoken out against Trump. Notably, she was among the Congress members who most loudly called for the full disclosure of files related to the prosecution of Jeffrey Epstein, the late accused sex trafficker whose social circle encompassed the US and global elite – even after the Trump administration tried to convince her to drop her support for a measure that would compel such a release.

Trump responded to Boebert by calling her “dumb”. He also kicked off his second presidency by vetoing a Boebert-sponsored bill that would have funded a drinking water project in her district after the legislation unanimously cleared the House and Senate.

That prompted a critical statement from Boebert saying: “If this administration wants to make its legacy blocking projects that deliver water to rural Americans, that’s on them.”

Boebert struck a more conciliatory tone in Friday’s interview with the AP, remarking, “I respect President Trump, I still work with him, I want to continue to work with him. Not for him, with him.”

She only referred to her future plans in vague terms, telling the outlet: “Too many people come to Congress and get rich. I am not one of those. I’m gonna go make some money now.”

#158. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#156. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#153. ‘Worth fighting for’: senators vow to restore financial watchdog agency after Guardian investigation

Democrats – including incoming banking chair and both majority leader candidates – said wealthy interests were prioritized over the needs of working Americans

In response to a Guardian investigation, four US Senate Democrats say they will work to resurrect a financial watchdog agency that has abandoned dozens of enforcement actions under the second Trump administration.

The Guardian found that after the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB) dropped more than 20 lawsuits against banks and corporations accused of a range of financial misconduct, complaints against those firms rose by an average of more than 75%. In many of these recent complaints, consumers described the same issues the CFPB’s suits had sought to address.

The Massachusetts senator Elizabeth Warren – who first proposed the CFPB’s creation after the 2008 financial crash, and is now poised to become chair of the Senate banking committee in January – promised to investigate the administration’s attempts to weaken the CFPB.

“Donald Trump has sidelined the financial cop on the beat that has returned more than $21bn directly to Americans who were cheated by big banks and giant corporations,” Warren said in a statement.

The Guardian’s reporting, she added, “shows what came next: Trump’s billionaire friends and Wall Street donors getting away with scamming Americans. The next Congress will defend the CFPB, investigate how the Trump administration has abandoned cheated Americans, and put forward legislation to protect consumers and bring down costs.”

Since Trump took office, the CFPB has dramatically scaled back public enforcement actions against companies – even while Americans submit record numbers of complaints to the agency. After filing an average of roughly 30 public enforcement actions each year for more than a decade, the bureau has initiated just one since then director Rohit Chopra, a Joe Biden appointee, was fired on 30 January 2025.

The leadership of the CFPB under the second Trump administration has claimed that the agency has long been harmful to companies and consumers alike. An agency spokesperson asserted in a statement to the Guardian that the CFPB had cost Americans hundreds of billions of dollars – “making the American Dream, like homeownership, more costly by the thousands for each borrower”.

“This Weaponized agency attacked political enemies and disfavored industries and used novel legal theories and secret justifications to ruin lives and businesses,” the statement added. “We are fixing that and bringing the agency back to operating within statute and away from breaking the law and helping all business and Americans who were victims of thuggery.”

The Guardian’s reporting on the agency comes in the wake of midterm elections in which the economy and cost of living concerns were at the top of most voters’ minds.

In response to the investigation, the Senate minority leader, Chuck Schumer, vowed to “make sure that the CFPB is operating at its full capacity” once Democrats gain control of Congress.

“The Trump administration has done everything in its power to bow down to special interests and billionaires at the expense of American families,” Schumer said. “The CFPB was created to make sure that everyday folks could be protected from predatory actors, but Republicans have undermined it at every turn.”

The Maryland senator Chris Van Hollen, who announced on 12 November that he would challenge Schumer for the role of Senate majority leader when the new Congress convenes, said the CFPB’s rollback demanded greater focus on working Americans.

“The CFPB returned billions of dollars to Americans who were conned and scammed by big corporations,” Van Hollen said in a statement. “Naturally, many billionaires didn’t like that, so they had Trump and his cronies use every tool they could to dismantle an agency whose job is to stand up for working families.”

Van Hollen’s fellow senator from Maryland, Angela Alsobrooks, also weighed in.

“The CFPB works for the American people,” she said. “The CFPB is worth fighting for.”

#150. Better educated than ever — yet Germany's Gen Z is being shut out of its own future

Never before have young people faced so many challenges. Young Germans are asking: Will I be better off than my parents later in life?

It is becoming increasingly difficult for young people in Germany to find a job after finishing university or professional training. And finding an affordable place to live in a big city, and finally starting to live independently is becoming almost impossible.

Furthermore, young people do not feel that politicians are taking action on issues that matter to them, such as climate protection. The climate conference in Antalya, Turkey, is a case in point. It, therefore, shouldn't be a surprise that young people across Europe once again took to the streets this Friday, which was World Children's Day too, to make their voices heard. In Germany, Gen Z protests took place in 73 towns and cities.

What particularly infuriates many young people is being talked about rather than spoken to. Equally infuriating is seeing their protests branded as criminal before they have even begun, all because of the actions of a small minority.

One example of such rhetoric was when the youth wing of the conservative Christian Democratic Union/Christian Social Union (CDU/CSU) party in Berlin-Mitte suggested deploying the German army against demonstrations.

It can also be seen in the media, such as when Bild, Germany's highest-circulation tabloid, ran a headline that translated as: "Young, male, known to the police — who rioted after the demonstrations and where chaos threatens today."

"Young people are taking to the streets because they feel they are not being heard. And, of course, that feeling is strongly reinforced when the press devotes no more than a brief paragraph to their demands while focusing the rest of its coverage on how these protests could become problematic," says Erik Sczygiol, who until recently was deputy secretary-general of the Federal Conference of Student Representatives. "Of course, any potential security concerns need to be addressed, but they should not overshadow media coverage of the protests."

The 18-year-old Sczygiol is almost an exception. He was invited to participate in the launch of the World Population Report in Bonn alongside representatives of the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA) and the German Foundation for World Population (DSW). This year's report, entitled "Succeeding Generations: Empowering young people to chart the future," is particularly noteworthy because it focuses on Gen Z.

UN researchers surveyed 109,000 people across 73 countries for the report, some of whose findings are encouraging. Young people worldwide are better educated than any generation before them, with 78% of adolescents across the globe completing at least lower secondary education. But finding a job is becoming increasingly difficult, while informal employment is on the rise.

This economic uncertainty has far-reaching consequences for young people. According to the report, people often do not achieve financial independence until they are in their mid-to-late 20s, and a stable partnership and parenthood are often not realities before their early-to-mid 30s.

Nor do young people have a seat at the political decision-making table when it comes to shaping future reforms in the labor market, housing or climate protection. Florian Brod, 25, points out: "Almost half of the world's population is under 30, yet only 2.6% of parliamentarians worldwide are under 30."

Young people need and want change to protect their livelihoods and those of future generations. "Yet those who will have to live longest with the consequences of today's decisions are precisely the ones who have hardly any representation in the political decision-making process," says Brod.

He is somewhat close to the political decision-making process as he has served as a Youth Delegate for Sustainable Development for almost two years. In this role, he represents the interests of young people in Germany at United Nations conferences. This is certainly an important step towards giving his generation a greater say in political processes.

Nevertheless, both Brod and Sczygiol have had similar experiences. They often participate in conferences and panel discussions and are invited to submit their opinions, but these invitations often amount to little more than symbolic gestures.

"Having access to an international conference is a first step. But without substantive briefings ahead of the conference, without involvement in preliminary negotiations and, in some cases, without access to negotiation texts, there is little opportunity for us to help shape the outcome. When young people see their demands making little headway politically while they are simultaneously excluded from the search for solutions, frustration inevitably builds," says Brod.

Sczygiol adds: "We often get the feeling at the Federal Youth Conference that we were invited simply to provide a nice photo opportunity. We may be sitting at the table, but often only once a bill has already gone through 17 different drafts and is just days away from its final vote in the Bundestag. By that stage, young people have virtually no say in changing anything, and their voices are barely heard. Their contributions are often simply dismissed with: 'Well, that's just typical youthful nonsense.' And that is something that really frustrates young people."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Medical student Amelie Belosevic knows that feeling all too well. She served as Germany's Youth Delegate at the World Health Assembly in Geneva in May. Twenty-five countries sent youth representatives, which Belosevic sees as a clear step forward. However, she has one criticism. According to the United Nations Population Fund, 90% of the world's 1.8 billion young people live in the Global South. Yet, the region was represented by only eight delegates. As a result, Africa, Latin America and large parts of Asia were left virtually voiceless.

Her demands for Germany include better job prospects for young people entering the workforce and sustainable pension systems 40 years from now. "For a very long time, people have said 'I believe my children will have a better life than I do.' But that is no longer necessarily true. So it makes sense then that those who feel the greatest anxiety about the future are the people who have most of their life ahead of them. Sometimes, people don't understand that. We young people need to be able to believe in our future again!"

This article was originally published in German.

#148. Home Alone sequel reportedly in development with Macaulay Culkin returning

Christmas comedy hit is set for a follow-up from the point of view of the father with Seth Rogen as writer and producer

A Home Alone sequel is reportedly in development with original star Macaulay Culkin set to return.

According to Puck’s Matthew Belloni, Culkin met with Disney executives earlier this year with a pitch for a follow-up and now the project has been met with a green light. The new Home Alone film will bring the 46-year-old back to the franchise as Kevin McCallister for a comedy that, according to Deadline, will be from the father’s point of view.

The film will also recruit Seth Rogen and Evan Goldberg as writers and producers and they will work with Culkin to craft the script. Rogen and Goldberg recently worked with Disney on a Muppets TV special.

Home Alone was a major box office hit upon release in 1990, grossing $476m worldwide and becoming the year’s second-biggest movie. Culkin returned for the sequel Home Alone 2: Lost in New York, which made $359m two years later.

The franchise continued without the actor for three further movies and then in 2021, after Fox had been bought by Disney, there was a straight-to-streaming reboot.

Culkin later reprised the role in a 2018 commercial for Google and this year has once again returned as Kevin for a new McDonald’s advert.

Last year, he was asked about a sequel at an anniversary event.

“I kind of had this idea,” he said. “I’m either a widower or a divorcee. I’m raising a kid and all that stuff. I’m working really hard and I’m not really paying enough attention and the kid is kind of getting miffed at me and then I get locked out.”

In his pitch, the character’s son won’t let him in and would be the one setting the traps with the house as “some sort of metaphor for our relationship”.

The original film’s director, Chris Columbus, who later went on to direct the first two Harry Potter adventures, has spoken out about a possible sequel. “I think it’s a mistake to try to go back and recapture something we did 35 years ago,” he said. “I think it should be left alone.”

#147. No one else in Reform is suitable to lead the party, Nigel Farage says

Farage issues warning to potential rivals and says he will fight next election as leader if ‘still here and fit and healthy’

Nigel Farage has said he does not see another person who could do his job as leader of Reform UK, in a warning shot to potential rivals who might seek to replace him.

The Reform UK leader delivered a message that he was going nowhere despite his worries over his personal safety in the wake of the killing of the Reform politician Ann Widdecombe.

There has been speculation over Farage’s future in light of police and parliamentary investigations of Reform’s financial affairs. The Commons standards watchdog is due to report soon on whether he should have declared a £5m personal gift from the crypto billionaire Christopher Harborne before the last election. If he gets a substantial suspension from parliament, he could face a second byelection, contested by other main parties, in his Clacton constituency.

However, Farage sounded a note of defiance in an interview with the Sun, saying: “These are very thoughtful moments, but, you know what, if I’m not here doing this job right now, who else could? Who else would? I don’t see that person. So I’m gonna do it.”

A number of other Reform UK politicians are potential contenders to succeed him, including the party’s economics spokesperson and former Conservative, Robert Jenrick, the home affairs spokesperson, Zia Yusuf, and the former leader Richard Tice. Another possibility is the return of Rupert Lowe, who left Reform in acrimonious circumstances and founded his own party, Restore Britain, but who is popular on the online hard right.

The party is going through a difficult period after slipping from first place in the polls to second or third behind behind Labour and sometimes the Conservatives.

Asked by the Sun if he had “blown” his chances of reaching No 10, Farage said: “No, far from it.”

He admitted there had been “self-inflicted wounds” in the past year but said he still had a chance of becoming prime minister.

He told the newspaper that he would fight the next election as Reform leader if he was “still here and fit and healthy”.

He also batted away questions about the £5m, which he has insisted was to pay for his personal security despite the fact the party has been providing this.

“What’s it got to do with you? Drugs, sports cars, desert islands. It’s a gift,” he said, adding that although the party had had “ebbs and flows” and had made mistakes after growing “too far and too fast”, he was confident it was “more than through the worst of it”.

On the prospect of another battle over Europe as Andy Burnham weighs the case for rejoining the EU, the Reform leader said: “If you ask me, have I got the energy and the enthusiasm to do this again? I would say to you, hold my jacket. I’m ready for it.”

He also warned of people taking to the streets with “direct action” if Brexit is reversed.

In a separate interview with the Times while travelling to Brussels, where Burnham was attempting a reset deal, Farage said he believed the issue of Europe would dominate the years to come.

“It’s going to be absolutely huge. It’s going to be much bigger than anyone realises. The genie is out of the bottle; it literally can’t be stopped now,” he said.

He also ruled out joining a new campaign called Stay Out, led by former Tories, suggesting he would make his own case for keeping Brexit.

#145. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#143. Two men killed after glider crashes into Loch Leven in Scotland

The pair had taken off from the Scottish Gliding Centre at nearby Portmoak on a training flight

Two men have been killed after their glider crashed into Loch Leven, a large body of water in Perth and Kinross, on Friday morning.

Police Scotland said their bodies were recovered from the loch by the emergency services after the alarm was raised at 11.05am on Friday.

The force said next of kin had been told and the incident was not being treated as suspicious. Two HM Coastguard helicopters and an air ambulance were sent to the scene, along with a fire service appliance and a specialist crew.

The pair had taken off from the Scottish Gliding Centre at Portmoak airfield near the loch’s eastern shore in a Perkoz two-person glider on a training flight.

A spokesperson for the gliding centre said: “The incident is currently under investigation by the Air Accident Investigation Branch [AAIB] of the Department for Transport and the police in conjunction with the British Gliding Association.

“Our club is cooperating fully with these organisations. At the present time, there is no indication as to the cause of the accident.”

An AAIB spokesperson said: “A multidisciplinary team including inspectors with expertise in aircraft operations, engineering and recorded data have been deployed to gather evidence and make inquiries.”

#141. Ukraine updates: Russian attack 'one of largest,' Zelenskyy says

Ukrainian President Zelenskyy said Russia launched one of the largest aerial attacks since the invasion began. Poland accused Moscow of a "provocation," saying two missiles landed in the NATO country. Follow DW.

Below you can follow the latest updates from Russia's war in Ukraine on Friday, 20th November:

German Defense Minister Boris Pistorius "welcomed in every respect" a move by Russian President Vladimir Putin to consult Europeans on the future of Ukraine (see entries below for more details).

During a trip to the Polish capital Warsaw, Pistorius added that declining such an invitation to Moscow would send the "completely wrong signal."

"We need to talk. Only by talking can we ultimately achieve results," he said.

Pistorius said reaching an agreement was the goal — not simply sitting down in a room together.

"He [Putin] must now show that he truly means what he says and that he will then take the necessary steps to ensure that, at the very least, a ceasefire is established and that peace-sustaining stability can once again take hold in Europe," the German minister said.

The comments came the same day a pair of Russian missiles and a drone were reported to have violated Polish airspace (see entries below).

While Russian officials said Germany, France and the UK had been invited to send representatives to talks on Ukraine, no details about a time or place for such talks have been announced.

Denmark's PET intelligence service has said it suspected Russia was behind a plot to kill a US-Ukrainian rapper at a Copenhagen concert.

"In our assessment, this case shows that Russian intelligence services attempted to carry out a targeted killing in Denmark," PET head Rasmus Kieffer-Kristensen told a news conference.

Kieffer-Kristensen added that Russia was suspected of enlisting the help of a local criminal gang for the killing.

The Danish Foreign Ministry subsequently summoned the Russian ambassador and expelled the Russian Embassy's defense attache, Foreign Minister Lars ​Lokke ​Rasmussen said.

The foreign minister also called on European countries to stop issuing visas to Russians and said the Danish government would have taken harsher steps if Russia had a larger diplomatic presence in Denmark.

This week, Ukraine launched a series of strikes on the Kerch bridge, which links occupied Crimea to mainland Russia.

The 19-kilometer (12-mile) bridge has both rail and roadways, part of which has been destroyed in the strikes.

The bridge is widely seen as a symbol of Russia's annexation of Crimea in 2014, and damage to it is regarded as a significant psychological blow to Moscow.

Following the strikes, Russian President Vladimir Putin vowed to retaliate and warned the response would be "far more painful" than the damage Ukraine had caused to the Kerch bridge, as seen in the sliding image below.

At least 1,144 children were killed or injured in Ukraine during the first 10 months of 2026, the United Nations said.

UNICEF, the UN's children's agency, said that between January and October, at least 114 children were killed and 1,030 injured.

The agency said it was the highest number of casualties seen in more than 3 years.

"That is more than three children who have been killed or injured every single day" this year, said UNICEF spokeswoman Louise Wateridge.

Last month alone, at least 146 children were killed or injured across Ukraine, the UN agency said.

"Attacks involving missiles and armed drones continue to put children and families at risk in their homes, schools, and communities, including far from the frontline," UNICEF warned.

British Prime Minister Andy Burnham has called Russia's violation of Polish airspace "utterly unacceptable."

"The incursion into ​Polish airspace is utterly unacceptable. I condemn it unreservedly," ​Burnham said at an EU-UK ​summit press conference ‌in Brussels.

The incursions were reported as Poland held military exercises involving the "Coalition of the Willing" that ​groups allies of Ukraine, including the UK.

British Defense Secretary Wes Streeting said the move was a Russian provocation aimed at dividing Europe.

"Europe ​will not be ‌deterred from our support for Ukraine," Streeting said. "We will not allow ⁠Putin to divide our continent, and we recognise that the ⁠frontline ​in Ukraine is the frontline for freedom and democracy everywhere."

Russian Deputy Foreign Minister Sergei Ryabkov has reportedly invited representatives from Germany, France and the UK to Moscow for talks on the war in Ukraine, according to Russian news agency Interfax.

"We are waiting for a reply," the diplomat was reported to have said.

On Thursday, Russian President Vladimir Putin said European officials would be invited to Moscow to discuss whether they should participate in US-led talks aimed at ending the war in Ukraine.

Interfax reported that Ryabkov stressed that such a visit would not automatically mean that European nations would be involved in the negotiations on ending the war on Ukraine.

A statement from the French president's office said Europeans were ready to visit Moscow if given a full role in Ukraine talks.

"Our requirement will be that the opening or formalisation of this dialogue clearly signify, from the Russian perspective, that Europeans have their full role in the negotiations and participate actively in the next round of talks with the United States and Ukraine," an advisor to President Emmanuel Macron told reporters. European leaders have been pushing for a seat at the negotiation table, with German Chancellor Friedrich Merz saying on Thursday that the outcome of the war in Ukraine would determine European security for years to come.

The European Commission said it has released another €7.9 billion ($8.67 billion) ‌to ⁠Ukraine to support the country's finances and keep public administration running.

"Ukraine continues to deliver on reforms, while defending itself each and every day. Today, we are disbursing nearly €8 billion — this is the largest payment made so far," European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said.

"This makes it clear: real progress on reforms brings real results and benefits. As Ukraine perseveres and moves further on its European path, they can count on our support," said von der Leyen.

It’s the ninth disbursement of funds under the mechanism to support Ukraine.

With today's release of funds, the EU and its member states have provided €245.5 billion in overall support to Ukraine, which includes €3.8 billion from the proceeds of immobilized Russian assets.

The German Foreign Ministry has expressed its support for Poland after reports a Russian missile had entered Polish airspace.

"Russian missiles have once again violated Polish airspace. This is part of Russia's ongoing provocations," Germany's Foreign Ministry said in a statement on X.

"Instead of signaling a willingness to negotiate, Putin is signaling the opposite: he is opting for confrontation," the post said.

"We stand firmly by our neighbor and NATO ally Poland," the statement concluded.

NATO Secretary General Mark Rutte said he had spoken to Poland's Prime Minister Donald Tusk about what he called "grave violations of Polish airspace overnight."

Rutte said that NATO jets had been scrambled in response.

"We stand in solidarity with Poland and will continue to strengthen our ability to deter and defend," Rutte said.

"This incident is clearly dangerous — a consequence of Russia’s ongoing war against Ukraine," the NATO chief said.

"I condemn Russia's attacks as well as its ongoing war. We continue to stand united as Allies and will continue to support Ukraine," Rutte said.

French Foreign Minister Jean-Noel Barrot said that Paris had requested a UN Security Council meeting after Russia launched one of the war's largest missile attacks on Ukraine.

"The Kremlin has struck Kyiv again. Civilians have been killed," Jean-Noel Barrot said on X.

"These are crimes. France condemns them in the strongest possible terms," Barrot said.

"I have requested a meeting of the United Nations Security Council. Russia will have to answer for its actions there," said France's top diplomat.

On Thursday at the Coalition of the Willing talks in Berlin, French President Emmanuel Macron said that Ukraine needed to be able to defend itself and that allies needed to ensure Kyiv had the means to do so.

The government of Estonia said road border crossings with Russia would be shut from December 1 on security grounds.

"The closure ​of the border crossing points is not a reaction to a ​single incident, ‌but the next step following Russia's aggression against Ukraine and the increasing frequency of sabotage and hybrid operations in Europe," Foreign Minister Margus Tsahkna said in a statement.

The Kremlin has denied the allegations saying they're part of a pattern of false allegations against Moscow originating from European countries.

Russian Foreign Ministry spokeswoman Maria Zakharova accused Estonia of  planning to erect a new "Iron Curtain" and said it would respond.

"Should Tallinn proceed with these ‌plans, our country will take the necessary ‌political ​and diplomatic measures in response to safeguard its national interests," Zakharova told a press briefing in Moscow.

The commander of Ukraine's Unmanned Systems Forces, Robert "Magyar" Brovdi said that his forces had hit 40 Russian oil facilities and defense industry targets in the past 50 days.

Brovdi said via the Telegram that among those targets, five of them had been hit more than once.

He went on to say that ​the Dorogobuzh ​chemical plant ‌in Russia's Smolensk region was ​struck ⁠on November 10 ⁠and ​November 20.

Ukraine has managed to adapt on the battlefield with the use of drone technology which has allowed hundreds of drone sorties, in some cases deep inside Russian territory, on a daily basis.

Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said that Ukraine was targeted by more than 400 drones and more than 110 missiles, 27 of which he said were ballistic missiles.

"The Russians spread this attack across many cities and regions of Ukraine. Absolutely all the targets were ordinary civilian infrastructure – things that literally sustain people’s lives," Zelenskyy said in a detailed post on social media platform X.

Zelenskyy listed what had been hit during the overnight strikes

He accused Russia of carrying out second strikes on targets that had already been hit, in so-called "double-tap strikes."

"People had essentially already been killed by the time specialists were inspecting the initial damage to the facilities, as the Russians were carrying out double-tap strikes," Zelenskyy said.

Zelenskyy said that in all 79 cruise missiles had been intercepted and that fighter jets had been scrambled to deal with the aerial threats involving F-16s, Mirages and MiGs.

"Ballistic missiles are, unfortunately, much more difficult to deal with, and that depends not on our capabilities but on the supply of missiles by our partners," Zelenskyy said.

Ukraine's leader called for a "clear response" from the global community.

It is important that our partners not only issue statements in support of Ukraine and Ukrainians, but also follow through on specific agreements on air defense," Zelenskyy said.

He pointed out the timing of the latest attacks and said Putin launched them "in a highly demonstrative manner – following weak meetings in China."

"Russia is trying to compensate for its military and diplomatic impotence with precisely this kind of terror, precisely these attacks on life," Zelenskyy said.

Ukrainian officials said that the death toll has risen to at least 8 following an overnight Russian aerial bombardment.

Prime Minister Sergii Koretskyi said six of the eight people killed were ‌in Kyiv. Two deaths were also reported at a railway station in the western Ukrainian city of Lviv.

Ukraine's Air Force said Russia had launched 84 cruise missiles in the attack, adding that almost all of them were destroyed.

An unspecified number of ballistic missiles and more than 400 attack drones were also reported to have been destroyed.

Poland's Defense Minister Wladyslaw Kosiniak-Kamysz said that several Russian flying objects entered Polish airspace during recent air attacks on Ukraine.

"Russian long-range aviation was operating, launching both Kinzhal and Kh-101 missiles, as well as a large number of jet-powered drones and conventional drones. … This Russian Federation attack on Ukrainian territory, which also had an impact on the Republic of Poland, was one of the largest since the beginning of Russia's full-scale war against Ukraine," the Defense Ministry quoted Kosiniak-Kamysz as saying.

It comes after Polish prosecutors reported earlier that a flying object had crashed in Poland's eastern Lublin region.

Polish officials said it had taken place during heavy Russian airstrikes on neighbouring Ukraine.

In July, a missile entered Polish airspace and crashed in the same region near the village of Tarnawa Kolonia.

According to the Polish government, it was a Russian CH-101 cruise missile.

#140. Florida mechanic found guilty of murdering British woman who attorney says came to US to die

Dwain Hall was convicted of stabbing Sonia Exelby, a music teacher who prosecutors say suffered from mental illness

A Florida mechanic has been found guilty of stabbing to death a British music teacher, whom he convinced to travel to the United States.

Dwain Hall, an auto repair and tow truck driver, groomed 32-year-old Sonia Exelby from Portsmouth, England, on a fetish website before luring her to the US.

Hall had pleaded not guilty to the charges of kidnap and first-degree murder.

At trial, jurors heard from several witnesses, most of them law enforcement officials, who detailed Hall’s email, phone records, social media accounts and other records to trace his movements after Exelby travelled from the UK to meet him.

Exelby’s disappearance from her home sparked an international investigation. Prosecutors this week described how she suffered from mental illness. Investigators said that Exelby had traveled to Florida after posting online about wanting someone to end her life.

Hall’s arrest affidavit said that Exelby had previously expressed suicidal thoughts and investigators alleged that Hall knew about her struggles and used that vulnerability to bring her to Florida, the Ocala Starbanner reported.

The authorities said Exelby boarded a flight to the US seemingly with an intention “to be possibly sexually abused, tortured and murdered by unknown individuals living in the United States”.

After Exelby traveled to the US in October 2025, authorities say Hall picked her up from the Gainesville airport in central Florida.

He drove her to an Airbnb rental, where investigators later found evidence they say connected him to her disappearance and death, including transactions on her bank cards and a video of Exelby on Hall’s cellphone.

According to the Times, Junior Barrett, Hall’s attorney, argued that Exelby took her own life. “She came here with the intention of dying, she thought she was worthless, useless, a burden on other people,” he said in his closing statement.

“This isn’t a question about what porn he likes. You can disagree with him about what he likes, but the case can’t be decided on you not liking his lifestyle. The fact he did not stop her from killing herself does not make it murder.”

Prosecutor Amy Beth Berndt said jurors should use their “common sense”. She added that Hall targeted Exelby for her mental illness and killed her for his sadistic pleasure.

Berndt said Exelby “was beautiful. She was kind and she was deeply loved by everyone who knew her. And she also suffered from mental illness for most of her life. She should be alive today.”

She added: “No one could save her from the defendant, the ‘Alpha Sadist’. He took advantage of a severely depressed, mentally ill woman who was incapable of making any rational decisions.”

After the verdict was read, Exelby’s brother Barry Wilton said: “This last year has been one of the hardest of our lives. Losing our dear sister Sonia has had a profound impact on me and my entire family. I’m not sure we will ever be the same again.”

Wilton then addressed Hall directly, adding: “You pretended to care for Sonia. You didn’t at all. You used her to fulfil your sick fantasies. You had her life in your hands. You had the opportunity to save her, but instead you preyed on her vulnerabilities and you took her from us.”

#139. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#135. Police arrest teacher who missed sentencing after indecent assault conviction

Sally-Anne Bowen was found guilty of having sexual relationships with two pupils at a boys’ school in the 1980s

A chemistry teacher who had sexual relationships with two pupils at a boys’ school in the late 1980s has been arrested by police in Hampshire after she failed to turn up to her sentencing hearing.

Sally-Anne Bowen, 65, could not be found at her home by Metropolitan police officers on Friday morning after a warrant was issued for her arrest, a court heard.

The former teacher, who was in her 20s when she had sex with one pupil up to 30 times and kissed a second boy when they were aged about 14 to 15, was convicted in August of seven counts of indecent assault and granted bail before her sentencing.

Her trial heard she wore short, tight skirts and flirted in class, with one former pupil comparing her behaviour to “a Sabrina Carpenter video”.

After her conviction she was granted bail and was due to be sentenced at Harrow crown court on Friday.

But Bowen, from Hemel Hempstead, Hertfordshire, did not turn up to court in the morning, and the judge, Karim Ezzat, issued a warrant for her arrest, “not backed by bail”.

Neelam Gomersall, prosecuting, said Bowen’s partner of 36 years, Darren Scotchbrook, was at the address when officers arrived, but Bowen was not.

Gomersall had earlier told the court there was “no evidence” that Bowen could not attend the sentencing, and that information from Scotchbrook was “suggestive of refusal rather than inability” to attend.

Bowen’s barrister, Mark Kimsey, said: “Our instructing solicitor has tried to contact her and the phone rings but doesn’t answer.”

He had earlier said that Bowen was in contact with solicitors on Thursday and had engaged with a pre-sentence report, adding there had been “concerns as to her welfare due to the content of the messages” from her.

In an update on Friday afternoon, the Met said: “On Friday November 20, officers were made aware that a 65-year-old woman had failed to appear at Harrow crown court for sentencing.

“A warrant was issued for her arrest and officers promptly launched inquiries to locate her.

“At around 3pm, officers from a neighbouring force found the woman at a location in Hampshire and arrested her on suspicion of failing to appear at court.
