“She has been taken into police custody. A new sentencing date will be set by the court in due course.”

Bowen’s trial heard that she was in her mid-20s when she taught at Christ’s College in Finchley, north London.

One victim had told jurors he had had sex with her “about 20 or 30 times” over several months, considered her his girlfriend and kept it secret. He said it had led to “the destruction of my education”.

The second victim described spending lunchtimes with Bowen in a cafe, where he was “goaded” into kissing her by other boys and recalled touching her breasts.

#133. JP Morgan boss says he will set ‘example’ in offsetting UK tax hike on banks

Jamie Dimon said he would seek to claw back added costs of ‘unfair penalties’ as bank surcharge rises in January

Jamie Dimon, the chief executive of JP Morgan, has said he will find ways to offset the chancellor’s tax hike on banks, calling the increase unfair.

Speaking at an event in London on Thursday, Dimon said he would seek to claw back the increase and was “setting an example so that [governments] understand the trade-offs of these unfair penalties”.

The bank surcharge is to rise from 3% to 6% in January as a temporary 15-month measure. It was unclear how JP Morgan, which employs 23,000 people in the UK, would make up for the extra cost.

In comments first reported by the Financial Times, citing people who attended the event, Dimon said he would set “an example” in making up for the added costs, and that the decision was “more in sorrow” than in anger.

Dimon has repeatedly lobbied Labour against raising taxes on banks, and suggested in May that he could scrap plans for a new £3bn tower in the Canary Wharf district of London that is expected to serve as JP Morgan’s UK headquarters.

Shortly after the chancellor, John Healey, announced the policy during his inaugural budget in October, a source familiar with the matter told the Guardian that plans for the 279,000 sq metre (3m sq ft) building were still going ahead. But the bank also said at the time that the policy “lacks principle” for singling the sector out, adding that it operated in a “highly competitive global marketplace”.

JP Morgan also has a sprawling campus in Bournemouth, where it employs more than 5,300 staff who process more than $1tn (£750bn) worth of global transactions each day. Earlier this month, the Guardian reported fears that the advance of artificial intelligence could put their jobs at risk.

Lenders in the UK pay a 28% corporation tax rate, higher than the standard 25%, and the separate surcharge on their UK balance sheets, which is set for the temporary rise. JP Morgan, the biggest bank in the US, expects to pay £120m more in taxes because of the increase, the FT reported.

#128. Violent protests and killings in Bangladesh ahead of exiled Sheikh Hasina’s planned return

Awami League party called for ⁠nationwide protest on Thursday after the government rejected Hasina’s request for passport

Parts of Bangladesh were shut down Thursday as protests by supporters of former prime minister Sheikh Hasina sparked clashes, bomb blasts and arson attacks, with the ancestral home ⁠of former leader Muhammad Yunus set ablaze in the violence.

Hasina’s Awami League party had called for a ​week-long programme of protests and rallies to mobilise support ‌for Hasina’s planned return in December, culminating in a ‌nationwide lockdown on Thursday.

Hasina, who has lived in exile in neighbouring India since she fled Dhaka following violent ⁠protests demanding her resignation in August 2024, has requested a new passport and identity card to facilitate her return. Her supporters are protesting against the government’s rejection of that request.

To deter ⁠violence, 97 platoons of the Bangladesh Border Guard fanned out across seven districts, including Gopalganj and Faridpur, considered to have many Hasina supporters, a spokesperson ⁠said.

Police said former interim leader Yunus’ ancestral home in Chattogram, about 300km (186 miles) south-east of Dhaka, ​was set on fire late on ‌Wednesday.

It is likely that flammable ‌substances were thrown into an unused ground-floor room, police told Reuters, adding that Yunus’ paternal family members, who live on the upper floors, are safe.

Reuters could not immediately reach the Yunus Centre thinktank that speaks on his behalf to seek comment. Yunus and his government, which led Bangladesh after Hasina’s ouster, have faced hostility from her supporters.

During the last 10 days of protests, police have reported dozens of crude bomb blasts and incidents of ‌vehicles being set afire in at least 28 districts, injuring several police officials and members of the public, media have said.

Late on Tuesday, an activist of the ruling Bangladesh Nationalist party (BNP) was allegedly killed by unidentified people during a protest near Hasina’s home district of Gopalganj, police said.

On the same night, a crude bomb exploded outside the Dhaka residence of cultural affairs minister Nitai Roy Chowdhury, and the house of a BNP lawmaker and shops ‌belonging to BNP supporters in Gopalganj were set on fire, police added.

On Wednesday morning, a bus company helper was burnt to death in the southern district of Pirojpur after two buses ​were set on fire, authorities said.

Tuesday marks a year since Hasina was ​sentenced to death by a local court that found her guilty of ordering a deadly crackdown on a student-led uprising against her in 2024. She has lived in exile in neighbouring India since fleeing Dhaka.

On Thursday, authorities detained former education minister Nurul Islam Nahid, who faces charges ⁠over several killings during the 2024 protests, from his house in Dhaka, police told Reuters.

At ​least 37 branches and offices of ​Grameen Bank, set up by Yunus, have been targeted in arson attacks or ​attempted fires since 10 November, ‌with 14 more ​attacked since midnight ​on Tuesday, the bank said.

Awami League leaders, most of them in hiding, could not be reached for comment.

#126. Myanmar committed genocide against Rohingya minority, world’s top court rules

Judgment finds ‘intent to destroy a substantial part of the Rohingya population’ after case seen as critical test for dealing with most serious of state crimes

Myanmar committed genocide against its Rohingya minority during a 2017 military campaign, the world’s highest court has ruled, in a historic and unprecedented decision.

The judgment from the international court of justice (ICJ) – which has never previously found a state responsible for genocide – ends a seven-year legal battle that was seen as a critical global test for dealing with the most serious of all state crimes.

The ICJ president, Yuji Iwasawa, said a majority of nine judges on the 15-seat court found that “the Republic of the Union of Myanmar has committed genocide against the Rohingya”.

The 2016-17 campaign by Myanmar’s military forced more than 700,000 mostly Muslim Rohingya to flee across the border into Bangladesh, arriving with reports of mass killings of adults and children, gang-rapes and burned-down villages.

Nearly a decade later, in a refugee camp in Bangladesh, crowds of Rohingya people watching on outdoor TV screens leaped up in celebration and applause as the final verdict was read out.

One Rohingya survivor, Yousuf Ali, had travelled to the court to hear the verdict. “Today, we have finally been heard, and it is a profound relief,” he said. “This judgment gives hope to our children and the next generation of Rohingya. But our fight is not over.”

Governments had been anxiously awaiting the decision handed down in the Netherlands, where the UN-run court is based, in part because the ruling will set a precedent for genocide claims, notably a high-profile case against Israel.

In what could become a crucial factor for the Gaza case, where Israel claims its mass killings are part of a counter-terrorism operation, judges dismissed Myanmar’s claims of carrying out legitimate military operations against insurgents.

Genocide, defined after the Nazi Holocaust by a 1948 UN convention, is harder to prove than war crimes because it requires evidence of specific “intent” to destroy a national, ethnic, racial or religious group. Iwasawa said during the reading of the decision that the court was “fully convinced that Myanmar committed acts falling under … the genocide convention with an intent to destroy a substantial part of the Rohingya population in Myanmar”.

After the ruling, Britain’s foreign minister, Ed Miliband, said the government was expelling Myanmar’s defence attaché and called “urgently” for a session at the UN security council, the body that has the power to enforce ICJ rulings.

The case was raised by the Gambia, a Muslim-majority state, on behalf of Myanmar survivors. It rested on claims of genocidal statements and “patterns of conduct” that showed attempts to destroy the group during assaults on Rakhine state, including gunning down people as they fled and blocking their return.

In January, the ICJ held closed-door sessions to hear from survivors who had travelled from camps in Bangladesh. More than a million Rohingya still live there, in the world’s largest refugee settlement, and fewer than 600,000 are estimated to remain in Buddhist-majority Myanmar.

Refugees who had fled to Bangladesh told the Guardian in 2017 of a massacre in the village of Tula Toli, where survivors described systematic shooting of civilians, mass graves and the drowning of children in a river. That village, which satellite footage later showed had been burned down, was one of several analysed by rights investigators and built a case for genocide.

During hearings, the Gambian justice minister, Dawda Jallow, told the court the case was not “about esoteric issues of international law”. “It is about real people, real stories and a real group of human beings,” said Jallow. “The Rohingya of Myanmar. They have been targeted for destruction.”

Legal experts see Thursday’s judgment as a key test for sexual violence in genocide assessments, as the convention says the crime can include acts with the intent to “prevent births”. Investigators have said gang-rapes and soldiers’ mutilation of women’s genitals were carried out intentionally and systematically to stop reproduction.

The court appeared to back that assessment, accusing the military of using sexual violence to “physically and mentally impair the willingness and ability of the [Rohingya] population to procreate”, referring to cultural stigma for rape survivors. Myanmar had also imposed other measures to restrict births, the court ruled, such as limits of two children per Rohingya family.

Myanmar has denied carrying out a genocide, claiming its actions targeted a Rohingya militant group that conducted guerrilla-style attacks. Its military responded with massive “clearance operations”.

However, judges rejected Myanmar’s claim that its actions were primarily intended as counter-terrorism operations, or even that they were an attempt to punish or forcibly displace the population but without committing genocide.

The country’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing, who headed the military during the 2016-17 attacks, has a pending arrest warrant from the international criminal court (ICC), which, unlike the ICJ, prosecutes individuals and not states, on allegations of crimes against humanity.

The former general overthrew the elected government of Aung San Suu Kyi in a 2021 coup, triggering a civil war that has left tens of thousands dead. He was installed as civilian president in April after elections that were widely discredited.

While the ICJ does not have the power to enforce rulings, its decisions are considered binding, carry diplomatic weight and can shape international action, including sanctions.

During a reading of the decision that lasted more than two hours in a packed Great Hall of Justice in The Hague, Iwasawa called for Myanmar to provide reparations and restitution. This included allowing the safe return of Rohingya to their homes, returning or replacing their property, and providing physical and mental rehabilitation.

The ruling was also significant as it formally classified the Rohingya as a distinct ethnic group under international law, something they have been denied by Myanmar, which refers to them as Bengalis or foreign migrants.

Reached by phone shortly after he left the courtroom, the ​lead counsel for the case against Myanmar told the Guardian the mood had been emotional in the room as the judgment was read out in front of his legal team, which included Rohingya.

“This is the ICJ recognising their history of discrimination, recognising them as a group, recognising the horrific violence they endured and suffered,” said Arsalan Suleman. “It was a really emotional moment for them and the whole team. We felt the weight of the court’s judgment.”

Israel and its chief ally, the US, have been closely following the Myanmar case, as the decision provides the strongest hint of how judges could assess the Gaza case, brought by South Africa. UN independent investigations, as well as genocide scholars, have already said Israel has committed acts of genocide in Gaza.

A judgment finding Myanmar had breached the convention has been predicted to lead to the US imposing sanctions on ICJ judges, including this year’s Nobel peace prize winner, Navi Pillay.

Washington had considered the Rohingya crisis to be a genocide, based on a decision by the Biden administration. However, the administration of Donald Trump has been on the offensive against international rights bodies, including imposing sanctions on the ICC.

The US state department said in a statement that it would study the ICJ’s decision “to fully assess its implications” and that “the proper interpretation of the genocide convention is of paramount importance, as is preventing politicisation of the ICJ”.

It reiterated US support for the people of Myanmar and said it shared “grave concerns over the continued abuses of human rights against Rohingya Muslims and members of other minority groups”.

The Trump administration of has been implacably opposed to international legal organisations. On Thursday, the president used a trilateral meeting with Russia and China on Thursday to condemn “rogue globalist organisations”. But Trump’s ire has been directed most towards the ICC, a separate body which the administration has vowed to dismantle “brick by brick”, rather than the ICJ.

Two other ICJ cases dealing with atrocities in the 1990s Balkan wars fell short of finding states directly responsible for genocide, although Serbia was found legally responsible for failing to prevent or punish genocide committed by Bosnian Serb troops at the Srebrenica massacre.

Other UN tribunals have established genocide in only a handful of cases since the Holocaust, including Cambodia’s Khmer Rouge rule in the 1970s and the 1994 mass killings in Rwanda.

#125. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#122. ‘Everyone struggled for this justice’: tears in court as Rohingya vindicated in Myanmar genocide ruling

Relief in the immediate aftermath of a landmark ruling, but what happens next is in the hands of governments around the world

There were tears in the halls of The Hague in the Netherlands and an eruption of applause in the refugee camps of Bangladesh as the scattered communities of Rohingya watched the UN’s top court rule the Myanmar military had committed genocide against them.

For almost a decade, the Rohingya have awaited this moment; recognition from the international court of justice (ICJ) that Myanmar in 2017 intentionally tried to destroy the mostly Muslim minority group through a campaign of mass killings and rape. More than 10,000 Rohingya were killed and 700,000 fled to neighbouring Bangladesh.

By the time they spilled into the court’s halls, the relief was evident. A Rohingya community leader threw a big thumbs up as he left the courtroom and others immediately took out their phones to call family in the refugee camps.

Salma, 30, a Rohingya survivor who lost family members in the 2017 genocide, said: “Everyone worked hard and struggled for this justice, for this cause. When they hear it today, everyone will be happy, thank God. I don’t know how to express it or even what to do with myself.”

Another Rohingya present, who cannot be named because of safety concerns, said they cried as the judge went through each of the rulings.

“During the last few minutes I couldn’t even sit down any more, I had to move to the back because I didn’t know how I’d react,” they said. “I saw some of the other Rohingya around me crying. They were so emotional.

“Now the responsibility is with the international governments. It has been declared genocide now and they have to act.”

In Bangladesh, where most of the Rohingya population have lived – in the world’s largest refugee camp – since being forced to flee in 2017, the verdict was translated into the Rohingya language for the crowds who had gathered to watch on screens specially set up for the purpose.

They immediately leapt up, cheering, applauding and shouting gratitude for the Gambia – the faraway African nation that brought the case to the ICJ on behalf of the stateless minority. Many held posters that read “Thank You Gambia” and “Justice = Safe Return”.

“Since we arrived here, we have been waiting and looking forward to this justice,” said Farida Khatun, 50, who has been a refugee in Bangladesh since 2017, when her village, Tula Toli, was targeted by soldiers. The village in north-western Myanmar, near the Bangladesh border, was the scene of a massacre in August of that year, with reports of mass killings and gang rapes.

She said, however, that she does not expect to be able to return home. Tula Toli was completely destroyed by Myanmar’s forces and no longer exists. “Even though today’s verdict makes us happy, my heart still cannot believe that they will take us back in true peace.”

The case has taken seven years to reach this point, with Rohingya researchers working to collect testimony from survivors. The initial hope for justice had faded during the long wait for a ruling. Many were also left deflated during early hearings when the one-time pro-democracy hero Aung San Suu Kyi defended the Myanmar military at The Hague.

Before today’s ruling, some were optimistic, others were prepared to be disappointed, but all said they knew themselves that they were victims of genocide.

The tension was finally released when the court declared Myanmar was responsible for genocide against the Rohingya, highlighting the use of sexual violence and attacks on children alongside the killing of Rohingya who tried to flee violence, which it said meant the violence amounted to more than ethnic cleansing.

The court ordered Myanmar to provide reparations for the Rohingya and end continuing breaches of the genocide convention.

There is now also an international criminal court investigation into the violence of 2017, looking specifically at the crime of mass deportation, and for which prosecutors in 2024 sought an arrest warrant for Myanmar’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing.

With continuing violence against the Rohingya, the possibility of a return to Myanmar, as happened after previous exoduses to Bangladesh in 1978 and the early 1990s, remains unlikely.

But many hope the ICJ ruling will be the start of a process of ensuring justice and lead to real international pressure to ensure their safety in Myanmar.

“We will be able to return to our homeland – with our rights, with our citizenship, and having received our full security. We will be able to return,” says Salma.

#120. US reacts cautiously to Myanmar genocide ruling as EU praises ‘critical’ role of UN’s top court

Reactions to ICJ ruling underline global divisions over court with China raising concerns about judicial overreach

The US has greeted with caution the world’s highest court’s ruling that Myanmar committed genocide against its Rohingya minority, while China has raised concerns of judicial overreach.

Reactions to Thursday’s historic ruling underlined deep international division over the role of the court and similar institutions. The international court of justice (ICJ) had never previously found a state responsible for genocide and the decision could set a precedent for future genocide claims, notably a case against Israel.

The EU hailed a “landmark” judgment from the ICJ, while the UK reaffirmed “full support” for the court and the international legal order. Australia called on Myanmar to comply fully with the judgment and its obligations under international law.

But China, which has offered rare international support for Myanmar’s military rulers since they seized power five years ago, highlighted “issues such as jurisdiction overreach and expanded interpretation”.

“China … believes that such practices are not conducive to the settlement of disputes,” the foreign ministry spokesperson, Guo Jiakun, told a news briefing when asked about the ICJ ruling. “The international community should make joint efforts in this direction to avoid complicating this issue.”

Western nations cut ties with the unstable and poor south-east Asian country after the 2021 coup that ousted a civilian-led government.

Myanmar’s presidential spokesperson said on Friday that the ruling was “unfair”, noting the judges’ nine to six vote. The country’s military rulers have denied carrying out a genocide, claiming their actions targeted a Rohingya militant group that conducted insurgent attacks.

“When we look at the result, not all 15 judges agreed with the decision,” said Khaing Khaing Soe, speaking at a press conference in the capital, Naypyidaw. “So this was not a unanimous decision … I would say it is not fair.”

The US state department said on Thursday that Washington was examining the judgment, while stressing the need to avoid “politicisation” of the ICJ.

Donald Trump’s administration has taken a dim view of international law, vowing to dismantle the international criminal court (ICC), but has yet to question the legitimacy of the ICJ.

The state department reiterated US support for the people of Myanmar and said it shared “grave concerns over the continued abuses of human rights against Rohingya Muslims and members of other minority groups”, noting that the US was the largest single humanitarian donor to the Rohingya humanitarian crisis response.

In Brussels, the EU said Myanmar must fully implement the ICJ’s ruling “without delay” and that “compliance with ICJ’s decisions is not optional, it is an obligation under international law”.

“The European Union holds the ICJ in the highest regard and remains steadfast in its support for its critical role in the peaceful resolution of disputes and the upholding of the rule of law at international level,” an EU spokesperson said in a statement.

“The Rohingya people have endured immense suffering, including displacement and violence, and their plight must not be forgotten. This landmark ruling underscores the imperative of justice for victims and the necessity of upholding international law.”

The UN secretary general, António Guterres, called the ruling “a demonstration of international justice and a reaffirmation that impunity for such horrific acts must not stand”.

The UK expelled Myanmar’s defence attache and called for measures to “ensure the court’s ruling sends a clear message that such crimes cannot be committed with impunity”.

The ruling caps a lengthy legal campaign since August 2017 when Myanmar’s military launched what it called a “clearance campaign” in Rakhine state in response to an attack by a Rohingya insurgent group. Reports rapidly emerged of mass rapes and killings by security forces, as well as the burning of thousands of homes.

Judges rejected Myanmar’s claim that its actions were primarily intended as counter-terrorism operations, or even that they were an attempt to punish or forcibly displace the population but without committing genocide.

The country’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing, who headed the military during the 2016-17 attacks, has a pending arrest warrant from the ICC – which, unlike the ICJ, prosecutes individuals and not states – on allegations of crimes against humanity.

The former general overthrew the elected government of Aung San Suu Kyi, triggering a civil war that has left tens of thousands dead. He was installed as civilian president in April after elections that were widely discredited.

While the ICJ does not have the power to enforce rulings, its decisions are considered binding, carry diplomatic weight and can shape international action, including sanctions.

Under the 1948 convention, to establish that genocide occurred the court had to find that there was an intent to destroy the Rohingya “in whole or in part”.

About 1.2 million Rohingya refugees now live in Bangladesh, primarily in crowded camps across the Cox’s Bazar district, where they endure harsh conditions marked by fire hazards, infectious diseases, monsoon landslides, inadequate sanitation and strict work restrictions.

Judges at the court based in The Hague have also been asked to rule on other allegations of genocide.

South Africa has accused Israel of breaching the genocide convention during its offensive in Gaza, a claim Israel vehemently denies. That case is years from completion. An interim ICJ judgment in 2024 ordered Israel to ensure its forces do not commit acts of genocide against Palestinians in Gaza.

Ukraine also has a case pending against Russia. Shortly after Moscow’s full-scale invasion in February 2022, Kyiv accused Russia of fabricating genocide claims against Ukraine to justify the attack.

During a Berlin press conference with the emir, Merz said, "This strengthens both of us, particularly in future technologies such as artificial intelligence, semiconductors and aviation."

The Qatar-Germany collaboration is aimed at improving security and defense projects that also include defending against cyberattacks, with Merz saying, "We are particularly open to new partnerships in the defense industry."

That cooperation comes as both nations look to beef up defenses amid regional conflicts with global consequences.

Sky TV said it still planned to send a team to the season finale in Abu Dhabi, just across the Strait of Hormuz from Iran, on December 6.

Germany's electricity exports rose in the first three quarters of this year, according to official data released on Friday.

According to the energy association BDEW, Germany has been a net importer of electricity since 2023.

The neighbouring countries Austria, Denmark and the Netherlands, were the most important buyers of German electricity in the first nine months of 2026.

Together, they bought about half of all German electricity exports.

The volume of German electricity exported rose by 25.5% compared with the same period last year, to 48.8 terawatt-hours, while its value increased by 51.3% to €4.3 billion ($4.7 billion).    Electricity imports into Germany fell by 13.3% in volume, but at 49.2 terawatt-hours they remained slightly above exports.

While imported electricity cost an average of €116 per megawatt-hour, 39% more than a year earlier, exported electricity from Germany averaged only €87 per megawatt-hour, some 20.6% more than a year earlier.

With a high share of renewable energy sources, wind and solar, Germany tends to offer electricity when supply is high and prices are low, but in times of shortage it has to pay higher prices for imported electricity.

Lufthansa's parent company Lufthansa Group had 67,100 employees in Germany by the end of last year. In the letter seen by news agencies, Lufthansa said financial struggles were the reason behind the planned cuts.

"Lufthansa is currently not competitive in terms of its cost structure, efficiency and productivity," the letter said, while pointing out that the company operated at a €180 million ($197 million) loss in the first nine months of this year.

In addition, Lufthansa Airlines is now in its third straight year of being unprofitable.

In regards to this year, Lufthansa has faced rising fuel costs spurred by the US-Israeli war with Iran, further complicating its financial outlook.

Leaked documents raise new questions about Deutsche Bank's dealings in Russia up to Moscow's invasion of Ukraine, adding to scrutiny over the bank's risk controls and past controversies.

#118. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

Nikhil Gupta admitted to plotting with Indian government official to hire hitman to murder dual US-Canadian citizen

An Indian ⁠man was sentenced to more ⁠than 19 ​years in US prison on Friday after admitting to orchestrating a failed Indian government-backed plot to kill a Sikh separatist in New York ⁠City, in connection with what US and Canadian authorities have called a broader effort to target Indian dissidents.

Nikhil Gupta, 55, pleaded guilty in February to murder-for-hire, conspiracy ⁠to commit murder-for-hire and conspiracy to commit money laundering.

The discovery of alleged ‌assassination plots against Sikh separatists in the US and Canada has tested relations with India, which has ‌denied involvement in such plots.

“I’m ​sorry to Mr ‌Pannun, I’m sorry ​to the communities I hurt, ​I’m sorry to my own family,” Gupta said.

Pannun told the court that he was grateful to the US for prosecuting Gupta and said he would keep advocating for Sikh sovereignty.

“Self-determination for my people and my homeland, Punjab, is my lifetime goal,” he said.

#112. Israel delays building contracts for West Bank settlement after UK sanctions

Attorney general says E1 settlement decision should not be taken by caretaker government led by Netanyahu

Israel has postponed issuing contracts for the construction of a large settlement on the West Bank seen as likely to render a two-state solution unviable, days after the UK imposed sanctions against building firms operating in the area.

Israeli diplomats insisted the move was not in response to the measures from Downing Street. Gali Baharav-Miara, the attorney general, said any decision on settlement expansion should not be taken by the caretaker government led by Benjamin Netanyahu but by whatever new administration was formed.

She ordered the Israel land authority and ministry of housing not to open the tender process. Israeli diplomats suggested the UK foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, had acted prematurely on Monday in sanctioning the eight construction firms. But UK diplomats believe the growing political pressure on Israel over settlements, including from US Democrats, is having an impact.

The deferral puts the E1 settlement expansion in the mix for any coalition negotiations. The leader of the Change bloc, Gadi Eisenkot, seen as Israel’s most likely next prime minister, has been clear in opposing violent settler activity, as has the newly elected Knesset speaker, Matan Kahana, an ally of Eisenkot.

Eisenkot has also opposed the E1 settlement expansion, describing it as a mistake likely to alienate international opinion. He said of the plan: “I recall that Netanyahu himself opposed this for a long time, and due to coalition pressure, he gave in and approved it.”

Miliband has said it would mark the death knell of a future Palestinian state. The UK, along with a group of European countries, has recently recognised Palestine, and the preservation of a two-state solution has been described as the north star of Labour policy towards the region.

The construction tender was published for more than 3,401 housing units in the area. At the beginning of November, the Israel Defense Forces began demolishing homes in a Bedouin community within the E1 area.

Israel has already enforced the downgrading of the UK consulate in East Jerusalem as a reprisal for Miliband’s decision in September to try to end trade links with the illegal settlements.

#111. At COP31, conflict over climate science slows action on global warming

Attacks on climate science and disinformation risk delaying key measures at COP31 in Antalya. As the UN climate talks draw to a close, how will that affect efforts to come to an agreement?

The annual UN climate conference is the place where countries negotiate the world's plans to limit global warming and adapt to its consequences — and who will pay for climate action as well as damage from storms, floods and droughts.

But this year at COP31 in Antalya, an ongoing dispute over the timing of a key assessment of global warming is risking the scientific foundations of global climate action, say observers.

"We are extremely concerned about the situation," said a diplomat with the European delegation who could not speak on the record, adding that it came down to the future of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), the world's leading body of climate scientists.

The IPCC's scientific assessments, published every six to seven years, provide the basis for major international climate agreements, including the 2015 Paris Agreement. The IPCC began evaluating the latest climate science in 1988 and its most recent report was published in 2023.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Researchers should have actually already started work on the next edition but the last plenary session was put on hold in October at the urging of Russia, Iran, Saudi Arabia and Kenya.

"The IPCC finds itself in a difficult situation," a spokesperson from the German Environment Ministry told DW at COP. "We stand ready to help resolve this issue, so that the panel can continue its work. To that end, we are holding many discussions with various countries to explore possible solutions."

At the core of the dispute is the question of whether future UN climate talks base their negotiations on the latest scientific knowledge — or not.

That's especially relevant for the next "global stocktake," due in 2028, a report card on the world's climate efforts, which will shape action plans for countries around the world to lower planet-heating greenhouse gas emissions.

The European Union, Brazil, the United Kingdom, small island states and several other countries especially at risk of the effects of climate change have called for the IPCC's seventh assessment report to be published in 2028, so it can be used to inform next steps for climate action. The report cover climate protection and impact.

"It's much more difficult without these scientific reports. Otherwise, we're just talking to ourselves or relying on information that's no longer current," said a delegation source.

But Russia, Iran, India and Saudi Arabia want the IPCC report postponed until 2029, after the global stocktake.

The official reason: the tight timeline would be detrimental to the quality of the research, and could hinder the participation of researchers and governments representing developing countries.

Disagreements over budget issues have also held up the IPCC's work. Among other expenses, the funds are used to pay the travel arrangements for the hundreds of volunteer scientists, especially those from poorer countries, to attend the IPCC sessions.

"No budget means no more IPCC," said a member from another delegation, also speaking off the record.

It hasn't come to that point yet, but without planning certainty the whole process risks being delayed — along with the climate negotiations that depend on this knowledge.

Researchers with the IPCC, however, have said the timeline is attainable. "We have no time to lose," said an open letter released in October signed by more than 100 researchers from 36 countries — including India and China. "We stand committed to continue working to deliver the [seventh assessment report] in a manner that is rigorous, timely and inclusive."

But while the dispute over the timeline won't alter the scientific findings themselves, it could delay their political impact.

In its pre-COP communications, France accused the countries delaying the report's publication schedule of attempting to "obstruct science in order to lower the ambition of the global stocktake."

The IPCC isn't a stranger to attempts at political persuasion: at past climate conferences, delegates from many countries have regularly negotiated the exact phrasing of the summary reports intended for policymakers.

Speaking at COP31 on November 17, EU Climate Commissioner Wopke Hoekstra said it was "fundamental to protect the integrity of climate science and the role of the IPCC as the source of best available science."

Although there is no vote on the IPCC at COP31, it's possible that its significance and a timeline for the next report will be included in the final declaration of the climate talks, expected this weekend.

The IPCC has been warning for years that disinformation is hindering global climate action. And the struggle for information and control over the narrative in Antalya hasn't just been limited to the IPCC.

DW was able to obtain a copy of a presentation which included misleading statements on the COP's electrification targets, one of the key goals at this year's talks. Turkey has proposed boosting global electrification to 35% by 2035, to reduce the world's reliance on planet-warming fossil fuels.

The contents of the presentation, said to have been leaked to some states in advance, was confirmed to DW by two countries. Its origin, however, could not be verified.

The presentation portrays electrification as a business venture undertaken by wealthy countries at the expense of poorer nations. The International Energy Agency, however, has calculated that developing countries could save around $100 billion (€90 billion) in fossil fuel imports by 2035 through electrification.

The presentation also gives the impression that all countries must meet fixed electrification targets. In fact, according to the UN, the 35% target represents a global goal, with individual countries free to determine their own national pathways.

This article was originally written in German.

→ Baca artikel asli

Local sources told the Anadolu news agency that the driver had first struck three young men, then returned and ran over five others who had gathered to help them.

#106. Israeli tanks cross ‘yellow line’ boundary into Hamas-controlled central Gaza

First incursion across boundary since ceasefire deal comes as Eisenkot seeks to form government without Netanyahu

The Israeli military crossed into Hamas-controlled central Gaza on Wednesday in its first ground operation across the “yellow line” boundary since a fragile ceasefire was agreed last October, forcing dozens of displaced Palestinians to flee their tents.

Israeli tanks rolled along the Salah al-Din road, Gaza’s main north-south route, a day after the US-backed Board of Peace, a body overseeing the ceasefire, accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities.

An Israeli military spokesperson said troops were carrying out an operation targeting “Hamas infrastructure” west of the yellow line in central Gaza.

The operation lasted about 24 hours and on Thursday the military said it had been “completed”.

The IDF ​has not commented extensively on the operation, which came as the Israeli opposition politician, Gadi Eisenkot, has been seeking to form a government without the caretaker prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, who has built his political reputation on promises to keep Israel safe.

A Gaza security source told AFP there was “intense Israeli artillery shelling” north of Nuseirat refugee camp and reported Israeli military vehicles in central Gaza “amid ongoing gunfire and airstrikes since midday”.

Hamas condemned the Israeli raid and the forced displacement of Palestinians as “a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement” and a dangerous escalation. The Israeli military did not immediately respond to Hamas’s statement.

Residents reported hearing tank fire in the area. Palestinian medics said one person was killed in an Israeli airstrike near the site of the raid.

After the statement from the IDF that it had concluded the tank raid to dismantle a suspected Hamas tunnel, the defence ministry’s body in charge of civilian affairs in Palestinian territories, Cogat, said Palestinians would again be free to move in the targeted areas near al-Bureij and Nuseirat refugee camps.

In separate incidents on Wednesday, Palestinian medics said two people were killed by Israeli fire, one in Deir al-Balah and the other in Gaza City. The Israeli military said it was unaware of any incident in Deir al-Balah and had struck a militant in Gaza City.

Israel has stepped up its attacks on Hamas, which it says has been regrouping and rearming. Hamas has accused Israel of trying to derail the US-brokered ceasefire agreed in October 2025.

Israeli strikes have continued since the ceasefire, and Hamas remains armed.

Last week, an Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in southern Gaza killed six people, including a surgeon and a disabled man, and wounded 28 others, according to the hospital director.

The body overseeing Donald Trump’s plan to end the war in Gaza has accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities, leaving Palestinians in dire conditions and the ceasefire at risk of collapse.

In its second report to the UN security council since the truce began 13 months ago, the Trump-led Board of Peace said the gap between what was agreed and what was happening on the ground remained the biggest threat to the deal. The report, seen by Reuters, warned that “a ceasefire that delivers nothing visible cannot be sustained indefinitely”.

Gaza’s health authorities say at least 1,600 Palestinians have been killed since the ceasefire came into effect. The Israeli military says five of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

Palestinian authorities say more than 74,000 people have been killed in Gaza since Israel launched its military campaign in response to the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, in which about 1,200 people were killed.

In June, an ⁠independent UN inquiry determined that Israel was continuing to commit genocide by deliberately targeting Palestinian children. A previous report by the commission in September 2025 found that Israel had committed genocide in Gaza, and Israeli officials, including the prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, incited those acts. Netanyahu is separately wanted by the international criminal court (ICC) for war crimes.

#105. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#103. Cumin-laced fizzy sodas are giving cola giants a tough fight in India

Lahori Zeera will close this year with $115-120m in revenue

It began as an impromptu kitchen experiment.

Saurabh Munjal, Nikhil Doda and Saurabh Bhutna - three cousins in India's north-western Punjab state - were messing about at home post dinner one evening, when Doda casually brewed up a tangy cumin-flavoured fizzy soda using regular pantry ingredients.

They enjoyed its earthy, piquant taste so much that they decided to explore options to sell it commercially.

Cumin-laced sodas had always been sold at street-side stalls in India, but it was a broken market of local companies. Not many had built a carbonated beverage brand around the flavour.

The trio persuaded their families to invest some capital and launched Lahori Zeera in 2017. Zeera is the Hindi name for cumin.

Almost a decade on, the drink has become a bit of a sensation in India, challenging the entrenched monopoly of global cola giants, especially in the all-important mass-market ten-rupee ($0.11; Â£0.078) beverage category - it will close this year with revenues of $115-120m.

Its popularity reflects a growing consumer preference for distinctly Indian flavours and drinks perceived to be more healthy, say analysts.

Lahori Zeera is one of several cumin-flavoured soda brands that have mushroomed across the country over the past decade.

There's Jeeru from Mumbai-based Xotik Frujus, Campa Jeera from billionaire Mukesh Ambani-owned Campa Cola and at least half-a-dozen other variants from regional players including Fizz Jeera Masala from House of Bindu.

Bindu launched their cumin soda nearly 25 years ago, but sales were flat until some years ago, according to Megha Shankar, a director with the company.

"Now it is the fastest growing category among carbonated beverages," Shankar told the BBC. "Its contribution to our revenues has doubled from 20% to 40% in the last five years."

Soaring demand for the product has pushed House of Bindu to widen its footprint across the country, after being largely based in southern India for over two decades.

Parle Agro, external, one of India's oldest consumer brands, also rolled out its own cumin soda brand Dhishoom nationally last year after exclusively selling it in rural markets since 2012.

Online orders for these fizzy drinks, meanwhile, were up 900% this summer according to a report from one of India's online delivery companies.

Cumin is a warm, earthy spice widely used in Indian cuisine

The cumin frenzy is reshaping India's non-alcoholic beverages market, which was valued at $32 billion in 2024 and projected to double by 2033.

According to a report from Kotak Mutual Fund earlier this year, Lahori Zeera, Campa and other smaller challengers - now dubbed as India's "ethnic beverages market" - collectively doubled their share of India's carbonated soft-drinks market to around 15% by September last year, while the combined market share of Coca-Cola and PepsiCo fell from roughly 93% to 85%.

"This is a "Jio moment" for beverages. Just as telecom incumbents bled market share when data prices crashed, beverage incumbents are bleeding volume," the Kotak report said, drawing an analogy to the disruption caused by the entry of Reliance-owned Jio Telecom in India's telecoms market in 2016.

"In global markets,â¯establishedâ¯duopolies rarely lose more than 1-2% share annually; anâ¯8% swingâ¯in less than a year is a structural disruption."

Saurabh Munjal, CEO of Archian Foods which manufactures Lahori Zeera, told the BBC that the company has "always been in a situation where we are sold out each year. We've doubled our capacity to 1.2 million bottles per day last year and are still unable to meet demand".

For decades, the colas have had a dominant presence in India, appealing to the aspirational part of the population as lifestyle products that were cool to consume.

With millions of dollars in marketing budgets splashed out on billboards, Bollywood stars and cricket championships, they sold a very western idea of hydration to India, says Munjal.

But home-grown brands are now mounting a serious challenge with a combination of smart distribution, attractive pricing and a turn towards nostalgic Indian flavours such as cumin, raw mango, kokum fruit and tamarind.

Munjal says that for long, there has been a tendency among consumer brands in India to either westernise Indian products or "Indianise" western products. But with Lahori Zeera, which uses the tagline "apna desi thanda", meaning India's own cold drink, his desire is to change that.

Shankar says her brand has also recently launched a zero-sugar version of their cumin soda to capture the growing market for healthier non-cola drinks.

Global giants like Coca-Cola and Pepsi have long held a monopoly on India's carbonated beverages market

This success notwithstanding, sustaining this winning trajectory will not be easy for these ethnic beverage brands, say analysts. The very factors that propelled their rise could also limit growth.

"The million-dollar question is how big can the cumin-flavoured drink market become. And I suspect it won't become as big as the colas," Rajiv Agarwal, a professor at Mumbai-based SP Jain Institute of Management, who advises family businesses on strategies to succeed against multi-national corporations, told the BBC.

The 10-rupee price point, which accounts for the bulk of sales volumes in India's cumin-flavoured carbonated drinks market, is another double-edged sword.

While it has driven up sales, the soaring cost of plastic, packaging, sugar and other raw material amid the global energy shock could start to hit margins, which are already very thin.

Raising prices, meanwhile, could affect volumes, forcing brands to further reduce bottle sizes, something the cola companies had resorted to a few years ago.

Then there is always the threat of bigger, deep-pocketed players coming in to disrupt the category.

"Competition is extremely aggressive," says Agarwal. And the established global players are better organised in terms of systems, processes, distribution channels and bottling arrangements.

Coca Cola, which had acquired the cumin-flavoured Rimzim brand in the 1990s, is strengthening its play in this category and reportedly doubled production last year.

But the challenges haven't deterred the ambitions of the homegrown players.

"Indian flavours need to find a global spotlight," says Munjal, who is looking to tap the global market in the coming years.

"For generations, we've grown up drinking colas of the world, but I hope the next generations will drink the zeeras of India."

#102. Myanmar ruling gives new relevance to genocide convention and is ‘full of messages’ for Israel

Hailed as ‘a rare moment of justice’, judges’ decision sets out a roadmap for assessing the question of intent

The international court of justice (ICJ) verdict making Myanmar the first country in history to be found guilty of committing genocide does not just deliver justice for Rohingya victims. It also gives the 1948 genocide convention new relevance in governing the actions of nation states.

In their nine votes to six majority ruling, the panel of judges in The Hague set out a detailed roadmap for how the convention should be applied to real-world situations in which a government is accused of committing genocide against a whole population group, especially when it comes to assessing the question of intent.

The lengthy judgment will have been read particularly carefully by Israel, the only other country currently before the ICJ accused of genocide.

It is also relevant to Germany, which has been brought before the court by Nicaragua on the accusation it has facilitated and failed to prevent genocide in Gaza.

“I think it’s a judgment that brings the genocide convention alive,” said Philippe Sands, a leading human rights lawyer who was part of the Gambian legal team. “It gives the convention practical, real meaning. And if the alternative view had been taken, the convention would essentially become a dead letter.”

Amnesty International’s secretary general, Agnès Callamard, called the ruling “a rare moment of justice for survivors, victims and their families”. But it was also an assertion of humanitarian law in an increasingly lawless world.

“This is a good day for international law. We needed it,” said Dr Iva Vukušić, an assistant professor in international History at Utrecht University and a war crimes expert. “It is really important, clarifying a lot of law, and that’s going to be very helpful going forward.”

There will be a spillover from this ruling to its sister tribunal in The Hague, the international criminal court (ICC), providing a benchmark for when genocide charges could be made against individual perpetrators.

The Trump administration, instinctively hostile to international legal institutions, is also watching critically. The US ambassador to the UN told the ICJ before this judgment not to “lower the legal threshold for proving genocide” lest it be “weaponised” against US and allied soldiers.

The court did not directly lower the standard for demonstrating genocide, and the Gambia – which brought the case to The Hague – did not ask it to.

The judges kept the existing benchmark for judging genocide intent as the “only reasonable inference” of Myanmar’s actions and referred extensively to prior case law. But it went a long way towards clarifying how that standard should be applied in assessing atrocity crimes committed by states.

Before this case, only Serbia had faced the charge of genocide at the ICJ, in cases brought by Croatia and Bosnia and Herzegovina. In the Bosnian ruling in 2007, Belgrade was found to have failed to prevent the 1995 genocide in Srebrenica, but in both cases it was cleared of committing the genocide directly.

The new ICJ judgment finds the Myanmar military directly responsible for genocide against the Rohingya, a Muslim minority targeted in a ferocious counterinsurgency campaign that reached its peak in 2017.

In assessing the Myanmar campaign, the ICJ looked not just at the death toll of about 10,000 in assessing the scale of the atrocities, but at the total Rohingya population targeted in northern Rakhine state, 815,000 people. Significantly, it cast its net wider than just the death toll, considering mental damage done to the survivors, particularly victims of sexual violence, and the impact on reproduction in the Rohingya community.

Most importantly, the ICJ judgment provided a guide to interpreting the standard set by Serbian cases, in which a finding of genocide could be made only if there were not other plausible motives for mass killing.

In this judgment, the court ruled that Myanmar could have had other motives, such as the desire to displace or punish the Rohingya population, but that those motives could exist alongside genocidal intent. The judges said genocidal intent was the “only reasonable inference” from the “totality” of evidence.

In justifying that conclusion the judges pointed to several factors that international law experts say are relevant to the Gaza case, which is now hearing written pleadings and is expected to take at least another three years.

The common factors include the targeting of children, which the court described as striking at the “foundation” of the Rohingya as a group – the ICJ said up to 2,109 Rohingya children under the age of five had been killed, while more than 5,000 Palestinian children under five years old have been killed in the Gaza war.

Another incriminating factor cited by the court was the “bulldozing and large-scale terrain clearance” after the destruction of villages, followed by “rapid construction” including security installations, which has been the hallmark of Israeli army occupation of over 60% of the Gaza Strip.

“It’s full of messages for Israel,” said William Schabas, an international law professor and expert on genocide at Middlesex University. “There are obvious differences, of course, the evidence will be different, but if you exchanged the word Myanmar with Israel, you wouldn’t have to change much in the judgment.”

The fact that there were six dissenting voices on the bench suggested that substantial parts of the majority’s reasoning were not beyond dispute, and lawyers involved in the case cautioned that it did not necessarily mean that the same bench would find Israel guilty of genocide in Gaza. But the same lawyers added that the opposite was almost certainly true. If this case had failed, the case brought by South Africa against Israel would have been all but over.

#100. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.
