#99. More climate money for Africa? Europe says use what's already pledged

Africa is pushing for adaptation finance to be tripled by 2035, from an annual $40bn (Â£30bn)

In Uganda's northern Karamoja region, Elijah Lobur and his family now eat just one meal a day.

A severe drought that began in April has destroyed crops, while food brought in from elsewhere has become increasingly unaffordable.

"Seven months without rain has brought us to this point where we now live in hunger," the 44-year-old farmer, who lives with his grandmother, wife and five children, told the BBC.

"Even some of the wild plants that we used to eat have withered and dried up."

Local officials say more than 90% of crops have failed in the region, where agriculture is largely rain-fed.

Further south, in Malawi, Mathews Nkhambala has experienced the devastating effects of flooding.

In April 2024, barely a year after Tropical Cyclone Freddy devastated the country's southern region, floods displaced people in Chikwawa district.

Nkhambala fled to higher ground with his wife and four children as floodwaters swept through his village.

"As we reached a safe height, we saw our house swept away right in front of our eyes. Everything I had was gone," he told the BBC.

He lost a harvest of 15 bags of sorghum and part of his farmland. His children were so traumatised that they stopped going to school.

"They refused to go to school for many months. They were so disturbed," he recalled.

After relocating and trying to rebuild his life, Nkhambala is now facing another climate challenge - this time a lack of rain.

"This time we fear there may be no harvest. I have no clue what I will do."

A government assessment after Cyclone Freddy found that Malawi had experienced more than 50 disasters linked to storms, floods, landslides and droughts since 1980, affecting millions of people.

While Uganda and Malawi are suffering the effects of climate change and their governments are asking for assistance adapting to the new global weather patterns, they are among 23 African countries yet to submit National Adaptation Plans (NAPs) to the UN climate convention secretariat.

These are the plans that detail how climate change has affected them and the risks they face, and so help make a case for what assistance they need. Without them, experts say, it becomes difficult to secure funding.

"Our region has experienced cyclical droughts for decades, but there has been no action plan to tackle these climate impacts," said Milton Lorpia, who works for Warrior Squad Foundation in Karamoja region of Uganda.

"If only such a plan were in place and implemented, it would certainly have helped us."

Mathews Nkhambala and his wife fear their harvest may fail this year

The African Group of Negotiators at the ongoing UN climate summit (COP31) in Turkey is pushing for adaptation finance to be tripled by 2035, from an annual $40bn (Â£30bn) baseline that it says developed countries committed to providing by 2025. The interpretation of that commitment remains contested.

The proposal was not accepted for formal negotiations at the summit, although African negotiators have continued to push for it.

The funding is intended to help vulnerable countries prepare for and withstand climate change-induced extreme weather, such as droughts, floods and wildfires.

But the European Union, one of the largest providers of climate finance, says the priority should be ensuring existing commitments reach vulnerable countries rather than setting new funding targets.

"We haven't come to the table with additional monies or additional capital. It's about what we have committed already and ensuring it's deployed," Darragh O'Brien, who leads the EU delegation at COP31 on behalf of the Irish presidency of the Council of the EU, told a press conference.

He acknowledged that developing countries faced difficulties accessing available funding, saying the process needed to be improved.

Wopke Hoekstra, the European Commission's climate commissioner, also argued that the summit should focus on delivering existing pledges rather than making new ones.

"If you are a vulnerable nation in the Pacific or one of the LDCs (Least Developed Countries) and the money is in the bank but does not land on the ground then it is effectively meaningless," he said.

'It can kill us': Kenyans fear impact of El NiÃ±o with floods predicted

Hoekstra said the EU had provided $30bn of the $100bn in climate finance committed by developed countries in recent years, adding that other wealthy nations should contribute more.

The scale of the need is substantial. According to the UN's Adaptation Gap Report published last year, sub-Saharan Africa requires about $51bn annually in adaptation finance, while the Middle East and North Africa needs $112bn.

Globally, developing countries will need about $300bn annually by 2035, according to the UN's latest report. Yet they received only about 10% of that amount from developed countries in 2024.

Climate-related loss and damage in Africa is projected to cost $440bn between 2020 and 2030, according to Nairobi-based think tank Power Shift Africa.

Under the 2015 Paris climate agreement, developed countries are required to provide financial support to developing nations, including for climate adaptation.

But why have so many African countries failed to prepare plans that could help them identify their needs and build resilience?

"If you look closely, the countries that are yet to submit their NAPs are already dealing with multiple crises, including debt distress, economic pressures and other issues," said David Abudho, climate justice lead at the aid agency, Oxfam.

"Their governments' ability to invest in climate adaptation is limited."

Sheila Apiny, regional climate justice adviser at ActionAid, said the lack of plans reflected a wider shortage of financial and technical support.

"The absence of NAPs in so many countries is, in itself, evidence of an adaptation finance and capacity gap," she argued.

She said this created a vicious cycle - countries lack support to prepare their plans, while not having the plans makes it harder to secure funding for climate resilience.

"When countries lack adequate adaptation planning and, more importantly, the resources to implement those plans, governments are forced into a cycle of responding to disasters instead of preventing or reducing their impacts," said Amos Wemanya, senior climate adviser at Power Shift Africa.

"Instead of investing in early warning systems, climate-resilient agriculture, water security and flood protection, scarce public resources are repeatedly diverted towards emergency relief and reconstruction. This costs more over time and leaves communities increasingly vulnerable."

Under the 2015 Paris climate agreement, developed countries are required to provide financial support to developing nations, including for climate adaptation

African negotiators acknowledge that adaptation plans help countries prioritise investments and attract funding, but argue that their absence should not be used to deny them financial support.

"Of course, having a comprehensive NAP helps countries prioritise investments and prepare proposals for climate finance," said Mahlet Eyassu Melkie, a senior strategic adviser to Ethiopia's COP32 presidency.

"But Africa's adaptation financing needs are based on scientific assessments, climate vulnerability, national priorities and regional estimates â not simply on whether every country has submitted a particular planning document."

The UN Development Programme, which has helped more than 45 countries prepare their plans, said NAPs can help governments mobilise international support and coordinate climate action.

Fred Njehu, a Pan-African political strategist at Greenpeace Africa, said their absence could nevertheless create significant obstacles.

"It is fair to acknowledge that missing adaptation plans act as a major functional barrier that severely bottlenecks the speed, volume and quality of international funding a country can access," he said.

But having a plan does not guarantee the money needed to implement it.

"Even countries that have completed their NAPs have not received money anywhere near what they require for implementation of their adaptation plans," Apiny said.

Kenya offers an example of how adaptation measures can help communities cope with climate pressures.

The walls of the sand dams store water underground when the rivers dry up

In the country's arid and semi-arid south-east, concrete sand dams slow the flow of seasonal rivers, allowing sand to accumulate and store water underground. Communities can access it during dry periods using hand pumps or by scooping it out.

UK charity Sand Dams Worldwide and its local partner, the Africa Sand Dam Foundation, said they had built about 1,300 such dams, improving access to water as droughts become more frequent and intense.

But many vulnerable communities elsewhere on the continent are still waiting for similar interventions.

Back in drought-hit Karamoja, Lobur wonders how different things might have been if farmers had access to small-scale irrigation schemes.

"We do have some underground water sources in some places. If there were proper plans and programmes, we could have tapped them to grow food, but we are having to live in hunger instead," Lobur said.

"My grandmother keeps reminding us how she lost eight children during a famine-like situation in 1982, and we all fear we might perish like that too."

#98. Iraq university’s removal of male professors teaching women sparks weeks of protests

Al-Qadisiyah University’s move a sign of the country’s slide into a Taliban-like gender segregation, say activists

The removal of male professors teaching female students at a university in southern Iraq has sparked protests and been criticised by activists as a Taliban-like gender segregation policy.

As many as 400 women are estimated to have joined protests over the past two weeks calling for the return of their professors at Al-Qadisiyah University in a move that appears to have surprised its administrators.

The university said the move was necessary to “prevent the mixing” of men and women and had been requested by the families of female students. But students said their families had known since before they enrolled that there were men on the teaching staff and supported their daughters’ protest.

Students said university bosses had mocked their protests and refused to discuss their concerns about the sudden loss of their academic supervisors. On social media, commentators also accused the women of seeking to keep male professors to “establish immoral relationships with them”.

“My classmates and I went to demonstrate, and the university president, Rahi al-Yasiri, blocked our path with his bodyguards, who then began making fun of us. He ordered them to take photographs of our faces, in an attempt to intimidate us and punish us later,” said Huda, 22, one of the female students.

Huda said the students tried to explain how men and women still worked together in society in Iraq. “Aren’t there doctors who treat both men and women? Why then do you prevent male professors from teaching us, too?” In response, Huda said Al-Yasiri replied: “Even if you hit your heads against the wall, I will not bring back the professors.”

The students said a large number of them were tied to graduation projects and research supervised by the professors. They said they had grown accustomed to their professors’ teaching methods, who also knew their students’ strengths and weaknesses, which made replacing them at this stage more complicated.

Female students said they had been told by the university that if they were not happy with the change, they should drop out.

An Iraqi-British feminist researcher, Balsam Mustafa, from the University of Cardiff, said the female students were “treated as if they are minors without the right to object to decisions affecting their academic lives”.

Mustafa said the removal of the male professors was a part of a worrying trend in women’s rights in the country, with “growing discussion about women’s clothing and chastity reflected in the celebration of female students who wear the abaya”.

An Iraqi lawyer and human rights activist, Israa Salman, said the restrictions on women was a “societal catastrophe”.

“Attempts are being made to suppress women’s talents and turn Iraqi society – a society that has produced remarkable minds and expertise, with women playing pioneering roles in many fields – into something resembling Kabul under Taliban rule.

“This contradicts Iraq’s constitution and the international treaties and conventions the country has signed and ratified,” said Salman.

#96. Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Shark Tank investor Kevin O'Leary is behind a major AI data centre project in Canada that has so far been embraced by legislators.

As Americans have grown increasingly sour on artificial intelligence (AI) data centre development, Canada's industry leaders are sensing an opportunity.

"Data centres in Canada looking pretty appealing right now," Lucy Hargreaves, CEO of non-profit policy group Build Canada, wrote on X the day after American voters passed 40 of 41 local ballot initiatives aimed at limiting or preventing data centre development in this month's midterm elections.

While the number of proposed AI data centres in the US dwarfs that of its northern neighbour, in recent months Canada is being increasingly pitched as a hub for these so-called "hyperscale" projects.

Those in favour argue that Canada's abundant land, natural gas and cold climate make it uniquely positioned to address some of the problems facing US AI infrastructure, from data centres' proximity to populated areas to noise pollution and strain on electricity grids.

"Some of the capital that would have been deployed to build data centres in the US is now more available for Canada," Hargreaves told the BBC in an interview.

"The question really is whether or not we want to go for that."

Unlike in the US, pushback to these projects in Canada has been muted - at least so far. Many are going ahead without significant disruptions, and legislators have openly embraced them without major political consequences.

At the heart of Canada's AI data centre boom is Alberta, where 90% of all currently proposed sites are set to be built.

The western Canadian province, rich with natural gas deposits, has pitched itself as "North America's destination of choice" for AI data centres, including by offering a "concierge" service for investors to help build these projects at "unparalleled speed".

One of those investors is Kevin O'Leary, the Canadian businessman, TV personality and an ally of Donald Trump, who announced plans this year to build two large AI data centres - one in Alberta and one in the US state of Utah.

His planned sites, both named Wonder Valley, are being received in very different ways on either side of the border.

Residents in Utah have organised a fierce opposition campaign., external The pushback led outgoing state senator Stuart Adams to demand in June that O'Leary scale down the project by 75%, external, from 40,000 to 13,000 acres.

Financing for the Utah Wonder Valley data centre is still underway, and it is unclear when - and if - shovels will hit the ground on the project.

The proposed 7,000-acre, 7.5-gigawatt Alberta Wonder Valley project, meanwhile, has received strong backing from both the provincial government and the local municipality.

The project is billed as "off-grid", external, relying on its own power system fuelled by natural gas sourced from the region. In April, the Alberta government exempted the project from an environmental impact assessment, external, arguing it was unnecessary, though it will still need to obtain permits prior to construction.

The project was met with only limited opposition at a local town hall earlier this year. One resident, Dan Harms, told Bloomberg in June , externalthat he saw the economic benefits of the Wonder Valley data centre and that it was being built "out in the middle of nowhere".

In an interview with BBC News, Reeve Ryan Ratzlaf, of Greenview Municipality in north-western Alberta, said the project was the "perfect fit" for his rural community, which had been looking for an industrial tenant to fill a resource-rich parcel of land for 13 years.

Some community members have expressed concern about the amount of water needed to cool the proposed site annually, which is projected to be six million cubic metres, or about the size of 2,400 Olympic-size swimming pools.

Ratzlaf argued the amount was a drop compared to the annual flow of the nearby Smoky River, from which the centre would pull water. More broadly, he said he believed that economic benefits to the region outweighed potential risks.

"It's a giant warehouse with computer equipment inside," he said, arguing that it was less risky than petrochemical plants that could have been built in its place.

Speaking to BBC News, the CEO overseeing both O'Leary projects, Paul Palandjian, said the two were proceeding at an "equal" pace.

He acknowledged it had become more of a "hot topic" in the US than in Alberta. But there are signs public anger towards AI data centres in Alberta has grown in recent months.

Recent public information sessions on provincial AI data centre plans were attended by some furious residents.

"If no one wants this, why are you pushing forward with this?" one resident asked in an August town hall reported on by the Canadian Press, external, held shortly after Alberta announced that Meta would build a C$13bn ($9bn; Â£6.8bn) AI data centre in the province.

Sturgeon Lake Cree Nation has also recently sued the government over its decision to override the environmental assessment for O'Leary's Wonder Valley data centre, labelling it "irrational" in court filings.

This week, the Alberta government proposed legislation that would put restrictions on where data centres could be built in a bid to ease some concerns.

There are also signs that public cooperation may be fading elsewhere in the country.

On Saturday, hundreds of protesters gathered in Canada's Atlantic province of Nova Scotia against a proposed AI data centre, external that will be used by technology giant Anthropic.

But experts say the US backlash offers a cautionary tale for Canada as it tries to capitalise on the AI boom.

"AI is generally not very popular. This is both true in Canada and the United States," noted Sean Mullin, a senior fellow at the University of Toronto and former adviser to Canada's prime minister on the national sovereign AI compute strategy.

"I think we could experience some of the same backlashes if we're not thoughtful about it," he said.

BBC went to Arizona before the midterms to understand the data centre boom in the US

So far, lawmakers in Canada have stood firm.

The Carney government, which has made reducing Canada's reliance on the US a priority, has framed the development of AI data centres as a matter of national security and sovereignty. It has projected that Canada needs to expand its computing capacity 16-fold over the next four years to meet domestic demand.

Alberta is defending its data centre plans by saying they will create jobs and generate revenue, and that sites will be developed "responsibly".

In Ontario, Premier Doug Ford has said that his province needs to move "full steam ahead" with building data centres.

Mullin said one reason the backlash had been less severe in Canada was simply due to scope and timing.

"There has been less of these projects happening, and as a result, they've attracted less attention," he said.

Canada has released some high-level guidance on data centre development, which has been co-signed by Anthropic, Meta and other big AI players. It calls on data centres to pay for electricity costs, minimise water use and create long-term benefits for the regions they are in.

If politicians don't tread carefully, Bek MacIntosh, an Alberta resident who has fought a proposed hyperscale data centre in her community, said she believed public discontent could translate into angry votes, like those seen in the US.

MacIntosh successfully halted a data centre in Olds, Alberta,, external over concerns with its scale, scope and proximity to her home. Even after her eight-month battle, she said she was not opposed to all AI infrastructure and believed Canada had the resources and landscape to build them responsibly.

But she fears Alberta has opened the door to "the most reckless of reckless developers", and warned that voters might have a "knee-jerk reaction" if provinces don't take care to develop AI data centres responsibly.

"And I don't think any political party in Canada is prepared for it."

'We all need data, but do we need the largest data centre in Europe here?'

Did a data centre revolt affect the US midterm election results?

Why the US midterms sparked an AI stock selloff

#95. Burnham seals trade and youth mobility deals in biggest step towards EU since Brexit

Agreements reached on food and drink, emissions and to allow young people to move around continent, but may be derailed over student fees

Andy Burnham has taken the biggest step towards deeper ties with the EU since Brexit, sealing deals to ease trade and create a mobility scheme for young people as he declared a “new world” of cooperation between the two sides.

At a major summit in Brussels on Friday the EU and UK announced three deals, on food and drink, emissions trading and a youth mobility scheme, which will allow 100,000 young Britons to live, work, study and travel in the EU, with reciprocal access for Europeans in the UK.

But the trio of deals could be derailed in six months, as the EU threatened to block them unless the UK allows European students to pay domestic-level fees to study at British universities.

Burnham rejected suggestions there was any confusion over the deals coming into force: “The deal is done. It is there in black and white in front of you,” he said, adding that the legislation would enter parliament next week.

The European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen, played down the disagreement, saying she was “confident we are going to find a solution”.

Hailing the “historic” agreement Burnham said: “This is a good day for Britain and this is a good day for Europe,” as he argued that the agreements answer “the biggest challenges of our times and the biggest worries that people have”, citing the cost of living, lack of economic growth, illegal immigration and challenges facing young people.

The prime minister said he would “brainstorm” with the EU over future ties, while also reiterating that he would not get ahead of public opinion.

Speaking to reporters, Burnham stressed that nothing would happen in the current parliament that went much beyond this deal. The so-called brainstorming process was, he said, “just looking at options in a new world, and having an open discussion about what those might be. It will be to inform the next parliament.”

Burnham also challenged political opponents, notably Nigel Farage, who was in Brussels on Friday to hold a retaliatory press conference, to justify why they would reject a deal which, he said, would boost growth and lead to cheaper food and electricity. “Why would anybody turn their back on that?”

The prime minister said the deals, which were initiated by Keir Starmer, would add 0.5% to British GDP, a £17bn boost to the British economy.

Farage said Burnham had been naive and the EU had “used this moment to strongarm the British prime minister over tuition fee cuts for hundreds of thousands of EU students”.

EU leaders sought to play down differences over tuition fees. “We are heading in the same direction and that is what matters,” the European Council president, António Costa, said of the broad relationship. He described the UK as “our closest neighbour, a reliable ally and a natural partner”.

Von der Leyen said it was up to the UK to decide where the journey was going, adding: “We welcome this brainstorming, so count on us.”

In a sign of tension in the negotiations, the EU agreed a declaration that the three deals would not come into force without equal treatment on fees for EU students at British universities.

“The [EU] Council and [European] Commission consider that this agreement is to provide for equal treatment with nationals as regards tuition fees for all students,” stated the confidential statement seen by the Guardian. The EU would not adopt the three agreements before “the conclusions of the negotiations … providing for equal treatment on tuition fees”, it added.

Another potential flashpoint could be the UK’s status as a rule-taker. The food and drink and emissions trading deals require the UK to align with EU rules. British experts will be invited to relevant EU meetings, including dealing with potential animal health emergencies. But Britain will not have voting rights. Burnham rejected suggestions this was a fig leaf, saying it would be “quite a moment” to have British experts back in EU meeting rooms.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

France was said to be instrumental in shaping Britain’s exclusion, although Paris argues its position was widely supported and reflects the reality that a non-member state cannot be a decision-maker.

Following the talks, Burnham was invited to a lunch of pea and mint soup, battered North Sea cod and chips, followed by Brussels waffle with fruit.

The summit began with a highly choreographed welcome ceremony in the Europa “space egg” European Council building, where a quintet played the British and EU anthems.

The UK hailed a joint decision to create a “partnership for economic resilience”, as an answer to its concerns over the EU’s Made in Europe plans. The EU is still finalising legislation that will give EU companies preference when public money is spent on the key technologies, such as renewables and electric cars. The UK has been arguing for Britain to be included in Made in Europe to ensure UK firms are not locked out of European supply chains.

In response, the two sides agreed to set up “a common prosperity and economic security space”, essentially an answer to shared concerns about artificially cheap Chinese goods that are depressing prices across Europe. But questions over whether the UK needs to align with EU trade defences remain open. Burnham said the two sides had reached an agreement “to work together where we have common threats”.

In an echo of the Brexit years, the final deal emerged after late-night talks went to the wire.

The German Social Democrat MEP Bernd Lange called on both sides to resolve the tuition fees issue to make studying affordable in a way that works for universities: “We can’t open the door to young people and then price them out of walking through it.”

The EU has welcomed Burnham’s breaking of taboos over rejoining, but also voiced frustration at British negotiating tactics. “The UK has a tendency to make even small questions so difficult,” an EU diplomat said. “A British prime minister talks about a change of approach … Then in negotiations it seems like British negotiators act the same way they always had.”

#93. EU wants ‘closest possible relationship’ with UK after agreeing Brexit reset deal – live

President of European Council says EU ‘remains open to have the closest possible relationship’ with the UK

The British prime minister Andy Burnham said his deal with the European Union will add up to £17bn a year to the British economy as he paved the way for even closer ties to Brussels. Burnham said it was a “good day for Britain and a good day for Europe”, as he met EU chiefs in Brussels to announce a package covering food and agriculture exports, emissions trading, and a youth experience scheme which could open the doors for 100,000 young Europeans to temporarily work in the UK, with Britons able to do the same on the continent.

Burnham ‌said Friday’s deal was “probably the limit” of what could be done before the next national election due by mid-2029, but he envisaged an informal conversation in about a year ‌with the EU about what could be achieved next.

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said she welcomed Burnham’s willingness to have a broad debate about future relations with the EU. “We are open for brainstorming, and we welcome this brainstorming, so count on us,” she said.

At the summit, postponed from July after the resignation of Burnham’s predecessor Keir Starmer, ‌the two sides addressed some of the Brexit losses with a package of agreements. The most significant allows cross-border trade in animals, plants and related products, such as sausages or seeds, without the certificates and controls currently required. Britain has estimated this could add up to £5.1bn per year to its economy.

A further agreement will link the emissions trading systems of Britain and the EU, which would exempt British and EU goods, such as steel, from each other’s border carbon levies. Each side is also set to allow 100,000 young people a year in to work or study for three years.

However, one potential dampener emerged as the 27-nation bloc and London jostled over linking ultimate implementation of the new deals to Britain agreeing to cut university fees for EU students. Britain ⁠prefers to offer only “significantly reduced” fees, and the aim is to resolve the issue within six months.

The chief executive of the British Retail Consortium (BRC) called the SPS deal a “big win” for UK retailers but warned that businesses need “at least 18 months” to transition to the new arrangements.

Nigel Farage, the veteran anti-EU campaigner and leader of the Reform UK party, said the deal “was about the first steps, incrementally, to taking us back into membership of the European Union,” and again predicted an early election next year. Farage told reporters he would campaign to preserve Brexit. “I am ready for this fight,” he said.

Denmark’s foreign minister on Friday said Moscow’s defence attache in Copenhagen would be expelled after Russia was suspected of being behind an attempted assassination of a Ukrainian rapper.

The UK prime minister called the newly agreed post-Brexit reset deal a “win-win” for the UK and the EU, although the level of tuition fees EU students will pay to study at UK universities remains unresolved.

The prime minister met Ursula von der Leyen, the European Commission president, and António Costa, the European Council president, at the EU-UK summit in Brussels.

Students and young workers have taken to the streets in more than 20 European countries in coordinated protests over the surging cost of living and housing, deteriorating learning conditions and rising military spending.

Activists from more than 50 groups in countries including Belgium, France, Germany, Greece, Italy, Portugal, Spain and the UK held rallies on Friday, lighting flares, burning cardboard models of tanks and waving placards, many reading “fight for our future”.

Calling it Europe’s biggest coordinated youth protest of recent years, the Youth Strike coalition said demonstrations were planned in about 400 cities under the slogan “fight for our future, not their wars”.

It made five broad demands, including an end to militarisation and conscription; structural investment in education and welfare; solidarity with Palestine; climate and social justice, and a “radical transformation of the system we live in”.

An Italian organiser, Alessandro di Miceli, said the Europe-wide protests were “to claim our right to a better and dignified future”. Specific demands differed from country to country, he said, but were all “deeply connected between each other”.

A Portuguese organiser, Leonor Chicó, said: “As young people, we know that we must organise and fight for our future, because no one else will do it for us.” Governments “show us every day that the needs of our generation are not their priority”, she said.

Shabbir Lakha from the UK-based Stop The War coalition said the mobilisation was “a crucial step towards building a lasting international movement capable of bringing together young people, students and workers around common demands”.

After weeks of demonstrations across several countries sparked by long-running French school protests, governments are struggling to respond to growing calls for affordable housing, functioning public services and greater social equality.

Speaking to British reporters following the leaders’ lunch, Burnham stressed that nothing would happen in the current parliament that went much beyond this deal.

He said: “There is a clear democratic mandate for what has been done today. It’s in the Labour manifesto… There will be nothing beyond the significance of this in this parliament.”

The so-called brainstorming process was, he said, “just looking at options in a new world, and having an open discussion about what those what those might might might be – it will be to inform the next parliament”,

He also challenged political opponents, notably Nigel Farage, who was in Brussels on Friday to hold a retaliatory press conference, to justify why they would reject a deal which, he said, would boost growth and lead to cheaper food and electricity.

“Why would anybody turn their back on that?” he said. “I think it’s for them to justify now [whether] they won’t support the bill that will be presented to parliament next week.”

The campaign group Best for Britain says the UK-EU agreement is an “essential and laudable step” towards Britain rejoining the union.

Commenting on the deal, chief executive Naomi Smith said:

double quotation markToday marks one small step in our economic fortunes but one giant leap for young people’s opportunities, and British businesses who for too long have had their horizons narrowed and wings clipped by needless Brexit red tape.

Having drawn a line under a decade of drift and division, the prime minister must now build on the bold leadership he has shown so far, and take the essential and laudable step towards restoring our EU membership, as the majority of voters the length and breadth of Great Britain now demand.

The joint commitment to open a dialogue on bloc wide touring artists permits (15:20) is a win for the UK.

Brussels always resisted talks on the topic as work permits for singers, actors and other performers are a national competence and not the preserve of Brussels.

This is also the reason behind the length of time it will take to implement the youth mobility programme – the joint statement confirms it will be at least 2028 and latest 2030 when the scheme will be operational.

One EU diplomat this week said of the youth scheme:

“The problem is the UK is negotiating with Brussels but it is the member states that make the decisions.”

However if Brussels can find some way to achieve a “one size fits all” work permit for under 31s in the youth mobility scheme that might apply to touring artists too.

Retailers and manufacturers have welcomed Andy Burnham’s new trade deal with the European Union as a “big win” but warned they need “clear and early” guidance from government to aid the transition, Press Association reported.

The chief executive of the British Retail Consortium (BRC) called the SPS deal a “big win” for UK retailers but warned that businesses need “at least 18 months” to transition to the new arrangements.

“Reducing border friction and simplifying trade will cut costs and support smoother trade in food, drink and plants between the UK and EU, including moving goods to Northern Ireland.

The benefits will be felt across the entire supply chain, from producers and suppliers through to retailers and the millions of consumers they serve.”

The priority now must be to get the detail right and give businesses at least 18 months, alongside clear and early guidance, to transition to the new arrangements without unnecessary cost or disruption.”

But the BRC also told the Press Association that it does not expect to see prices on supermarket shelves fall in response to this deal, even though this is a welcome intervention.

Upward pressures remain heavy and will not be alleviated by the agreement, it said.

The National Farmers Union said they had worked with the Government to secure the deal which “should help” the industry, but warned that it was “only the beginning”.

President of the NFU Tom Bradshaw said that while reducing border friction is welcome, “rebuilding lost exports will take time” as in some cases British products have been replaced by goods from elsewhere.

The managing director of Marks + Spencer Food, Alex Freudmann, said in a blog on Friday that the deal will “remove bureaucracy” from the current regulations.

“How hard can it be to get a spaghetti bolognese from a factory in Lincolnshire onto a shelf in Dublin?

Today, it takes hundreds of extra miles, mountains of paperwork, a vet’s signature and the Latin name of a cow (Bos taurus, if you’re wondering).

This is what food trade looks like after Brexit. And it’s why we need the UK-EU food trade deal announced today.”

To celebrate the EU-UK reset deal Andy Burnham and his cabinet are invited to lunch with the European Commission and European Council.

The menu couldn’t be more fitting: creamy pea and mint soup, battered North Sea cod and chips, followed by Brussels waffle with fruit.

Because everyone in the EU loves a bit of Brussels waffle, in both senses.

For what it’s worth, the EU’s explainer – helpfully written as a Q&A - is here.

The main document from today’s summit is this one: the joint declaration signed by the two sides.

I won’t read it chronologically, but jump a bit, reflecting what I think you are looking for, and not how the document is written.

On “people to people” relations (pages 7-8), the document confirms the Youth Experience Scheme (YES) that will allow “100,000 people from the EU and the UK respectively to reside for up to three years to engage in activities such as work, studies, au-pairing, volunteering or simply travelling.”

On timing, it says the sides “aim to apply this agreement no later than 2028,” and want it to kick in “no later than January 2030.” It will apply to those up to the age of 31, so effectively will help those born in 1999 and later.

The document also confirms the UK’s return to the Erasmus+ programme in 2027, which we already knew about.

On the thorny issue of tuition fees, it says the parties will “work towards a supplementing agreement” to be agreed within six months. It’s very much unfinished business on that point.

In a win for artists and performers, the document also includes a somewhat vague, but promising, commitment to “explore further mutually beneficial solutions” to help touring artists with ridiculous red tape currently in place.

The statement also covers some of the key sectoral deals we mentioned earlier (pages 9-10), on emissions trading systems, sanitary and phytosanitary measures (SPS), and yes – of course – on fish (noting the decision on full reciprocal access to waters to fish until 30 June 2038).

In similarly practical decisions, there is also a note on “the significant progress achieved in discussions on the mutual recognition of professional qualifications for architects” which are set to continue.

The statement also covers important issues on judicial and security matters, noting plans to enhance cooperation between law enforcement agencies and, particularly, on the removal of foreign national offenders and migrant returns (pages 11-12).

The new language on economic resilience and cooperation is on pages 13-14, including commitment to “counter technology leakage in critical sectors, mitigate risks to critical infrastructure, and [to build] resilient supply chains” and so on.

The document, finally, also covers foreign and defence policy, in generally vague language, but across several pages (1-7).

It includes a long list of things the EU and the UK agree on, including on their support for Ukraine, foreign and defence policy priorities related to Africa, Arctic, Gaza, Iran, Lebanon, Nato, Moldova, and the Western Balkans.

It also includes a line on commitment to “effective multilateralism” through the UN, the international criminal court, which continues to attract attacks from the US, and the global rules-based trade system.

Meanwhile, the UK government has published some documents from the reset deal here.

27 pages to go through this beautiful Friday afternoon.

And more to come, no doubt, as there are no legal texts published yet.

Burnham rejects a suggestion that today’s deal is merely “a fig leaf,” saying it is “a little harsh”.

He says today’s developments are “a sign of sensible, constructive collaboration where it is beneficial for both sides.”

“Our experts and European experts, in the same room, dealing with these challenges, together, that is progress. I would say not a fig leaf.”

And then we are back to questions on today’s deal and the tuition fees disagreement – and whether the two EU leaders agree with Burnham that today’s deal marks the high point of what can be done without a broader vision for the EU-UK relationship.

Costa smiles and says that the focus needs to be on building “the closest relationship possible” between the EU and the UK, but how close it can get needs to be defined by the British.

“In the meantime, we need to work very hard to try to solve problems to our companies, to our citizens, to improve the competitiveness of our economies,” he says.

But, somewhat echoing Burnham’s language earlier (14:37), he focuses on younger generations and says “these young people, a large part of them, was not here ten years ago, but they are here today.”

“And this is our duty to open to them new opportunities,” he says.

He says today’s deal on youth mobility for 100,000 people “is the best way to give future to our shared values, our mutual interest, our historic bond and cultural values that we share together.”

Von der Leyen says “we can always work on an incremental improvement to our relationship,” but she agrees that there is a much broader question on “the wider picture, the bigger picture” of how Europe should respond to “dramatic and big changes globally.”

The next question is on something very different – on continuing hybrid attacks about different European countries, asked in the context of airspace violations in Poland.

Two Russian cruise missiles and a jet-powered drone are reported to have crashed in Poland during Russian air attacks on Ukraine that took place amid major western military exercises by allied countries.

EU’s Costa says “it shows that, unfortunately, Russia instead of showing willingness to engage in peace talks continue to spread the threats.”

“This is very dangerous and shows Russia is going in the wrong direction,” he says.

Burnham says the incursion into Polish airspace is “utterly unacceptable,” as he says he wants to send “a strong message of solidarity to prime minister Tusk and the Polish people who we will support, always, in any way that we can.”

He makes a broader point on the back of that, saying “Europe is becoming more coordinated in terms of defence and security,” and today’s agreement helps with that.

He pivots to Ukraine more broadly, saying “if European security is to come into that question, first we must have a ceasefire, and we must have the basis of a peace deal between Russia and Ukraine.”

“We will have to think this through carefully, together, to ensure that we are stronger in the next decade, than we were in the last.

I think some of what we’ve seen in Europe came at us without us being fully prepared. Not just the aggression, but also the disruption within our democracies and other factors that have sought to weaken Europe.

We will come back stronger in this decade, and we collaborate more deeply on security and defence.”

Asked about British people making up their minds, von der Leyen smiles that “I have many British friends and they are very good at making up their minds.”

On today’s deals, she backs Burnham’s line that today’s deal matters.

“We have three major agreements where many people thought we would never be able, to come to the finish line.”

#90. Mysterious Mercury is about to get a visitor - after spacecraft's eight-year epic voyage

After an eight-year journey travelling almost six billion miles (10bn km), a spacecraft called BepiColombo is about to reach its final destination and get into orbit around Mercury.

It will be entering an extremely hostile environment: Mercury is the closest planet to the Sun and temperatures can reach a fierce 430C and then plunge to -180C, and it is constantly blasted with intense solar radiation.

Until now, the planet has been little explored but the hope is this mission will solve some of Mercury's major mysteries.

These include uncovering why there is ice in its craters despite its proximity to the Sun, and understanding the mysterious hollows that are dotted all over its surface.

"I think it's a little bit of an under-loved planet in the inner Solar System, but I think that's one of the reasons why it is so compelling," said Prof Emma Bunce, from Leicester University, who is a principal investigator on the mission.

"There are lots of reasons, scientifically, why we really want to understand Mercury, how it formed, how it evolved, what it looks like today, but also how it interacts with the Sun and the extreme space weather that it experiences."

The spacecraft - just seen here - has taken selfies as it has closed in on Mercury

The BepiColombo mission, which is a collaboration between the European Space Agency (ESA) and Japanese Space Agency (JAXA), blasted off in 2018, launching from French Guiana.

With the spacecraft heading deep into the inner Solar System, it has had to battle the Sun's immense gravitational pull.

So it has had its brakes on for the entire journey, and has also taken a very convoluted path - looping once around the Earth, twice around Venus and six times around Mercury - to slow it down enough to get into orbit.

This journey should have taken seven years, but a problem with BepiColombo's propulsion system meant the already epic voyage was extended by a year.

The final manoeuvre to get into orbit is a perilous one: it needs to perform a 37-minute handbrake turn to be grabbed by the planet's gravity.

If anything goes wrong, the spacecraft could hurtle past Mercury.

A close up view of the scarred surface of Mercury

Only two spacecraft have visited Mercury until now - both Nasa missions.

Mariner-10 flew past the planet three times in the 1970s, and then the Messenger spacecraft arrived at Mercury in 2011 and spent four years in orbit.

But there is still much to discover about this tiny world.

Mercury is only a bit bigger than our Moon, and is covered in ancient volcanoes, towering cliffs and colossal craters.

And time on Mercury passes in a mind-bending way.

The planet zips around the Sun so quickly, a year on Mercury lasts just 88 Earth days. But it turns incredibly slowly on its own axis, so a single day there lasts 59 Earth days.

These strange hollows have not been seen anywhere else in the Universe

As well as investigating why Mercury has ice in its craters in the polar regions, the mission will also spend time studying strange dents in the planet's surface.

There are thousands of hollows all over it. They are shallow but with steep sides and often clustered together, and in planetary terms, they are young: about 100,000 years old.

Scientists know they are not impact craters - larger pockmarks on the surface caused by colliding space rocks - but they are not really sure what the hollows are or how they formed.

BepiColombo will also help to study another aspect of Mercury that has been puzzling researchers. Despite the planet's tiny size, it has a huge metal core.

Prof Emma Bunce said that the planet could once have been much bigger, but was potentially the victim of a "hit and run" early in its history.

"One of the explanations is that the mantle was stripped away in a large collision," she explained.

"Or it's a combination of collisions over a longer period of time that then strips away its outer outer regions, its mantle and its crust, and leads to the planet that we see today."

Or she said, it could have just formed that way in a metal-rich part of the early Solar System.

A life-size model of BepiColombo is on display at the Science Museum in London

Unusually, this is a sort of "buy one get one free" mission - there are two spacecraft onboard.

A few weeks after arriving at Mercury they will separate.

An ESA craft will study the surface close-up, the other, which belongs to JAXA, will view the planet from a distance.

A life-size model of BepiColombo is on display at the Science Museum in London. It was used to test the craft while it was being put together.

Jessica Marshall, a systems engineer at Airbus, who built the spacecraft, showed us Bepi's key features - and said the moment of separation would be "nerve-wracking" for the team.

The two spacecraft had to be very securely attached together for their launch and long journey, but now, eight years later, they have to delicately disconnect.

"We have designed a special mechanism that we have thought very carefully about," said Marshall.

"It's been exposed to the harsh radiation of space and highly different thermal temperatures, and after eight years this is a spring mechanism that has to then release very gentlyâ¦ As it releases, it actually also twists in order to start the spin of that spacecraft as well."

With two orbiters at Mercury, both packed with instruments, the BepiColombo team hopes to find answers to Mercury's mysteries.

Of course, they may uncover many more once they arrive.

#89. Denmark to expel Russian diplomat over alleged Moscow link to attempted killing of rapper

Russian intelligence thought to be behind plan to kill US-Ukrainian musician Kyivstoner before concert in Denmark

Denmark’s foreign minister has said Russia’s defence attache in Copenhagen would be expelled after Moscow was suspected of being behind an attempted killing of a US-Ukrainian rapper.

“We have just summoned the Russian ambassador to the ministry of foreign affairs and notified him of the government’s decision to expel the Russian defence attache from Denmark,”, Lars Løkke Rasmussen told a news conference. The expulsion will happen within seven days.

“We are saying this very clearly: we have exposed you,” he added.

Danish police said they had averted an attempt by Russian intelligence services ⁠to kill the US-Ukrainian rapper Kyivstoner in September before a planned performance in Denmark.

Rasmussen also said that Denmark was considering suspending Schengen visas to Russians.

“We have launched work at the European level to further tighten the rules governing access of Russian nationals to Europe,” he said. “We are now stepping up our efforts towards a halt to all non-essential visas.

“The strongest response Denmark can give is not to waver and to support Ukraine. And we fully intend to continue supporting Ukraine.”

Police ⁠thwarted the alleged ⁠plot when ​they arrested three men with links to the Danish criminal underworld shortly before Kyivstoner, whose real name is Albert Viktorovich Vasiliev, was due to perform at a ⁠concert on 25 September, which was then cancelled, officials said. Vasiliev holds US and Ukrainian nationality.

Rasmus Kieffer-Kristensen, the head of Denmark’s national security and intelligence service, said: “It is our clear assessment that the assassination attempt was planned by the Russian ⁠intelligence services.”

According to prosecutors, the suspects were driving towards the concert venue north of Copenhagen with two loaded pistols and two knives when police arrested them at about 9.55pm, five minutes before the concert was due ‌to begin. A fourth suspect was arrested later that evening, police said.

One of the suspects had bought a ticket to the concert, according ​to the charges.

The suspects are charged under Section 108 of Denmark’s criminal code, which covers assisting a foreign intelligence service, as well as attempted murder. They denied wrongdoing in court, the broadcaster DR reported.

Kieffer-Kristensen said: “A Russian assassination attempt is not something we’ve seen in Denmark before, ⁠and that gives me an impression of an increasingly aggressive and ​risk-taking Russia.

“We believe ​that the case is important ​for understanding the threat that Denmark faces from Russia.”

#87. 'I tell my son it's just fireworks': Life inside Yemeni city besieged by Houthis

Houthi attacks on Taiz have killed more than 140 civilians since August, local government officials say

In the Yemeni city of Taiz, ringed by jagged mountains and Houthi rebel fighters, Ashwaq has only one way to try to soothe her frightened child - lie.

"When my 11-year-old son hears a loud sound, I tell him, 'Don't be afraid, it's just fireworks'," she says. "I don't want him to panic, but deep down I know he is old enough to understand what is happening around him. He has lived through war for most of his life."

Taiz has long been a prize - and a front line - in Yemen's civil war.

Houthi forces tightened their grip last month, fully encircling the government-controlled city, and the population - estimated to be between 600,000 and 1 million.

Since then, Yemen's third-largest city has been cut off from the world, and its residents have been largely unheard. We reached seven people inside the city who mapped out the anatomy of the siege.

They gave detailed accounts of the Houthis' death grip on Taiz. Only a minimal amount of food can get in. Prices for staples like rice, sugar and cooking oil have doubled or even trebled. Essential supplies, like medicine, water and fuel, are dwindling.

Burning piles of rubbish are producing toxic smoke that hangs over the city

On streets that used to hum with traffic and background chatter, a silence has fallen. Schools are closed and restaurants are shuttered. Suffering is being heaped on suffering. Piles of rubbish are trapped inside the city, alongside the people. When residents try to get rid of it by burning it, there is a toxic smoke. Even the air is now a Houthi weapon.

From positions on the hills, rebel fighters - and snipers - can wage war on the city below.

Estimates for the death toll vary widely. At the Office for Siege Affairs, a local government department, they say 148 civilians have been killed by Houthi attacks since August.

Some of those we contacted in Taiz do not want to be identified because they fear for their safety. Many in the city are reluctant to even speak on the phone because the Houthis control key mobile carriers.

"Sometimes it feels like the city has become a huge prison," says Ashwaq, a teacher and mother of three, who only wanted her first name to be used. She yearns for the old Taiz.

"It used to be a lively city," she says. "People loved life, worked hard, visited one another, and had hopes for the future, despite all the difficulties. Now, we hardly go out anymore. The [Houthi] shelling can happen any time, and sniper fire near the front lines makes even a short trip dangerous."

She keeps her son and two elder daughters indoors.

"I worry about them every single day, every hour, and honestly every minute," she says.

Taiz has been a major battleground in Yemen's civil war for more than a decade

War is not new to Taiz. The strategic city is the gateway to Aden - the temporary seat of the internationally recognised government - and to southern Yemen.

Houthi rebels - backed by Iran - reached the outskirts of Taiz in 2015, cutting off roads, and laying siege from three sides. A year earlier, they had seized Yemen's capital, Sanaa.

More than a decade on, the government, which is weak and fractured, has been unable to reclaim the capital and much of north-western Yemen. That's despite the backing of Saudi Arabia, and its air strikes.

In recent months, the conflict has flared up and expanded after a four-year lull. The Houthis made a lightning advance, taking territory from the government, and striking Saudi Arabia with missiles and drones.

A long-running civil war has become a threat to regional stability.

An emergency room doctor, whom we are calling Yaser, bears witness to that.

Among the war wounded recently brought to his hospital were four children, shot by Houthi snipers. They usually aim for the head and chest, he tells us. Two of the four children died.

The hardest thing he faces, as a father himself, is treating wounded children. "I have to rise to the responsibility, compose myself and support the families," he says. "It's a difficult situation that I cannot even put into words, when I see a father looking at his badly wounded child right in front of him."

Most of the injured are victims of shelling. Reaching the hospital can be an ordeal. Some families have to walk for hours from outlying areas.

They arrive exhausted, like the doctors who will treat them, and who are struggling with "everything", says Yaser. Even sterilising fluid for surgical instruments is in short supply.

He tells us the hospital is running on empty, and can carry on for two more weeks, "maybe".

Taiz's hospitals have lost 70% of their capacity and 120 health centres have closed, its governor says

If the emergency rooms are busy, so are the graveyards.

"Funerals heading to the cemeteries have become the defining scene in Taiz, around the clock," says Taiz's Governor, Nabil Shamsan.

It's not just bombs and bullets that kill, he tells us.

"The people of Taiz are losing their lives due to supply shortages, lack of food, and loss of income," he says. "Every single day, we bury dozens of Taiz residents."

He remains inside the city, and inside the siege. "I live among my people," he says.

On a video call, he looks weary but delivers a lengthy chronicle of a city being starved of oxygen - literally.

He tells us that hospitals have lost 70% of their capacity, and 120 health centres have closed. Diseases are spreading, neonatal deaths have increased, and malnutrition is surging. Hundreds of kidney patients are not getting dialysis because they can't get to hospitals or because fuel is short when they do.

The Houthi leader, Abdul Malik al-Houthi, has denied Taiz is besieged. Late last month, he claimed that routes remained open for food, medicine and humanitarian aid.

That is denied by the government, residents of Taiz, aid agencies and the United Nations.

What's at stake in the battle for the city is more than territory, says Shamsan.

He says the Houthis - who mostly follow a branch of Shia Islam - want to change the character of this predominantly Sunni city, long known as a seat of learning and a bastion of resistance.

"Everyone knows that Taiz is a hub of culture, a city characterised by freedom, political pluralism and all forms of civic life," he says. In sharp contrast, he says, the Houthis aim to impose their strict religious doctrine and drag the city "back to the dark ages".

Whatever the pressure on the city and its people, the governor insists the Houthis will never win.

"Until the population perishes," he replies.

Essential supplies, like medicine, water and fuel, are dwindling

They are defending their city, alongside relatives, friends, neighbours, and five brigades of pro-government troops, who can't do the job on their own.

They don't have heavy weapons or enough numbers for the expanding front line.

Training camps have sprung up for volunteers, but the lessons can be as brief as a day or two before they are sent to join the fight.

Akram (not his real name) is already a veteran. He's 31, with a child on the way.

When Taiz was fully encircled before, in 2015, he broke away from his studies and took up arms. In that round of fighting, the Houthis gained control of his district on the outskirts, and he was uprooted. He hasn't seen his old home since then.

Last month, when Taiz was completely cut off once again, he bought a rifle, paying about $1,200 (Â£910) for a Chinese version of a Kalashnikov. It used to cost less than half that sum.

He tells us this fight is different to 2015.

"This time the Houthis have new technology and new missiles," he says. "They can wage a war in the air with drones. We fear the death of our families and our civilians, and ourselves."

He has already lost four friends in the past month, who left behind wives and children.

But Akram, and many others, see defending Taiz as their duty. He says that "all the sons of Taiz" will fight the Houthis "and their extremism".

The armed group is currently designated as a foreign terrorist organisation by the US (which has gone back and forth on the issue) but not by the UK or the European Union.

"They say God gave them the right to rule everyone in Yemen. We don't accept that," says Akram. "We live in freedom. We cannot surrender; we cannot leave. We cannot breathe without our city."

Almost 350,000 people have been displaced by the fighting in recent months

But it can be suffocating inside Taiz, where nightfall brings no rest.

"Every night we have bombing and shelling," says Ahmad Al-Basha, a photographer. "I think the Houthis prefer to fight at night. Maybe they don't want us and our children to sleep inside our homes."

The people of Taiz feel let down, he tells us, by their government and by the international community. "We feel frustrated and sad," he says, "because we don't see any move to help us."

Many believe the Houthis will keep a chokehold on Taiz from the outside, and exert significant control over the city without trying to enter it.

Ahmad believes it won't come to that but fears the worst if it does.

"Maybe they will clean Taiz of any resistance, kill people and settle scores with anyone who is against them," he says.

His greatest concern is for his children, who cannot escape the sounds of war.

Like Ashwaq, the teacher, he tries to explain it away.

"I tell them it's a party," he says, "and if it's really loud, I say it's a big party."

As Taiz endures bombing, shelling, and a sense of abandonment laced with growing hunger, Ashwaq has a parent's plea for the international community.

"Please don't forget Taiz," she says. "We are ordinary families. We want our children to study, play and dream like other children around the world. We are not just numbers in news reports."

Additional reporting by Wietske Burema and Albaraa Mansoor

#86. Misty-eyed Burnham gambles on closer ties with EU as he throws down gauntlet to Tories and Reform

PM challenges rivals to rebuff plan he says could add 0.5% annual growth to economy and offer young people hope

Several sentences into a slightly convoluted answer about where UK-EU relations might go next, Andy Burnham abruptly changed tack. “The important point is that the Britain of today and the EU of today is not the one of 2016,” he said. “Life has moved on in so many ways.”

This is undoubtedly true. But Burnham’s political gamble on closer ties with Brussels hinges on the assumption that most British voters feel this way as well.

Seeking to push his deal on food checks, emissions trading and a new youth exchange scheme, Burnham was at first in full consumer politics mode, saying it would bring cheaper food – with a namecheck for Tesco, Asda and other supermarket chains – as well as lower electricity costs and a claimed 0.5% boost to overall growth.

But as the Brussels press conference went on, the prime minister became more misty-eyed, particularly in describing the youth exchange scheme as a gift to a generation stripped of choices by the 2016 referendum and then Covid. “Hope is a word,” he intoned. “And it starts now.”

For all that Burnham is a skilled political salesman, his oratory is backed up with some helpful political context, matched with cooperative allies.

To begin with, not only politics but polling results are now different. A new constituency-level survey released on Friday showed that of 394 constituencies estimated to have voted leave in 2016, voters in 274 of them would back rejoining the EU – including Burnham’s Makerfield seat.

Standing alongside the UK prime minister in the venue, Ursula von der Leyen, the European Commission president, and António Costa, the European Council president, at times resembled proud parents, beaming at every positive phrase.

They, of course, began work when the EU reset was the personal project of one Keir Starmer. His name was not mentioned on Friday, and it is undeniable that Brussels finds life easier dealing with a UK leader who is both bold and popular.

Speaking to British reporters later in the day, Burnham openly challenged political opponents to rebuff a plan that, he said, could add 0.5% annual growth to the economy in the long term – more, he said, than any other single intervention.

“Why would anybody turn their back on that?” he said, with particular focus on Nigel Farage, who was, soon after Burnham spoke, hosting a retaliatory press conference of his own, 10 minutes’ walk from the EU building.

Even with the tight timing – Farage had to delay his event as the summit timetable slipped – the Reform UK leader drew a decent crowd of reporters. But there will be a sense in No 10 that beyond Farage, most of the formidable Brexit forces of 2016 have melted away.

Kemi Badenoch, who has been advised by her team to talk more about the economy and less about Brexit, spent the day on a visit in Essex. The Conservatives did send out a condemnatory press release about the EU deal, but it focused more on Burnham “reopening the divisions of the past” than the specifics.

More trenchant was a statement from Stay Out, the recently formed Brexit purity group, which floated its argument that rejoining the EU would cost the UK £750m a week.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

For all that the dominance of vote leave alumni in Stay Out gives the campaign the slight air of a historic battle re-enactment society, Burnham cannot ignore them. A resonant sidenote of the summit was how he was happy to talk about the specifics of the deal, but not much beyond that.

While it was the PM himself who provoked a debate about a future in which the UK rejoined the EU, the brakes were firmly on in regard to any discussion of such ideas.

Pre-summit talk of possible closer future links with Brussels was replaced by much vaguer language about mutual “brainstorming” on what would come next, with UK officials saying any progress could be months in the making.

Another sign of the caution embedded within Burnham’s ambition was the lineup of UK cabinet ministers who watched as the prime minister, von der Leyen and Costa arrived on Friday morning.

Alongside those you might expect – Ed Miliband, the foreign secretary, John Healey, the chancellor, and Jonathan Reynolds, the business secretary – was Anneliese Midgley, a close ally of Burnham who has the job of chief whip, No 10’s liaison with Labour MPs.

She was there because a bill setting into UK law the new EU deal will be presented to parliament next week. Most Labour MPs agree with Burnham that the world has changed since 2016. But, as with the voters, he needs to bring them along with him.

#82. Spain frees wealthy US-sought activist on bail as Irish police raid Dublin home

Pro-Palestinian donor James ‘Fergie’ Chambers released as court considers extradition request

In a blur of events surrounding the Trump administration’s effort to extradite James “Fergie” Chambers, Spain released the wealthy US activist and donor on bail even as Irish police raided a Dublin house owned by Chambers and his partner, Stella Schnabel, acting on a request from US authorities.

Chambers’s case is the first known instance of the US seeking to extradite a citizen over alleged support for Hamas, an accusation apparently tied to an international money-laundering charge in the criminal complaint underpinning the extradition request and donations to organizations helping Palestinians in Gaza.

Speaking outside Madrid’s Aranjuez prison upon his release on Friday, Chambers called the charges against him “baseless”, adding that his release was “hopefully the first step in recognizing that internationally”.

Chambers also said: “Spain is a country that has stood on the right side of this most important issue of our time, that has stood on the right side of the genocide, and where this is some reason and some justice.”

An appeals court ordered the conditional release on Friday due to issues surrounding the legally required US translation of the extradition request’s core documents into Spanish, first reported on by the Guardian. He had been detained since 10 July, when Spanish authorities arrested him in Ibiza.

The order asserts that the translation issues cause “a diminishment, at least for the time being, in the solidity or foundation of the extradition request”.

Chambers’s release follows a separate decision on Thursday by the three-judge panel at the high court tasked with deciding on the extradition to pause the proceedings in order to evaluate the impact of the alterations the US made to the original, machine-made translation of the extradition request into Spanish.

The Guardian detailed four examples of the alterations on 7 November and the US admitted to tampering with the translation, calling them “substantial [and] inadvertent” on 17 November – and citing the same four examples. Chambers’s defense team has asserted for weeks that there are as many as 30 such alterations.

The changes introduced prejudicial notions with specific legal implications that in turn wound up in the Spanish prosecutor’s report on the case, Aitor Martínez, a member of Chambers’s defense team, told the Guardian earlier this week. The judges must consider the prosecutor’s report in deciding on the extradition request.

The high court was expected to rule on the extradition this week, following a 13 November hearing. But as of Thursday’s decision, the deadline has been extended indefinitely as the judges evaluate the translation, including a 345-page report submitted by the defense team that identifies 30 “incriminating changes” Trump administration officials made to a translation prepared by DeepL, “an AI-first translation platform”, according to its website.

The decision also gives the prosecutor an opportunity to address the issue in writing, after dismissing it in the 13 November hearing by referring to “supposed translation errors”.

Chambers’s views and donations to progressive and humanitarian projects have been extensively profiled in US media, including his controversial public comments on Israel and Gaza. A week after the 7 October 2023 Hamas attacks on Israel, he posted on X about Israel’s response: “Stop calling it ‘collective punishment’/There’s nothing to be ‘punished’ for/No faction of the Palestinian resistance, Hamas or other, has done *anything* wrong/It’s simply genocide/Like it has been.”

The heir to $250m from his family’s business, Cox Enterprises, is being sought in extradition because of such views, his defense team has asserted throughout the case.

Meanwhile, the Irish Garda Síochána, acting on a request from US authorities, raided a Dublin house owned by Chambers and Schnabel on Wednesday, destroying the front door and removing electronic items and other possessions, according to their attorney in Ireland, Ciarán Mulholland.

Schnabel, who is five months pregnant, said: “It is difficult to see this raid as anything other than a desperate fishing expedition by the US authorities to find evidence to bolster their case against Fergie.”

The attorney told the Guardian that the raid was the result of a “mutual legal assistance” request from the US – and that such a request “wouldn’t give [the Garda Síochána] permission to kick in the doors of someone’s dwelling in Ireland”.

“It’s unusual for the Irish state to be acting in such a clandestine manner,” Mulholland said. He has asked authorities to produce a search warrant and an itemized list of what was taken. “My fear is that whatever was taken has been put on a plane and taken to the US,” he added.

“This [extradition case] shows the complete disregard for due process … that the Trump administration seems to have,” Mulholland said.

#78. Beau Lamarre-Condon’s explanations of body disposal ‘ring hollow’, court hears

Former police officer has pleaded not guilty to murdering Jesse Baird and Luke Davies in Paddington in February 2024

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Beau Lamarre-Condon knew he had murdered two men and his attempts to explain the disposal of their bodies “ring completely hollow”, a court has heard.

Lamarre-Condon is accused of fatally shooting Jesse Baird and his new partner, Luke Davies, with his police service weapon in Baird’s Paddington home in Sydney on 19 February 2024.

The former officer has pleaded not guilty to two counts of murder and one charge of aggravated break and enter.

On Friday, Crown prosecutor Brett Hatfield SC went through Lamarre-Condon’s attempts to clean up the alleged crime scene and dispose of the bodies as the trial wrapped up its ninth week.

The defence does not dispute Lamarre-Condon’s actions in the aftermath of the deaths, instead arguing he did so because he “panicked considerably”.

But Hatfield told the New South Wales supreme court his conduct was not that of a person who panicked after something unintended, but behaviour that was “deliberate, sustained and very purposeful”.

The crown prosecutor said Lamarre-Condon took extensive steps to distance himself from the deaths, manufactured evidence to make it look like Baird was alive by texting people from the dead man’s phone, and “disposed of the bodies in an utterly callous manner”.

“The lies, concealment and disposal of the bodies are not equally explained by manslaughter or accident,” Hatfield said.

“His conduct is immediate and the steps are so extreme you might think it’s only consistent with having intentionally killed both men and a planned murder.

“His explanations in his evidence … ring completely hollow.”

Lamarre-Condon claimed his initial steps came out of concern for the housemates, but by leaving the bodies in the courtyard, he risked them making a “completely terrifying discovery”, Hatfield told the court.

Hatfield said the accused had an understanding that “exactly what he’d done was murder”.

“His conduct was an exercise in avoiding accountability in what he knew he had done,” he said.

Earlier on Friday, Hatfield re-iterated the prosecution’s arguments about the day of the alleged murders, playing the audio of the two gunshots that killed Baird.

The lawyer invited the jury to consider what they could do in the four seconds between the shots.

Making a gun gesture with his own hand, Hatfield argued there was only time to walk a few steps and fire and “not opportunity for much more than that”.

Lamarre-Condon then came up with a “pathetic moral dilemma” and claimed he feared he could be killed in jail, when he knew he would be in protective custody, the prosecution alleged.

His decisions to go to Sushi Train for dinner with his friend and undergo a minor elective surgery in the aftermath of the alleged murders showed Lamarre-Condon was calm and at points “completely relaxed”, Hatfield alleged.

But within days, the accused must have been “feeling the world closing in”, the prosecutor said.

“He wants credit for handing himself in to police … but even in his last hours he’s there going to Bunnings, buying paint and painting the inside of the van … still trying to cover up what happened,” Hatfield told the court.

Lamarre-Condon eventually helped police locate the bodies, but only after being told about the state’s no body, no parole laws.

“The bodies were left … for a further four days after he’s arrested,” Hatfield said. “Evidence all the while being destroyed.

“Imagine the grief [of the families] on those four days.”

The defence alleges Baird and Davies were killed in two separate struggles when Lamarre-Condon was acting in “self-defence”.

Going through forensic evidence, Hatfield noted Davies’ DNA was found on the gun, but says this is “entirely unremarkable evidence”, alleging Lamarre-Condon had handled his body at the scene and then touched the weapon.

“It’s just something he’s grabbed on to in order to weave this invented narrative about Mr Davies shooting himself,” the lawyer said.

Earlier evidence provided by a weapons expert indicated it would take the force equivalent to almost 3kg to pull the trigger, which would not happen without a conscious movement on behalf of the person holding the gun, the prosecution alleged.

The trial before Justice Natalie Adams continues.
