#41. South Korea catches 111 students with new measures against exam cheating

How South Korea tackles cheating on its college entrance exam

South Korea's education ministry says 111 candidates were found with unauthorised smart devices during a high-stakes nationwide exam, after officials introduced urgent measures to tackle rising cases of AI-assisted cheating.

Five of the candidates were caught red-handed using smart devices during the exam, authorities said on Friday.

Half a million students sat for the college entry exam, also known as Suneung, which took place this week under the shadow of several recent high-profile cheating scandals.

Prior to the Suneung, authorities announced anti-cheating measures such as emergency alerts sent to every single mobile phone in the country to detect unauthorised devices in exam halls.

On Friday, President Lee Jae Myung called for an overhaul of the exam system to tackle AI-assisted cheating.

In South Korea, public examinations "significantly affect the distribution of opportunities and resources", Lee said, adding that using AI to cheat in such tests "rocks the basic order of our society by undermining fairness and trust".

Concerns over academic dishonesty had been brewing in recent weeks, after reports of candidates cheating with the help of AI devices.

Last month, two candidates were caught cheating with AI glasses during university entrance exams.

The education ministry said earlier this month that four students across middle and high schools were caught using AI glasses to cheat this year.

These fears came to a boil over the weekend, when the top scorer of South Korea's certified tax accountancy exam admitted to cheating by using a miniature camera, mobile phone and AI tools.

His confession came amid speculation over his unusually high score, with just one out of 160 questions wrong.

"I used a microcamera to photograph the test questions and sent them to an AI, then checked my answers on my smartphone to commit the cheating," said the candidate, 34-year-old Han Won-seok, in a video published on Sunday.

His confession, days ahead of the Suneung, prompted authorities to introduce last-minute measures to prevent cheating during the national exam.

The measures included nationwide emergency alerts on the exam day.

Three alerts were sent between 08:00 and 15:00 on Thursday, causing phones across the country to blare out in unison - even if they were on silent mode, and even if they belonged to tourists using data roaming.

The phones did not ring out if they were switched off or on airplane mode.

It was the first time that South Korea tapped on its national disaster alert system to catch exam cheaters.

Candidates also had to cover up the buttons on their shirts and jackets to block any hidden cameras, and authorities conducted inspections of eyewear.

All this made even honest test takers paranoid.

Ahead of the Suneung, one high school senior told the Korea Times that he was worried his horn-rimmed spectacles would be mistaken for smart glasses.

Another told news outlet Yonhap that he usually wore glasses but made an effort to put on contact lenses for the Suneung to avoid raising suspicions.

Held every November, the Suneung is an infamous eight-hour marathon of back-to-back exams.

Students attempt about 200 questions across various subjects, which include Korean, maths, English, social and natural sciences, among others.

The country itself comes to a standstill - construction work halts, planes are grounded and military training gets suspended to facilitate an optimal testing environment.

Many South Koreans see the Suneung as a culmination of years of formal education - and a turning point that determines their university placements, careers, and social statuses.

#39. Anthropic shares slump on second day of trading as markets reel from AI sell-off

Nasdaq erases 2026 gains and Asian markets record worst week since 2020 as investors question huge sums being poured into AI build-out

Who are Anthropic’s seven co-founders, now worth $180bn?

Anthropic’s shares fell at the start of its second day of trading, as its record-breaking IPO failed to stem a sell-off in AI-linked companies.

As they dropped, the wider stock market fell with them, with the Nasdaq Composite erasing all its gains for 2026. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 briefly entered correction territory.

Shares in the frontier AI company behind the Claude chatbot, which floated in the US on Thursday, fell by as much as 12% on Friday. They dropped to $85.12, towards the $83 level at which Anthropic priced its stock market debut.

Anthropic’s first two days as a listed company have been volatile. Its shares jumped by 17% on Thursday, after briefly surging by almost 40% when trading in the firm began.

US technology stocks followed Anthropic down on Friday, knocking 2.9% off the value of the Nasdaq share index, with the memory chipmaker SanDisk falling 14% and rival Micron down 12%. The broader S&P 500 share index fell 1.66% in morning trading in New York.

Neil Wilson, a UK investor strategist at Saxo, said there was “just relentless selling in tech”. He warned: “Anthropic shares tumbled -12% to take an $85 handle as the post-IPO euphoria gives way to the reality of a steep sell-off across the tech and AI space that shows no sign of easing just yet.”

Global markets slumped on Friday too; Asian stock markets recorded their worst week since 2020, as shares in big chip companies fell. In Taiwan, stocks dropped 6.7% to hit their lowest point since mid-April, with the chip maker TSMC down about 6%.

The scale of the decline in Taiwan prompted the chair of its stock exchange to pledge to ensure the stable operation ⁠of capital markets, and reassure investors they should remain confident in the fundamentals of Taiwanese companies.

In Japan, the Nikkei index fell 5%, while South Korea’s tech-heavy Kospi index lost 5.5%. Shares in the tech investor SoftBank had their worst day in more than two years, down 14.5% in Tokyo, amid worries around its aggressive bets on AI.

European stock markets posted their biggest weekly loss since March 2025, with the pan-European Stoxx 600 dropping 1.9% on Friday. London’s blue-chip stock index hit a five-month low.

The sell-off followed losses on Wall Street on Thursday, where the main indices all fell by more than 2% on a day dominated by Anthropic’s IPO.

Chris Beauchamp, the chief market analyst at the investing and trading platform IG, said Anthropic could be “draining market liquidity”, as investors sell shares in other companies to raise funds to pay for stock in the newly floated AI firm.

“The slide from yesterday’s highs sends a message that there might be more at work here than investors realise, and while brokers are scrambling to buy more, that liquidity has to come from somewhere, and the other tech names may be the first ones to feel the pressure,” Beauchamp said.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Anthropic’s flotation has failed to ease worries around the AI trade, as investors increasingly question whether companies can deliver the returns necessary to justify the billions of dollars being poured into the AI build out.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior market analyst at Capital.com, said: “The IPO demonstrated that demand for AI exposure remains strong, but it has not been enough to overcome concerns about elevated valuations, profitability and the sustainability of investment spending.

“The broader market mood has shifted from buying the dip towards questioning whether the correction has further to run. The weakness in equal-weighted indices suggests that selling pressure is no longer confined to a handful of technology giants, while Europe’s recent outperformance has also faded.”

Nvidia shares fell by 7% after California served the chip company with a subpoena as part of an investigation into whether its advanced semiconductors ended up in China.

Britain’s FTSE 100 share index dropped by 5.1% this week, its biggest weekly fall since the start of the Iran war.

Fears that US central bankers may raise interest rates to fight inflation have lifted the dollar to a 19-month high, and pushed down the gold price – with gold on track for its worst week since March.

Bitcoin hit its lowest level in four months, dropping below the $60,000 point for the first time since early July.

#36. Catherine Zeta-Jones' daughter says parents warned her about 'nepo baby' label

Carys Douglas said her Oscar-winning parents taught her that "building my own identity depended on me and no one else"

Catherine Zeta-Jones's daughter has said her parents warned her people might have "preconceived notions" about her embarking on an acting career, as she shared her views on the "nepo baby" label.

Carys Douglas, 23, made her stage debut this year, playing the lead role of Nina in Anton Chekhov's The Seagull in Brooklyn, New York.

Zeta-Jones, who is married to Michael Douglas, son of Hollywood star Kirk, previously said the "nepo baby" term was "horrific". It refers to children of famous parents perceived to have achieved riches and success off their parents' backs.

But the young actor said her parents taught her to build her "own identity" and she has learned how to deal with the "pressure".

During an interview with Elle Spain, Carys Douglas said her parents "do not hide" from her that people would have preconceived ideas about her.

"They taught me that building my own identity depended on me and no one else. It was my responsibility, and it still is.

"As time has gone on, I've come to understand that there are different ways to deal with that kind of pressure," she said.

Her father, star of hit 1980s films such as Wall Street and Fatal Attraction, recently stated that he is not a fan of the "nepo baby" label, calling it an "unfortunate title".

"I guess you could say that about any business. The same thing with Hollywood royalty. My father's father was a junkman," Douglas, 82, said, referring to Kirk, who was considered a Hollywood Golden Age icon.

Zeta-Jones, 57, from Mumbles, Swansea, also criticised the phrase, saying it was "horrible because these kids didn't ask to be born into this, they're finding their own way in life".

"You have to go with your dreams, your passion," Zeta-Jones said.

She added: "But they love it, and they're good. Otherwise, Michael and I would be the first to go, 'you know what? Maybe law school looks really good'."

Catherine Zeta Jones has declared the phrase "nepo baby" is "horrific"

Zeta-Jones' big break came when she landed a West End role in 42nd Street in 1987, before she became a household name in the UK in 1991 after starring in the ITV hit The Darling Buds Of May.

She has since become a global superstar, with roles in The Mask Of Zorro, Entrapment, Traffic and 2002's adaptation of Chicago, for which she won an Oscar for Best Supporting Actress.

The couple's children, Dylan and Carys, have both followed in their footsteps into acting.

Michael Douglas said his children have been performing "since they were in kindergarten".

"Every play, every show, summer theater camps, graduating... I mean, they've done so much work. It's unbelievable," he said.

Carys Douglas described Three Cliffs Bay in Gower Peninsula, Swansea, as "stunning"

Douglas and Zeta-Jones' children predominantly grew up in the US, but the family regularly travels to Wales, a place they are fond of.

Speaking to British Vogue last November, Carys Douglas said her summers were spent reading on her grandmother's sofa and travelling around the Gower Peninsula in Swansea.

"I feel so lucky that this is where I'm from," she told the magazine, reflecting on the "weird but magical places" scattered across the peninsula.

"Those beaches are some of the most beautiful in the world," she said.

She also spoke about her "theatrical" family's traditions, recalling how "there'd be lots of little competitions for singing and pantomime shows all summer".

"It's true, the Welsh love to sing," she added.

#33. Caribbean nations fear economic impact of shuttering citizenship program after EU threats

St Kitts and Nevis to wind down citizenship-by-investment program after EU warns of end to visa-free access

The decision by St Kitts and Nevis to end a lucrative programme offering citizenship to wealthy foreigners in exchange for investment threatens its economy and ability to support people in need, the premier of the island of Nevis has warned.

On Friday, Terrance Drew, the prime minister of the twin-island Caribbean nation, announced he would wind down the citizenship-by-investment (CBI) programme, after the European Union threatened to end visa-free access for St Kitts and Nevis.

Mark Brantley, the premier of Nevis, which has autonomous self-governance in some aspects, said he understood that the “country was given very little choice”, but did not “support the winding down of the program”.

The 42-year-old CBI programme, which St Kitts and Nevis pioneered, allows wealthy foreigners to obtain citizenship and ultimately a passport by investing in infrastructure and other development projects. It has brought billions of dollars into the country and in some years accounted for the largest share of government revenue.

In June, the European Commission wrote to the five Caribbean nations that currently operate CBI programmes: St Kitts and Nevis, Dominica, Antigua and Barbuda, Grenada and St Lucia. It warned that countries may lose visa-free access to its 29-country Schengen area if they did not phase out CBI.

In Friday’s statement, Drew blamed concerns over the programme on mismanagement by previous administrations, including years of underselling to undercut other countries, a controversial prison project, weak escrow protections and investment sums not being properly paid into the citizenship unit.

He said his government had led major reforms to the Caribbean’s programmes with international partners, including the US treasury, but argued that continuing the programme risked visa-free travel to parts of Europe and access to international banking. That could disrupt services allowing visitors to use overseas cards and families abroad to send money home.

Brantley, however, argued it was unfair for the EU to force St Kitts and Nevis to make a move without offering assistance to navigate the transition to other industries.

“I have not yet seen from the EU any corresponding suggestions of assistance. And we have to realise that these citizenship-by-investment programs, not only in St Kitts and Nevis, but … smaller countries of the Caribbean, have contributed significantly to our gross domestic product and also to ensuring that our people … can get adequate access to water and to education, to healthcare and the like,” he said.

Brantley described the 1 June 2028 deadline the EU gave the country to phase out the lucrative programme as “frankly ludicrous”.

In his statement, Drew said the CBI programme “did a lot of good, but it also created a danger”.

“When one source pays for half or more of everything, you are at its mercy. If it stops, everything stops. That is putting all of your eggs in one basket,” he added.

The prime minister said the government aimed to diversify its economy by strengthening tourism, yachting, and digital and creative industries, among others. He added that the sale of clean, geothermal energy was “at the heart of it” with drilling and exploration already planned.

“I will not pretend that the road is easy. Less CBI money means we must spend wisely and plan carefully. We will. Our plan is to make this change by growing our economy. Raising taxes is not part of that plan,” Drew said.

Antigua and Barbuda has also expressed willingness to end the programme, though it is trying to negotiate a longer transition period. The five Caribbean governments have taken differing positions on the EU demand. According to local media, Grenada has indicated it wants its programme to continue until 2032, while Dominica and St Lucia have responded to Brussels without publicly committing to ending their programmes.

Jermine Abel in Basseterre contributed reporting

#30. Indian man sentenced to 19-plus years in plot to assassinate US-based Sikh separatist leader

Nikhil Gupta (seen in a 2024 courtroom sketch) pleaded guilty in a plot to kill Gurpatwant Singh Pannun, a U.S. resident who advocated for a sovereign Sikh state in northern India

A federal judge sentenced an Indian man to 235 months, more than 19 years, in an attempted plot to kill a leading Sikh political figure in the US.

Nikhil Gupta, an Indian national, pleaded guilty to murder-for-hire, conspiracy to commit murder-for-hire, and conspiracy to commit money laundering in February. On Friday, the judge said he likely will be deported or removed after his sentence.

US prosecutors alleged Gupta was directed by an Indian government official to kill Gurpatwant Singh Pannun, a leading figure of the Khalistan movement, which advocates for an independent homeland for Sikhs.

India has denied involvement. Its government has accused Pannun of being a terrorist, which he denies.

Pannun, an American citizen, is the spokesman for US-based Sikh separatist group Sikhs for Justice (SFJ), banned in India since 2019.

He previously told the BBC that "Nikhil Gupta's guilty plea was judicial confirmation that India's Modi government orchestrated a structured murder-for-hire assassination plot on American soil".

India has opposed the Khalistan movement, and its political parties also remain opposed to the matter. Sikhs are a religious minority that make up about 2% of India's population. Some groups have long called for a separate homeland for Sikhs.

Prosecutors alleged that in May 2023, Gupta was recruited by Vikash Yadav, an Indian intelligence officer, to carry out an assassination of Pannun.

Yadav allegedly provided Gupta with personal information about Pannun, including his address in New York and information about his daily activities, prosecutors said.

At his handler's direction, Gupta contacted someone to facilitate the assassination, but that person was actually a US government informant, prosecutors said. The informant introduced Gupta to a "hitman", who was actually an undercover officer with the Drug Enforcement Agency.

Gupta was arrested in June 2023 at an airport in Prague, Czech Republic and extradited to the US. He has been held at a jail in New York pending his sentencing Friday at a Manhattan court.

"He thought that from outside this country he could kill someone in it without consequence, simply for exercising their American right to free speech," then-US attorney Jay Clayton said in a statement, after announcing Gupta's guilty plea.

Following Gupta's arrest, a spokesperson for India's Ministry of External Affairs said that Yadav is no longer employed, external by the Indian government.

The country also set up an inquiry committee to address security concerns raised by the US, the spokesperson said.

Narinder Singh Randhawa, a Sikh separatist and successor of Hardeep Singh Nijjar as temple leader in Surrey, British Columbia, told the BBC the sentencing marks the "first time we see justice" against what he calls historic and systemic persecution of Sikhs by the Indian government in India and abroad.

Inderjeet Gosal, another Sikh separatist advocate based in Ontario, called the sentencing "a big community event".

"It's for all the community to finally witness someone getting caught red-handed."

Indian man admits plot to kill US-based Sikh separatist leader

Who was Canadian Sikh leader Hardeep Singh Nijjar?

Why are some Sikhs calling for a separate state?

Canada accuses India of role in Sikh leader's murder

India crushed a violent armed Sikh insurgency in the 1980s and 1990s aimed at demanding the creation of Khalistan. The movement has little support in India now although some in the Sikh diaspora continue to advocate for Khalistan.

Pannun is facing serious charges, including in connection with terrorism, in India, but he denies the allegations, saying he is an activist who just believes in Khalistan.

In a victim impact statement to the court, Pannun said the foiled assassination attempt has not deterred his activism.

"I am entirely willing to take India's bullets if that is the price required to achieve freedom," he wrote.

Gupta's case placed increased scrutiny on a separate incident, involving the 2023 murder of a Sikh separatist leader in British Columbia, which led to increased tensions between India and Canada.

In September 2023, then-Canadian Prime Minister Justin Trudeau said there were "credible allegations potentially linking" India to the murder of Hardeep Singh Nijjar, another advocate for the creation of Khalistan and an associate of Pannun's.

Nijjar was shot and killed by two assailants outside a Sikh temple in British Columbia. India also has denied involvement in his death.

The day after Niijar's murder, US prosecutors allege that Gupta told the undercover officer Nijjar "was also the target" and "we have so many targets."

#28. Hegseth uses Venezuela visit to boast of Maduro’s capture by US military

Defense secretary asserts US dominance on trip that would have seemed ‘impossible, almost unbelievable’ a year ago

Pete Hegseth has used his first visit to Venezuela since US special forces abducted its former president, Nicolás Maduro, to boast of the dictator’s capture during what he called “the single most sophisticated military operation in the history of Latin America”.

Maduro was controversially seized by Delta Force operatives in the early hours of 3 January, setting in motion an improbable rapprochement between Washington and Venezuela’s anti-imperialist “Chavista” regime, which has ruled since 1999.

Speaking outside the recently reopened US embassy in Venezuela’s capital, Caracas, on Wednesday, Hegseth said: “It’s a great honor to be here as the first United States secretary of war this century to set foot in Venezuela.​ Just one year ago my presence here … would have been deemed impossible, almost unimaginable.

“In fact, exactly one year ago today … [Maduro] stood on a stage not too far from here and yelled mockingly, ‘Come and get me!’” Hegseth recalled, before adding sarcastically: “Well, we answered that call.

“The reality is that … January 3 is the day that will go down in history as a day of liberation for Venezuela,” Hegseth claimed, although Maduro’s allies continue to hold power with US support.

Earlier in the day, Hegseth was welcomed to the Miraflores Palace by Maduro’s former vice-president, Delcy Rodríguez, who became acting president with Donald Trump’s blessing after his abduction.

In the aftermath of the deadly US attack, Hegseth was one of the bluntest Trump administration voices when it came to celebrating the invasion. “Maduro had his chance … he effed around and he found out,” the defense secretary bragged.

Speaking at the palace, Hegseth struck a more conciliatory tone as he vowed to deepen cooperation with Venezuela’s new leaders in brief comments that were broadcast by the state broadcaster VTV.

“I stand here on behalf of President Trump, who sends his greeting and looks forward to the opportunities, not just militarily … but also economically: the incredible resource capabilities that your country has, that we look forward to unlocking together with you,” Hegseth told Rodríguez and senior cabinet members, including the defense and foreign ministers, Gustavo González López and Oliver Blanco.

Hegseth said his visit to Rodríguez’s “beautiful country” was designed to re-establish and increase “defense cooperation” between “two great militaries” that less than a year ago were briefly at war. Earlier this month, the New York Times reported that Trump officials were trying to negotiate a long-term US troop presence in Venezuela as part of their push for regional hegemony.

“I appreciate your leadership and commitment to your country,” Hegseth told Maduro’s successor, a once outspoken anti-imperialist leftist who has overseen an unlikely realignment with Washington since her ally was grabbed.

During her five-minute address, Rodríguez, whose oil-rich country is widely seen to have become a US protectorate after January’s invasion, made no mention of Maduro, who is behind bars in New York City.

But referring indirectly to his capture and the subsequent reconciliation with Washington, she told Hegseth: “Thanks to the political and geopolitical maturity of our governments, we have – in a very short period of time, just a few months – turned what were very difficult and very tough times for our people, into an extraordinary space for cooperation and mutual benefit.”

Rodríguez hailed the “historic” energy deal her government recently struck with Trump, who has called it “the biggest oil deal in world history” as well as “the spoils” of his “conquest” of Venezuela.

Hegseth claimed the agreement, which reportedly involves 65bn barrels of reserves, “makes the strait of Hormuz a non-factor for the western hemisphere”.

Critics of Rodríguez’s political movement painted Hegseth’s visit as a humiliating act of genuflection for a president with no choice but to roll out the red carpet for the man whose troops captured her boss.

Writing on X, Luz Mely Reyes, a prominent Venezuelan journalist, said Hegseth’s visit was designed to underscore Washington’s “direct administration” of her country.

“Ten months after Nicolás Maduro’s capture, Washington no longer needs to fire shots to remind everyone who is in charge: the mere presence on Venezuelan soil of the architect of the January incursion reaffirms that the country’s operational sovereignty is subject to the White House’s directives,” Reyes said.

Reyes suspected the visit also carried a message for members of Venezuela’s armed forces. “Domestically, the trip forces the military high command to legitimize the man who directed the extraction of their former commander in chief,” she said. “In this regard, I wonder whether it might be a message of discipline intended to prevent any pockets of resistance in the barracks.”

Trump’s attempts to expand his Shield of the Americas anti-cartel coalition of Latin American governments suffered a major blow last month with the defeat of his far-right ally, Flávio Bolsonaro, in Brazil’s presidential election. The leftwing winner, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, has indicated his country will not join the initiative.

But Venezuela’s interim government is in little position to resist. During his surprise visit to Venezuela, Hegseth said Venezuela had gone from being “our most aggressive regional threat to [being] a compliant security partner”. He praised Rodríguez for working “closely, pragmatically, and diligently with the United States to stabilize Venezuela”. Hegseth claimed US and Venezuelan armed forces would work together to attack “narco-terrorists” operating on Venezuelan soil, vowing: “We will hunt you down. We will find you. And we will kill you.”

Concluding her speech at the palace, Rodríguez told her foe-turned-friend: “Thank you, secretary, and I wish you a lifetime of happiness.”

#25. Texas death-row inmate screams as execution takes more than one hour

Reinaldo Dennes was convicted over the murder of a diamond dealer in 1996

A US death row inmate has screamed in apparent pain during his Texas execution which lasted more than an hour.

This is "not working the way it should be," Reinaldo Dennes, 70, said as he was being injected with the drug pentobarbital, according to witnesses.

A spokeswoman for the Texas Department of Criminal Justice said there had been unsuccessful attempts to find veins for the injections, but added that Dennes had reported pain in parts of his body where there were no attempted injections.

Dennes's case comes weeks after the botched execution of Christa Pike, who survived two lethal doses and is believed to be the only individual to have survived an execution attempt by lethal injection.

Dennes was sentenced to death over the 1996 murder of Janos Szucs, a diamond dealer who was found shot multiple times.

His execution came on the same day another inmate was put to death in Florida.

Executions with a lethal drug usually last from a few minutes to over an hour.

Michael Graczyk, an Associated Press reporter who has witnessed many such executions, described how they had waited for an unusually long time before being taken into the chamber. According to him, such delays are usually due to an inmate resisting the procedure or difficulty in finding veins.

When the witnesses were finally allowed in, Dennes almost immediately exclaimed, "The pain! Go! Go!", Graczyk said. He then uttered an expletive and asked "the father" to "take this pain".

He then shouted: "My right leg is killing me!", describing it as a cramp. After another expletive, he grunted and said, "I don't think this is normal."

Another witness, the reverend Jeff Hood, told USA Today that Dennes's execution was "by far the worst one" he had seen.

"He [Dennes] screamed out, 'my leg, my leg, my leg'," Hood said. "He screamed out twice, 'it's not working like it's supposed to.'"

How widely used is the death penalty in the US?

The use of pentobarbital has been under renewed scrutiny since Pike, a 50-year-old convicted murderer, survived two attempts at execution via the lethal injection in September. She was taken to hospital in a critical condition and has since returned to prison.

Her botched execution captivated and shocked the world.

Pentobarbital is a potent sedative that slows down the body, including the nervous system. Doctors sometimes use it as a treatment for certain conditions, but in high doses it can also be used as a lethal injection to cause death.

Its use in lethal injections came under renewed scrutiny last year after a US federal government review during the Biden administration raised concerns about the drug's potential for "unnecessary pain and suffering".

The Biden government banned its use in federal executions, but that did not bind individual states, which can choose their own methods.

I've seen nearly 500 executions - but never one like Christa Pike's

Could Christa Pike face execution again and by what method?

What happened in Christa Pike's failed US execution, and how did she survive?

#24. Florida pastor found guilty over 2021 assassination of Haitian president

Christian Emmanuel Sanon faces life in prison after conviction on charges arising from killing of Jovenel Moïse

A Florida pastor with dual Haitian-American citizenship was convicted on Wednesday of US charges arising from the 2021 assassination of the Haitian president Jovenel Moïse.

Christian Emmanuel Sanon was found guilty on charges including conspiring to kill Moïse after a nearly eight-week trial in federal court in Miami. He had faced a total of eight charges including conspiracy to kidnap or kill a person outside the United States.

He faces up to life in prison at sentencing.

Federal prosecutors said south Florida was a hub for planning and financing the plot to oust Moïse and install a president of the conspirators’ choosing. The government said Sanon was the conspirators’ initial choice for Moïse’s replacement. Prosecutors also said Sanon helped raise money for the conspiracy and arranged travel to Haiti and lodging for himself and others in that country.

Sanon acknowledged in trial testimony that he was in communication at one point with people trying to replace Moïse, but pointed out that the group had moved on to another candidate long before the assassination. He denied any knowledge of plans to kill Moïse.

Sanon was born in Haiti and moved to the US as a teenager. He testified that he worked with churches in Haitian immigrant communities in Florida and the midwest.

Haiti has not had a president since authorities said that a squad of mostly retired Colombian soldiers killed Moïse and wounded his wife in a night-time raid at their home on 7 July 2021. The poorest country in the Americas, Haiti has a history of dictatorship, political upheaval and foreign intervention that has long hindered democratic rule.

The election of a new president is scheduled for next year.

Prosecutors said the scheme to kill Moïse included paying about two dozen foreign mercenaries with money obtained from US government programs that were intended to help businesses during the Covid pandemic, according to court records. US prosecutors have charged more than 40 people with crimes related to the assassination and a dozen have been convicted, including five who are serving life sentences.

The plot to oust Moïse began taking final shape in April 2021, when Sanon met in south Florida with a former Haitian senator and several US businesspeople, according to an indictment. The group agreed that Sanon, once in power, would award contracts to two Florida-based companies – Counter Terrorist Unit Federal Academy and Counter Terrorist Unit Security, collectively known as CTU – for infrastructure projects, security forces and military equipment, according to investigators.

CTU initially hired about 18 Colombian nationals with military training to provide security for Sanon, according to prosecutors. Conspirators also spent months obtaining weapons and body armor and attempting to build ties with Haitian gangs, officials said.

By June 2021, the conspirators realized Sanon had neither the constitutional qualifications nor sufficient popular support to become president. They shifted their support to Wendelle Coq Thélot, a former Haitian supreme court judge who died in January 2025 while a fugitive.

Sanon was previously scheduled to be tried with four other men who were convicted earlier this year, but his case was separated from theirs and delayed for health reasons.

#22. Race to be next UN secretary general exposes divisions among world powers

Debate about dominance of permanent five members could reignite if major powers block Costa Rican frontrunner

The race to become the next UN secretary general could reach a decisive moment on Friday when a UN security council vote is likely to reveal whether the Costa Rican frontrunner for the post would be blocked by the big powers. If so, the setback will reignite a debate about the dominance of the permanent five (P5) members at the United Nations.

Rebeca Grynspan, an economist and UN insider who is from a family of Polish-Jewish wartime refugees to Costa Rica, has been in the lead in all but one of the eight straw polls of the 15 UN security council members since the first secret ballot was held on 30 July. Each member goes down the list of candidates, casting an “encourage”, “discourage” or “no opinion” vote for each candidate.

Despite countries not being required to reveal how they voted, it is thought Grynspan’s candidature has been resisted by Russia and the US, two of the five permanent security council members that can ultimately wield a veto against a candidate. Knowledge among the security council delegations of how countries are voting is down to private intelligence, leaks and a process of elimination.

The intensive lobbying over the job has spread across the globe, but Friday is especially important since the poll is slated to differentiate the voting of the P5 from the 10 elected members (E10) of the security council. To be recommended as secretary general to the UN general assembly, a candidate needs nine votes and no vetoes. In Friday’s colour-coded secret ballot, the P5 will cast red ballots and the E10 white.

One diplomat said: “The electoral system means it is important how many votes you win, but equally how many of certain votes you don’t lose.”

The protracted 140-day deadlock confirms the polarisation that has hampered the incumbent secretary general, António Guterres, but also belies claims the role is now an irrelevance.

Friday’s secret ballot may also reveal whether the United Arab Emirates’ campaign for its diplomat, Lana Nusseibeh, is about to blow up in its face due to the hostility to the UAE’s brand of active, commercially driven diplomacy, not just in Sudan, but also Somalia, Western Sahara, Libya and Yemen.

A late entrant, in the two straw polls in which she has contested so far, Nusseibeh secured only seven “encourage” votes, six “discourage” and two “no opinions”. This is despite prestigious big-power backing and her acknowledged personal diplomatic skills.

Nusseibeh has US and Russian backing. The French foreign ministry may have misgivings about the UAE, but Emmanuel Macron, the French president, travelled to the UAE in the midst of French riots to see the UAE president, Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan, and secure 100,000 metric tons of diesel a month.

In the UK, the foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, has kept his counsel, but he shares a Polish-Jewish refugee heritage with Grynspan and cannot be an admirer of the UAE’s human rights record or its evasion of EU sanctions on Russia. The prime minister, Andy Burnham, has UAE connections from his time as mayor of Manchester, and Nusseibeh is a fellow alumnus of the University of Cambridge. At the UN she worked closely with the UK in an informal group called “Friends of the Future of the UN”.

Yet 68 British MPs, including the Labour chair of the international development select committee, Sarah Champion, last week signed a letter calling on Burnham to veto Nusseibeh, describing her as “a tainted and compromised figure”.

Some of the opposition to her from elected security council members is not wholly political. Latin American countries believe an informal rotation system means it is their turn to lead the UN. As a result Panama and Colombia, currently on the security council, will oppose her. China has also been supportive of this rotation principle.

But others deeply object to what they consider to be the UAE’s dollar diplomacy and preparedness to use its extraordinary wealth to exacerbate and exploit pre-existing divisions in a country to gain a commercial foothold. Somalia, for instance, has a seat on the Security Council, and its president, Hassan Sheikh Mohamud, this week went on TV to accuse the UAE of buying ports, establishing military bases and supporting armed forces outside Somalia’s national security framework without federal approval.

It is also possible that Finland and Latvia, the two elected members with the greatest stake in Ukraine, are unimpressed that the UAE gave Vladimir Putin red carpet treatment when he visited Abu Dhabi in December 2023, nine months after the international criminal court had issued an arrest warrant for child trafficking. Greece, less engaged in Ukraine, may be more flexible.

Since the last poll, Nusseibeh has been to Bahrain, Britain and Pakistan, a clue as to where extra votes may be harvested. She also had a “thoughtful exchange” with Zeng Jixin, the Chinese ambassador to the UAE.

The two hurdles are whether someone so deep inside the UAE inner circle would have the courage in office to show true independence, and whether her active denials of UAE involvement in arming the RSF in Sudan leave her irretrievably tainted. From a Palestinian intellectual family, she insists she would make peace in Sudan her number one priority.

In the last ballot on 5 November, Grynspan won 12 votes in favour and three negative. But on 16 October – the only colour-coded ballot so far – which distinguishes between permanent and elected members – she received two discourage votes from permanent members, the equivalent of a veto. Nusseibeh had not joined the race at that point.

In an administration as personalised as the US, it has been hard for Grynspan to gain influence, with many sensing that Mike Waltz, the US ambassador to the UN, is the key influencer.

Grynspan has been reluctant to make the fact she is Jewish a feature of her candidacy, but the election of a Jewish person to run the UN might be seen as something the US could embrace as transformative. Her age – she is 70 – could be considered a drawback among those calling for a younger generation to renew the institution.

Although there are other Latin American candidates, Grynspan is seen as a more viable candidate than her rival, the Argentine Rafael Grossi, the head of the UN weapons inspectorate. Russia has opposed Grossi, objecting to his interventions over the Zaporizhzhia nuclear plant in Ukraine.

One way or the other, the logjam has to be broken soon.

#21. Nearly 600 women killed in South Africa within three months

The killing of 13 women around Johannesburg earlier this year sparked a national outcry

Nearly 600 women were killed in South Africa between July and September this year, according to the country's latest crime statistics.

This figure includes 13 women whose partially naked and bruised bodies were found dumped in public areas in and around Johannesburg.

The murders triggered national outrage and police faced pressure to find the culprits, in a country with one of the highest rates of gender-based violence and murder rates in the world.

In the latest shocking killing, five women and a toddler were gunned down in Free State province earlier this week.

The latest crime statistics, released on Friday, revealed that 581 women were killed in the country between July and September, including 131 in the Gauteng province which includes Johannesburg.

The same statistics revealed a slight decline in the number of murders.

Police reported 5,590 murders, 204 fewer than in the same period last year. This still come to an average of about 60 murders a day.

It is not clear whether the number of women killed is an increase or decrease because the police had stopped giving these figures recently due to what they said were "significant anomalies" in the statistics.

Grim discoveries of murdered women spark fear in South Africa

Acting Police Minister Firoz Cachalia said there needed to be "greater efforts to prevent... violence" against women and children.

According to police in Free State province, south of Johannesburg, three masked men opened fire on a family in the township of Botshabelo on Wednesday night.

The trio were allegedly looking for the owner of the house and when they realised he was not there, "forced their way into the house and started shooting at the people inside", police said.

The owner's wife was also shot in the stomach and was taken to hospital. No arrests have been made.

On Thursday, the police chief for Gauteng province said that eight people had now been arrested in relations to the 13 murders which sparked a national outcry earlier this year.

General Tommy Mthombeni said three of the arrests related to the murder of Elizabeth Moselakgomo, an avid runner whose disappearance prompted a highly publicised nationwide search for her.

The 38-year-old went missing while out for her daily run near her home in Kempton Park.

Her body was found badly bruised and partially clothed on 12 September.

The police chief said two other people had been detained in connection with the death of Jabulile Ntimba, 33, who was discovered half-naked and wrapped in a sheet on the side of a road in Boksburg on 17 September. Ntimba's was the ninth body found in the Ekurhuleni region.

He said another person had been arrested for one of the Kempton Park cases while a suspect was arrested over Ntombifuthi Nxumalo's death in Tembisa, north-east of central Johannesburg.

Nxumalo was discovered at a cemetery on 26 September. She was found naked with serious head injuries as well as stones near her body.

Another arrest was made back in July, in relation to the death of Busisiwe Mahlangu, whose body was found in Kempton Park on 15 July. Hers was the first of the 13 bodies discovered in the area.

In September, President Cyril Ramaphosa vowed that "no stone will be left unturned" to find those behind the killings.

Last year, he declared that violence against women was a "national disaster".

#18. ‘Weak and dizzy’: the secretive ICE force-feeding tactics that ended a Cuban father’s hunger strike

Exclusive: Luis Alejandro Cora Pérez says in messages to Guardian from inside Texas facility pain of force-feeding caused him to break strike

The text message that came from inside the Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) detention center was stark: “I have spent five days eating regular food. If I don’t eat it, they put the tube in me to feed me.”

A Cuban asylum seeker, Luis Alejandro Cora Pérez, 32, said in messages to the Guardian this month that he was still weak and dizzy after three months without eating, but that he had broken his hunger strike because being force-fed was just too painful.

The Trump administration responded to requests for comment by denying it had force-fed Cora Pérez. But the detainee sent more details – and his is far from an isolated case.

Cora Pérez was one of two men on hunger striker at a Texas facility for whom ICE sought and received a federal judge’s permission to force-feed in recent weeks, a Guardian investigation found.

Cora Pérez and the other man were locked up inside the Port Isabel service processing center, an ICE detention facility in south Texas, just a few miles from the US-Mexico border, on the coast near where Elon Musk’s SpaceX launches rockets. Cora Pérez began a hunger strike in August to protest against his prolonged detention by federal immigration officials while his case plays out. At first he only drank water, then after three weeks, he says he began to drink one Ensure nutritional shake per day.

On 30 October, court records show ICE received permission to forcibly feed and hydrate him, and to restrain him while doing so.

The next day he says six guards at the center held him down, while medical staffers inserted a feeding tube through his nose and down his throat, and pumped nutritional liquid into his stomach – a process he called “traumatizing, both physically and psychologically”.

On 3 November, ICE then received permission from a federal judge to force-feed another hunger striker at Port Isabel, from Uzbekistan, federal court records show. The DHS says the Uzbek man has not been subjected to the practice, however.

On Wednesday night, after weeks of communications, Cora Pérez was suddenly transferred to another detention center in Texas. His relative says neither of them were alerted to any plans to transfer him, or a reason for it.

The DHS did not specify why Cora Pérez was transferred, adding that ICE makes custody determinations on a “case-by-case” basis based on “various factors”.

The revelations come as ICE’s detention practices continue to come under fire amid allegations of mistreatment, and as an increasing number of sporadic hunger strikes have been breaking out in ICE facilities across the country.

The two men’s cases in Texas, revealed here for the first time, are some of the most recent instances of government lawyers seeking a federal judge’s permission, as required by ICE policy, and receiving the go-ahead to force-feed detained hunger strikers. The Guardian’s reporting is based on court records, interviews, dozens of messages exchanged and an interview conducted with Cora Pérez via a facility phone. It further reveals how the Trump administration is increasingly obtaining such court orders to force-feed detained immigrants, a secretive practice widely condemned as torture by human rights organizations and medical ethics groups.

Detainees driven to hunger strike are typically using it as a last resort to protest against poor conditions and the Trump administration’s preference for arrest and prolonged detention while their civil immigration cases proceed through the system.

Cora Pérez said detention center staff inserted the feeding tube and left it in his body, a not uncommon practice, according to experts, and introduced nutrition – first a “clear liquid”, followed by a “milky liquid”, he recalled – through the tube twice more that day, before he cracked and said he broke his hunger strike, citing what he called “horrific torture”. He began voluntarily drinking nutritional shakes the following day, he said.

“ICE does not hold back on anything, and they do anything they want, like we have seen, even killing their own US citizens,” he wrote to the Guardian, referring to the fatal shootings of Renee Good by an ICE officer and Alex Pretti by a border patrol officer in January of this year during Trump’s pivotal immigration crackdown on Minneapolis.

Detained hunger strikers subjected to involuntary medical procedures, including force-feeding, have rarely spoken out about their cases or revealed their identities, much less while still detained. Cora Pérez’s messages offer a rare glimpse at conditions inside a detention center’s restricted medical unit where hunger strikers and suicidal detainees are held.

“They just took [the Uzbek man] in an ambulance after [him] fainting in front of the medical lobby where they take our vitals, our weight and our temperature,” Cora Pérez said in an alarmed message last Monday morning.

The Uzbek man was returned the following day to the facility, Cora Pérez said.

“I see him every day because I see him in the room in front of me, but they don’t let us talk to each other,” he added.

The Guardian independently confirmed the Uzbek’s man identity but is not publicly naming him, because efforts to contact him have been unsuccessful. The man arrived at the Mexico-US border in April 2022 and was released into the country while navigating his immigration case. He was arrested by border patrol officials in April of this year and placed in detention. The man does not appear to have any criminal record and the DHS did not point to any criminal history.

In a statement, DHS said that after they received the court’s permission to force-feed the Uzbek man, he “has voluntarily taken in fluids and there has been NO force-feeding or forced hydration”.

Security operations at the sprawling Port Isabel facility in Los Fresnos, Texas, are run by a private prison company, Akima Global Services, while healthcare is primarily handled by government workers.

“ICE respects detainees’ rights to exercise self-expression and medical autonomy by refusing nourishment and medical care,” the DHS said in a statement. “ICE Health Services Corps may provide involuntary medical treatment only to prevent imminent life-threatening harm or death. Medical staff may provide treatment only to medically stabilize the patient, bringing the detainee outside the imminent harm, incapacitation, or death.”

The US attorney’s office in the southern district of Texas, which represented ICE in its force-feeding request, did not respond to multiple requests for comment. Virginia-headquartered Akima Global Services, and its parent company Nana Regional Corporation, based in Alaska, did not respond to a request for comment.

Force-feeding cases are shrouded in secrecy. After a detainee launches a hunger strike, ICE often sequesters them in medical isolation cells for monitoring, according to ICE’s detention standards. The DHS said that ICE continues to offer hunger strikers three meals per day.

After a certain period of not eating, which varies per case, ICE will request a federal judge’s permission to force-feed the hunger striker. Many court records, and sometimes entire court dockets, are sealed and hidden from public view. In Cora Pérez and the Uzbek man’s cases, like in others, they did not have any legal representation to challenge the force-feeding requests.

The Guardian found at least five instances in which ICE requested permission to force-feed hunger strikers in Port Isabel since January 2025. The federal judge who ruled on Cora Perez and the Uzbek man, Rolando Olvera, has similarly ordered in at least three other cases, records show. Olvera’s judicial assistant said the court had no comment for this article.

ICE is known to have requested permission to force-feed at least 21 detainees across the US since January 2025, the Guardian found, but the number is probably higher since an unknown number of records and dockets remain sealed, unsearchable and not feasible to locate. Most of the cases coinciding with the second Trump administration and tracked by the Guardian are in Texas and Florida, with others in Georgia, Arizona and Washington state. Under the Biden administration, ICE force-feeding requests were also made in Louisiana and California.

Amy Zeidan, an emergency medicine physician based in Atlanta and an expert on healthcare in immigration detention, said force-feeding hunger strikers can amount to “torture”, and lead to significant psychological distress. She also said it was a violation of medical ethics.

“Essentially it’s like inserting a straw into your nose,” Zeidan said. “You have a gag reflex, so most times people will gag, and that’s really uncomfortable. And people will even vomit repeatedly if it continues to irritate their gag reflex.”

Zeidan added that the forceful insertion of a feeding tube can lead to the swelling of the nostrils and the throat, and even tear them, leading to bleeding.

“It is like torture – somebody is doing a painful procedure to you that you don’t consent to,” Zeidan added. “Autonomy is a fundamental principle of medical ethics. We can’t do something to patients if they don’t want to, even if we think it’s good for them.”

There are numerous cells and rooms inside the Port Isabel medical unit, including isolation cells and shared rooms for ill detainees, according to Cora Pérez. In recent weeks, there were five people in the Port Isabel medical isolation cells used for hunger striking or suicidal detainees, he added. He described the cells as “practically freezing”, constantly surveilled with a security camera, and with just a bed, toilet and sink. As well as himself, he says he observed the Uzbek man, a Middle Eastern man, a “tall” eastern European man and a Black man of unknown nationality in the isolation cells. Unit detainees are escorted by facility staff to showers and sometimes receive yard and television access, he said.

Cora Pérez worries for the other detainees. The Black man, whose identity or nationality he does not know, seems to have “serious mental health problems”, Cora Pérez observed.

“He harms himself and spends the night yelling and hitting the door, and does not let anyone sleep,” he said.

The Guardian has independently confirmed the eastern European and Middle Eastern men’s identities, but is not publicly naming them because efforts to contact them have been unsuccessful. The DHS did not comment on the other men inside the medical isolation unit, saying they could not comment without the Guardian providing their identities.

Cora Pérez had left his son and parents in Cuba and requested asylum at the Mexico-US border in 2023, according to a relative who spoke to the Guardian on condition of anonymity due to fear of retaliation by immigration authorities. Cora Pérez went to live with the relative in Florida, where he worked in maintenance at a hotel and as a food delivery worker, he said.

In March 2025, Cora Pérez was arrested by local police and later convicted of two misdemeanors, including “breach of peace” and “obstructing an officer without violence”, according to local records. He was sentenced to two months in jail, his relative said. But ICE placed a hold on him and as he was being released in June 2025, immigration officials took him into custody – a common system – and soon transferred him to the Adelanto detention center, a privately-run facility in California.

Days in Adelanto turned to weeks, then months, then a over year, according to a federal court order denying one of his petitions requesting his release. His multiple requests to immigration and ordinary federal courts for release from detention, while he fights his asylum case, have been unsuccessful.

“All his claims will be heard by an immigration judge, and he will receive due process. This administration will not ignore the rule of law,” DHS said. “Criminal illegal aliens are not welcome in the US.”

In August, he stopped eating to protest against his protracted detention.

“I have spent two birthdays behind ICE’s bars,” he wrote. “The reasons I did the hunger strike in Adelanto are for my political asylum, for the time I have spent detained, [and] because I have a 14-year-old son who is starving to death in Cuba because he does not have my help.

“Also due to the mistreatment by the officials,” he added.

Cora Pérez did not tell his family about the hunger strike, so as not to further worry them. It was not until he was transferred to Port Isabel in late September that he confessed to his relative he had been on a prolonged hunger strike.

“He doesn’t share details so that I won’t get upset or worry,” the relative said in a phone interview.

Cora Pérez’s mother, who is in Cuba, “does not know anything, because she is sick, and with his detention she has gotten worse”, the relative added, between tears. “I don’t want her nor his grandmother to know anything about it,” the relative added.

On 30 October, the federal prosecutors requested permission for ICE to forcibly feed and hydrate Cora Pérez. Hours later, a federal judge approved the procedures without hearing from Cora Pérez. Cora Pérez told the Guardian in various instances he felt it was unfair he was not given the opportunity to present his case to the judge before the judge approved his force-feeding.

“It’s very disturbing because the judge is basically making a decision on information that is only provided by one side,” said Linus Chan, a professor of clinical law and the director of the detainee rights clinic at the University of Minnesota law school. “That just seems ridiculous.”

Cora Pérez was force-fed the following day, he said in his messages and in a phone call, adding that he did not physically resist because he “had seen a lot of mistreatment of others detained”.

“[ICE does] what they want with us, whatever they want, because a federal judge gave them permission,” he added. “I have suffered on the island [of Cuba] and now at the hands of ICE.”

After asking Cora Pérez about the DHS’s denials that he was force-fed, he repeated that he was. “Brother, ICE says and does whatever they want. They deny, punish, retaliate and do everything at their own will. They have even sent Cubans to Africa,” he said.

The number of people detained by ICE has soared under the Trump administration. And due to policy and legal changes, tens of thousands of people are forced to stay in detention for long periods while they fight their immigration cases.

Chan, from the University of Minnesota, says prolonged detention and delays in their cases lead to logical conclusion. “That’s exactly when people protest,” he said.

“And the only form of protest, for many of these people, is a hunger strike,” he said.

As Trump’s hardline immigration crackdown continues and people are detained as a first resort while their cases play out, Chan said: “Hunger strikes will become more common.”

#16. Anthropic shares fall after blockbuster opening as wider tech sell-off continues

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic's shares have dropped back towards their initial price after a soaring first day on the stock market, as a wider tech sell-off deepens.

The fall in tech stocks continued to weigh on markets, with the S&P 500 down more than 2% and the Dow Jones Industrial Average down 0.7% by lunchtime.

Both US indexes touched correction territory, a point where they are trading at 10% or more down from a recent high.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index also fell more than 3.4% by the middle of trading on Friday.

The speed at which AI investment has been growing - with more than $1 trillion spent in the last year alone by a small handful of major tech companies - has led to ongoing concerns of a financial bubble. Questions are being raised over whether those investments will pay off, and when.

Despite a positive first day of trading, Anthropic's shares fell on Friday, dropping more than 12% to about $85 by lunchtime, close to its initial public offering (IPO) price of $83.

The artificial intelligence (AI) company's introduction to the Nasdaq stock exchange on Thursday was a crucial test for the industry and was largely seen as a success, with trading starting at $111.50.

The maker of the Claude chatbot raised a record $81bn in its initial public offering (IPO), but its share price moved significantly in the first day of trading.

The shares peaked at $115 on Thursday, before easing to end the day up 17% at $97.17.

Analysts said volatility was to be expected in the first days or even weeks of Anthropic's trading, as the company was the first frontier AI firm to go public so had no direct peers.

Some have also noted the similarities with Elon Musk's SpaceX, which at the time of its debut in June was the largest IPO on record. Shares in the rocket and AI company experienced large swings in its first few weeks of trading.

Most tech companies see their shares fall after hitting the stock market, often after reporting quarterly results for the first time.

But many, including Meta, Amazon and Google, have gone on to become the largest and most profitable corporations in the world.

Ed Moya, a strategist at market intelligence firm AlphaSense, said Anthropic's blockbuster IPO did not appear to have restored confidence in the broader AI market.

While AI demand was still very strong, Moya told the BBC that investors were trying to answer the question of how much were they willing to pay for that growth.

"As we've gone through the past past few weeks, it seems that there's been more risks that have surfaced," he said.

"Right now you're starting to see Wall Street is trying to shift from rewarding growth at any price, to demanding stronger cash flow and a clearer path to profitability."

Shares in some of the largest US tech companies also fell on Friday.

Nvidia and SpaceX were both down more than 6%, while Tesla shares fell more than 3% and Meta was down 3.4%.

Apple, Amazon and Microsoft experienced smaller falls, while Alphabet was up by 1.2%.

Europe's main stock markets - which contain fewer tech stocks - also took a hit.

Germany's Dax index closed down 2.6%, France's CAC 40 was down 1.9% and the UK's FTSE 100 ended trading 1% lower.

Earlier on Friday, tech-heavy Asian markets saw steep falls.

South Korea's Kospi and Japan's Nikkei 225 indexes closed down about 5%, with shares in memory chip makers Sk Hynix, Kioxia and Tokyo Electron, and other tech firms such as Samsung Electronics, seeing losses.

Taiwan's stock market dropped about 6% on Friday, taking the weekly fall to more than 17% and what was reported to be its biggest weekly fall since 1990.

#13. European Commission accused of sugarcoating figures to release funds to Egypt

Leaked documents raise questions about claims Egypt met welfare targets to qualify for €2.5bn in loans

The European Commission has been accused of sugarcoating figures to enable it to release funds to Egypt under a landmark pact with the authoritarian government of Abdel Fatah al-Sisi.

The charge relates to a €7.4bn deal promised by Ursula von der Leyen, the European Commission president, to al-Sisi on a high-profile visit to Cairo in 2024. The three-year “strategic and comprehensive partnership” was intended to bolster Egypt’s shaky economy, deter migrants from coming to Europe and shore up a region reeling from the 7 October attacks and Israel’s war on Gaza.

The disclosure raises broader questions about the EU-Egypt relationship, as Egyptian authorities face scrutiny over shrinking press freedom following the jailing and alleged torture of six journalists, the entire newsroom of Matsadaash, a factchecking and investigative platform, and the closure last month of one of the country’s last independent news outlets and conviction of its high-profile editor, Lina Attalah.

Attalah is appealing against that conviction and will be in court this week as she seeks to overturn other charges including “publishing false news”, for which she was sentenced to a year in prison in absentia without her knowledge.

The spotlight is on €5bn in EU “soft” loans to Egypt – lending with easy repayment terms, known as macro-financial assistance, that is meant to be available only to countries that respect human rights and democracy. While the EU conditions on democracy and rights are vague, the loans impose much more specific requirements on Egypt to carry out economic reforms.

Separate data sources – from leaked EU documents and deleted from an Egyptian government website – raise questions about the European Commission’s claims that Egypt met targets linked to social welfare programmes, which enabled the EU to release €2.5bn in assistance.

In confidential documents, the commission told EU lawmakers that Egypt had “broadly fulfilled” and then “fulfilled” two separate targets to increase the numbers of poor people on social welfare programmes between 2024 and 2026. But data points to a net reduction of 85,734 households as the Egyptian government removed people from its welfare programmes rather than expanded coverage.

If Egypt had expanded the programmes in line with the EU’s apparent conditions – and not removed any households – about 245,000 households, equivalent to roughly 909,000 people, would be getting these benefits. (The Guardian used World Bank data to make this rough calculation).

According to the leaked documents, the commission wanted Egypt to “further expand cash transfer programmes”, but despite a net reduction in beneficiaries it gave the green light anyway to two separate “soft” loans totalling €2.5bn.

In response to the Guardian’s findings, one of the European parliament’s leading spokespeople on EU-Egypt relations, the Dutch MEP Tineke Strik, said she was introducing a question to the European Commission demanding clarification.

“The finding by this investigation that the commission is seemingly sugarcoating Egypt’s performance is worrying,” Strik said. “From the beginning, the commission has used macro-financial assistance primarily as a tool to lure Egypt into migration cooperation, while considering formal conditions and its reporting obligations towards parliament as an inconvenient formality.”

Under the plan, Egypt has so far received €3.5bn in soft loans in three instalments, with a final payment of €1.5bn expected before the end of 2027.

The charge of sugarcoating figures relates to two social welfare programmes: the Takaful and Karama cash transfer programmes for poor and vulnerable households.

The Takaful (Solidarity) programme gives poor families cash payments as long as recipients have regular health visits and good attendance at school. The Karama (Dignity) programme gives unconditional cash payments to people living with a disability, older people, orphans and widows.

One of the EU conditions for Egypt to gain the first €1bn loan was to increase the number of Takaful and Karama beneficiary households by 100,000 to 4.7 million.

In a leaked report to lawmakers in the EU council of ministers and the European parliament, the commission described that requirement as “broadly fulfilled”.

The data in that report, however, reveals a net fall of 62,281 households during this period, owing to what the EU officials describe as “a multi-annual recertification process”. Some beneficiaries were added to the list but a larger number were removed because they were deemed ineligible.

In order to achieve a later instalment of €1.5bn, Egypt was required to “further expand” the programmes, adding 60,000 households to the Takaful and Karama beneficiaries list. A second leaked report to EU lawmakers shows that the commission concluded the target was “fulfilled” because 442,118 families were added to the list.

On this occasion, the commission did not report on numbers removed. Figures from Egypt’s ministry of social solidarity, however, show a net reduction of 23,453 beneficiaries to the programmes in the 11 months until June 2026, the period leading up to the release of that payment. The EU announced the release of the €1.5bn payment in July 2026.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

The data has since been deleted from the Egyptian ministry website but the Guardian has seen screenshots and archived webpages.

During a period from 2024 to 26 when the EU required Egypt to add 160,000 households to the rolls of the Karama and Takaful programmes, there was a net decline of 85,734 households.

Timothy Kaldas, the deputy director of the Tahrir Institute for Middle East Policy, said the commission had generally “done a poor job of leveraging the funds of European taxpayers to support genuine stabilisation” on the other side of the Mediterranean. “The strategic interest of Europe is seeing a more economically stable Egypt with a population that is less precarious,” he said.

Sandra Gómez López, a Spanish socialist MEP who recently visited Cairo to assess human rights in Egypt, said the data presented by the Guardian raised important questions. “The commission must explain transparently why it considers these commitments to have been fulfilled and demonstrate that European funding is genuinely contributing to improving social protection for the Egyptian population,” she said.

She stressed that the EU should not withdraw support, “but neither can we allow the country’s strategic importance to lead us to lower our standards when it comes to human rights”.

For critics, the questions about the EU’s willingness to hold Egypt accountable on very precise socioeconomic targets do not bode well for the broader but less defined goals for democracy and human rights.

Strik said: “The fact that Egypt and the commission even seem to ignore the crystal-clear socioeconomic conditions doesn’t increase my expectation that they adhere to the vague precondition on democracy and the rule of law.”

Claudio Francavilla, an associate EU director at Human Rights Watch, said: “Without benchmarks, the EU has turned a human rights conditionality into a blank cheque for Egypt’s authoritarianism.”

In a statement to the Guardian, the European Commission said the policy target on Takaful and Karama referred to new beneficiary families “and not to its net increase”. The spokesperson said there was “no contradiction” between this statement and that in a confidential document that said Egypt was required to “further expand cash transfer programmes”.

Asked about the release of the remaining €1.5bn in promised loans, the commission said it “will be made once all conditions have been met”, referring to 24 economic policy conditions, keeping an IMF programme on track and “taking concrete and credible steps towards respecting effective democratic mechanisms, including a multiparty parliamentary system, and the rule of law, and guaranteeing respect for human rights”.

It added: “Compliance with the conditionality is examined thoroughly” by EU officials and diplomats based on “information provided by the Egyptian authorities and other relevant sources”.

Egypt’s mission to the EU in Brussels did not respond to a request for comment.

#12. Russia targets Kyiv crossings days after Ukraine hits Crimean Bridge

Warehouses, railway stations, energy plants and residential buildings were hit during a night of heavy Russian strikes

Russia targeted bridges in Kyiv and railway stations overnight in a major attack launched days after Ukraine badly damaged the strategically important Crimean Bridge.

Ballistic missiles hit key crossings in the capital during a nationwide assault which Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky said was "one of the most massive strikes" of the war so far and left at least nine dead.

Russian President Vladimir Putin had threatened to retaliate for the attack on the Crimean Bridge, which the Kremlin built to link Russia and the occupied Crimean peninsula.

The latest Russian strikes coincided with talks around the war, and came as the Kremlin pushed back against a US claim that Russia could be open to Western-backed security guarantees for Ukraine.

The overnight attack against Ukraine was spread across several cities and regions, Zelensky said, with residential buildings and energy facilities also hit.

Air raid sirens lasted non-stop throughout the night and were ongoing into the morning.

Zelensky said over 110 missiles and more than 400 attack drones were launched by Russia, adding that "particularly insidious" tactics to launch the waves in 20 to 30-minute internals made it "more difficult to extinguish fires, clear debris, and restore services".

The three bridges targeted in Kyiv - the Podilsko-Voskresensky Bridge, the Darnytskyi Bridge and the Southern Bridge - were closed to traffic.

Kyiv Mayor Vitali Klitschko urged people to "avoid travelling into the city by private car unless absolutely necessary", adding to the disruption around the capital following a first round of strikes against vital crossings earlier this week.

At least seven people were killed and 17 more were injured, Klitschko added, with unspecified civilian facilities also hit.

Ukraine's major operation to take out the road section of the Crimean Bridge earlier this week was a tactical and symbolic blow for Moscow.

Since illegally annexing Crimea in 2014, Moscow has housed troops and military equipment there, with occupying forces travelling through the peninsula towards the front line.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky said an operation to weaken the bridge's defences and ultimately cause part of it to collapse had been weeks in the making.

Russian President Vladimir Putin - who personally opened the bridge in 2019 to international condemnation - said the Kremlin's retaliation for the strike would be "far more painful".

Of the nine fatalities confirmed so far, seven were in Kyiv and two were in Lviv, with at least 28 people injured.

The Sknyliv railway station in Lviv, a city in western Ukraine which has become one of the primary entry points to Ukraine for people and international aid, was badly damaged, the city's Mayor Andriy Sadovyi said.

At least two people were killed in a strike on a railway station in western Ukraine

The attack began hours after European leaders including UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham, French President Emmanuel Macron and German Chancellor Friedrich Merz said they were ready for negotiations involving Moscow to end the war in Ukraine.

The Berlin meeting of the so-called coalition of the willing, an international alliance of Ukraine's allies committed to providing security support in the event of a peace deal, was attended by Zelensky and US President Donald Trump's envoys.

Jared Kushner said the US "wants to make a deal with Europe by his side" after fears of an agreement negotiated between the US and Russia were reignited by Trump's one-to-one talks with Putin earlier this week.

He claimed Russia was "comfortable" with Ukraine having security guarantees based on Nato's Article Five, which requires allies to come to a country's defence if it comes under attack.

On Monday, Russia's deputy foreign minister Sergei Ryabkov pushed back against that, saying "quasi-Nato" membership for Ukraine would be "unacceptable" to Russia. The Kremlin has long cited the prospect of Ukraine joining Nato as a key justification for its war effort.

The US president dialled into the Berlin talks from Air Force One on his way back from China, where he met his Russian counterpart and Chinese leader Xi Jinping.

He reportedly questioned the decision of European leaders to commit â¬70bn (Â£58.6bn; $76.bn) in military funding for Ukraine.

The UK and France are jointly leading the first military exercise organised by the coalition of the willing, which involves thousands of troops from 11 countries and is taking place in Poland.

On Friday, Poland's Prime Minister Donald Tusk accused Russia of an "intentional provocation" after a Russian cruise missile landed in his country.

It struck around 50km (31 miles) from its border with Ukraine, the Polish Defence Minister Wladyslaw Kosiniak-Kamysz said on Friday.

Two more unidentified projectiles, suspected to be a drone and a second missile, landed in villages nearby and no one was harmed, he added.

Polish and Swedish jets were scrambled on Nato's eastern flank as Russia's attack on western Ukraine continued. They have since been stood down.

France's foreign minister Jean-NoÃ«l Barro has called for a meeting of the UN Security Council following Russia's attack on Kyiv.

He described the strikes, which reportedly damaged the Austrian and Dutch embassies, as "crimes" that "Russia will have to answer for".
